abstract_pt: "A Computação Evolucionária tem sido aplicada em domínios visuais, tais\
  \ como o design gráfico e a arte, de forma a possibilitar a exploração computacional\
  \ das possibilidades de design de uma forma mais abrangente. No entanto, na maioria\
  \ das abordagens de design evolucionário, o cálculo da aptidão dos artefactos evoluídos\
  \ é um grande desafio devido à dificuldade em avaliar automaticamente a qualidade\
  \ de artefatos de design visuais. Desta forma, grande parte das abordagens existentes\
  \ requerem, por parte do utilizador, a avaliação manual das soluções criadas ao\
  \ longo do processo evolucionário. Este processo causa fadiga o que, por sua vez,\
  \ compromete a evolução de artefatos com valor para o utilizador.\r\n\r\nNesta tese,\
  \ investiga-se o uso da Computação Evolucionária com atribuição automática de aptidão\
  \ para gerar artefatos de design visuais. Mais concretamente, focamos em três desafios,\
  \ cada um deles numa área distinta: design de tipos de letra, criação de faces humanas\
  \ e renderização não-fotorrealista. Para responder a estes desafios, criámos um\
  \ conjunto de abordagens evolucionárias capazes de gerar automaticamente designs\
  \ de glifos, imagens de faces e espécies de formigas artificiais que criam renderizações\
  \ não-fotorrealista.\r\n\r\nExploram-se diferentes abordagens de atribuição automática\
  \ de aptidão. Para a evolução de glifos, a atribuição automática é feita através\
  \ da combinação de técnicas de Aprendizagem Computacional, semelhança visual com\
  \ glifos alvo, algoritmos de procura de novidade e características visuais extraídas\
  \ dos glifos evoluídos. Para a evolução de imagens de faces, a atribuição de aptidão\
  \ é conseguida através do uso de detetores computacionais de faces. Por último,\
  \ para a evolução de espécies de formigas artificiais, a atribuição de fitness é\
  \ realizada através de um conjunto de estatísticas comportamentais calculadas durante\
  \ a simulação das formigas e de um conjunto de características de imagem extraídas\
  \ das renderizações resultantes. Adicionalmente, exploramos um conjunto de abordagens\
  \ que permitem ao utilizador controlar, através de uma interface gráfica, as propriedades\
  \ das funções responsáveis pelo cálculo da aptidão e, desta forma, expressar diferentes\
  \ objectivos e preferências que devem ser satisfeitas pelo algoritmo evolucionário.\r\
  \n\r\nOs resultados experimentais destas abordagens demonstram a sua eficácia na\
  \ evolução automática de: (i) designs de glifos com legibilidade e características\
  \ gráficas que correspondem às preferências do utilizador; (ii) imagens de faces\
  \ fotorrealistas que induzem detetores de faces em erro, que podem ser usadas para\
  \ melhorar o desempenho destes mesmos detetores, e que levam as pessoas a entender\
  \ estas faces como verdadeiras; e (iii) formigas artificiais que criam pinturas\
  \ visualmente apelativas e complexas, com características estéticas que satisfazem\
  \ as preferências indicadas pelos utilizadores. De forma geral, o presente trabalho\
  \ demonstra a viabilidade de usar Computação Evolucionária com atribuição automática\
  \ de aptidão para desenvolver geradores autónomos capazes de criar rapidamente uma\
  \ grande variedade de artefactos visuais em diferentes domínios visuais."
abstract_en: "Evolutionary Computation has been applied in visual domains, such as\
  \ graphic design and visual art, to enable the computational exploration of vast\
  \ design spaces. However, the process of assigning fitness has proven to be a major\
  \ challenge in most evolutionary design approaches due to the difficulty in automatically\
  \ evaluating the quality of a visual artefact. As a result, many approaches end\
  \ up relying on users to manually assess the candidate solutions generated during\
  \ the evolutionary process. This often leads to user fatigue and, consequently,\
  \ compromises the evolution of valuable artefacts for the user.\r\n\r\nIn this thesis,\
  \ we investigate the use of Evolutionary Computation with automated fitness assignment\
  \ to generate visual design artefacts. We focus on three challenges, each one in\
  \ a different area: type design, human face generation, and non-photorealistic rendering.\
  \ We address these challenges by creating evolutionary approaches capable of automatically\
  \ generating glyph designs, face images, and artificial ant species that paint non-photorealistic\
  \ renderings.\r\n\r\nDifferent approaches are explored to automatically assign fitness\
  \ and thus guide the evolutionary process. Firstly, for the evolution of glyphs,\
  \ automated fitness assignment is accomplished using a combination of Machine Learning\
  \ techniques, visual similarity to target designs, novelty search algorithms, and\
  \ visual features extracted from the evolved designs. Secondly, for the evolution\
  \ of face images, fitness assignment is achieved using pre-trained face detectors.\
  \ Finally, for the evolution of artificial painting ant species, fitness assignment\
  \ is performed using behavioural statistics calculated during the simulation of\
  \ the ants and image features extracted from the resulting renderings. On top of\
  \ this, we explore approaches that allow users to control, through a custom graphical\
  \ interface, the features that build the fitness functions and, this way, express\
  \ different goals and preferences that should be sought by the evolutionary process.\r\
  \n\r\nThe experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the presented approaches\
  \ in automatically evolving (i) glyph designs with high legibility and graphical\
  \ characteristics that match the user preferences; (ii) photorealistic face images\
  \ that may fool face detectors, improve their performance, and also deceive humans\
  \ into thinking they are real; and (iii) artificial ants that paint visually appealing\
  \ and complex artworks with varied aesthetic characteristics that satisfy the preferences\
  \ expressed by the users. Overall, this work demonstrates the feasibility of using\
  \ Evolutionary Computation with automated fitness assignment to create autonomous\
  \ generators capable of quickly creating a wide variety of design artefacts in different\
  \ visual domains."

Tiago Filipe dos Santos Martins

A B S T R A C T
Evolutionary Computation has been applied in visual domains, such
as graphic design and visual art, to enable the computational explo-
ration of vast design spaces. However, the process of assigning ﬁtness
has proven to be a major challenge in most evolutionary design ap-
proaches due to the difﬁculty in automatically evaluating the quality
of a visual artefact. As a result, many approaches end up relying on
users to manually assess the candidate solutions generated during
the evolutionary process. This often leads to user fatigue and, con-
sequently, compromises the evolution of valuable artefacts for the
user.
In this thesis, we investigate the use of Evolutionary Computation
with automated ﬁtness assignment to generate visual design artefacts.
We focus on three challenges, each one in a different area: type design,
human face generation, and non-photorealistic rendering. We address
these challenges by creating evolutionary approaches capable of au-
tomatically generating glyph designs, face images, and artiﬁcial ant
species that paint non-photorealistic renderings.
Different approaches are explored to automatically assign ﬁtness
and thus guide the evolutionary process. Firstly, for the evolution of
glyphs, automated ﬁtness assignment is accomplished using a com-
bination of Machine Learning techniques, visual similarity to target
designs, novelty search algorithms, and visual features extracted from
the evolved designs. Secondly, for the evolution of face images, ﬁtness
assignment is achieved using pre-trained face detectors. Finally, for
the evolution of artiﬁcial painting ant species, ﬁtness assignment is
performed using behavioural statistics calculated during the simu-
lation of the ants and image features extracted from the resulting
renderings. On top of this, we explore approaches that allow users
to control, through a custom graphical interface, the features that
build the ﬁtness functions and, this way, express different goals and
preferences that should be sought by the evolutionary process.
The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the pre-
sented approaches in automatically evolving (i) glyph designs with
high legibility and graphical characteristics that match the user pref-
erences; (ii) photorealistic face images that may fool face detectors,
improve their performance, and also deceive humans into thinking
they are real; and (iii) artiﬁcial ants that paint visually appealing and
complex artworks with varied aesthetic characteristics that satisfy the
xi

preferences expressed by the users. Overall, this work demonstrates
the feasibility of using Evolutionary Computation with automated
ﬁtness assignment to create autonomous generators capable of quickly
creating a wide variety of design artefacts in different visual domains.
K E Y W O R D S
Automated Fitness Assignment, Computational Creativity, Evolution-
ary Design, Evolutionary Computation, Generative Design, Machine
Learning

R E S U M O
A Computação Evolucionária tem sido aplicada em domínios visuais,
tais como o design gráﬁco e a arte, de forma a possibilitar a explo-
ração computacional das possibilidades de design de uma forma mais
abrangente. No entanto, na maioria das abordagens de design evolu-
cionário, o cálculo da aptidão dos artefactos evoluídos é um grande
desaﬁo devido à diﬁculdade em avaliar automaticamente a qualidade
de artefatos de design visuais. Desta forma, grande parte das aborda-
gens existentes requerem, por parte do utilizador, a avaliação manual
das soluções criadas ao longo do processo evolucionário. Este processo
causa fadiga o que, por sua vez, compromete a evolução de artefatos
com valor para o utilizador.
Nesta tese, investiga-se o uso da Computação Evolucionária com
atribuição automática de aptidão para gerar artefatos de design vi-
suais. Mais concretamente, focamos em três desaﬁos, cada um deles
numa área distinta: design de tipos de letra, criação de faces humanas
e renderização não-fotorrealista. Para responder a estes desaﬁos, criá-
mos um conjunto de abordagens evolucionárias capazes de gerar
automaticamente designs de glifos, imagens de faces e espécies de
formigas artiﬁciais que criam renderizações não-fotorrealista.
Exploram-se diferentes abordagens de atribuição automática de
aptidão. Para a evolução de glifos, a atribuição automática é feita
através da combinação de técnicas de Aprendizagem Computacional,
semelhança visual com glifos alvo, algoritmos de procura de novidade
e características visuais extraídas dos glifos evoluídos. Para a evolução
de imagens de faces, a atribuição de aptidão é conseguida através do
uso de detetores computacionais de faces. Por último, para a evolução
de espécies de formigas artiﬁciais, a atribuição de ﬁtness é realizada
através de um conjunto de estatísticas comportamentais calculadas
durante a simulação das formigas e de um conjunto de características
de imagem extraídas das renderizações resultantes. Adicionalmente,
exploramos um conjunto de abordagens que permitem ao utilizador
controlar, através de uma interface gráﬁca, as propriedades das funções
responsáveis pelo cálculo da aptidão e, desta forma, expressar difer-
entes objectivos e preferências que devem ser satisfeitas pelo algoritmo
evolucionário.
Os resultados experimentais destas abordagens demonstram a sua
eﬁcácia na evolução automática de: (i) designs de glifos com legibili-
dade e características gráﬁcas que correspondem às preferências do
xiii

utilizador; (ii) imagens de faces fotorrealistas que induzem detetores
de faces em erro, que podem ser usadas para melhorar o desem-
penho destes mesmos detetores, e que levam as pessoas a entender
estas faces como verdadeiras; e (iii) formigas artiﬁciais que criam
pinturas visualmente apelativas e complexas, com características es-
téticas que satisfazem as preferências indicadas pelos utilizadores.
De forma geral, o presente trabalho demonstra a viabilidade de usar
Computação Evolucionária com atribuição automática de aptidão para
desenvolver geradores autónomos capazes de criar rapidamente uma
grande variedade de artefactos visuais em diferentes domínios visuais.

Part I
P R E A M B L E

1
I N T R O D U C T I O N
The ability of Nature to create a myriad of designs that are simulta-
neously novel and high performing is remarkable. Natural species
exhibit a wide variety of forms that are adapted to the requirements
imposed by their environment. The way designs emerge in Nature
can be explained by evolution, which was ﬁrst described by Charles
Darwin in his book On the Origin of Species [54]. According to Darwin,
species gradually evolve and adapt to their environments over many
generations in a process that became known as natural selection, or
“survival of the ﬁttest”.
Since the late 1950s, the ﬁeld of computer science has been devel-
oping algorithms inspired by the Darwinian principles of biological
evolution. These algorithms imitate mechanisms of natural evolution
such as reproduction, variation, and selection to gradually improve
a population of candidate solutions for a given problem. These al-
gorithms are referred to as Evolutionary Algorithms ( EAs) and are
the basis of Evolutionary Computation ( EC), a subﬁeld of Artiﬁcial
Intelligence (AI). Today,EC is applied in many ﬁelds to tackle search
and optimisation problems that are difﬁcult to solve using traditional
techniques. In addition to science and engineering, EC has found a
wide range of applications in other ﬁelds such as design and art.
The application of EC in design problems consists in seeing the de-
sign process as an optimisation problem and ﬁnding high performing
solutions that satisfy requirements. However, ﬁtness assignment is a
major challenge in many evolutionary design approaches. Computa-
tionally measuring the quality of a design tends to be difﬁcult since,
in this context, it is generally hard to express computationally human
judgements [25]. Therefore, deﬁning an explicit ﬁtness function that
captures human preferences may be, in practice, unfeasible.
Problems in which it is hard to come up with a computational
ﬁtness function are often addressed using Interactive Evolutionary
Computation (IEC) (see, e.g., [273]). IEC approaches rely on repeated
user evaluation during the evolutionary process. However, the evolu-
tionary process usually needs a large number of ﬁtness evaluations
until a satisfying result can be obtained. This can put a signiﬁcant
burden on the user, leading to user fatigue, evaluation inconsistency,
and the valorisation of novelty over quality. The large number of
evaluations required from the user also limits the population size
3

4 introduction
and the duration of the evolutionary runs. Therefore, due to these
practical limitations, and due to theoretical questions that arise from
the automation of ﬁtness assignment in this type of domain, where
aesthetical, functional and cultural criteria may inﬂuence ﬁtness, make
it an open AI problem.
1.1 motivation
Nowadays, a new set of professionals with a background in Generative
Design (GD) and who work in the intersection between graphic design
and computer science begin to emerge. As a result, they often design by
writing customised computer programs. For instance, when existing
design software tools provide insufﬁcient support to the execution of
an idea or any design task, they may create custom tools tailored for
that purpose. We will name these tools “author tools”. Creating custom
tools allows them to go beyond the options and metaphors offered
by off-the-shelf tools. This allows the creation of unique designs that
would have been impossible or unfeasible to produce using existing
tools.
Most of the author tools created by these coders consist of GD
systems that operate like design generators (see Section 2.1). These
generators automatically create multiple alternative designs from a de-
sign concept. In addition, these generators allow the quick generation
of design alternatives that perform well in their intended function.
The motivation behind this work comes from the research on
biologically-inspired processes in the autonomous generation and
selection of design alternatives. We consider that AI techniques, such
as EC (see Section 2.2) and Machine Learning ( ML) (see Section 2.3),
can play a role in design exploration towards new valuable and unex-
pected designs.
1.2 research statement
The research hypothesis is that Evolutionary Computation and auto-
mated ﬁtness assignment can be used to create valuable autonomous
generators of visual design artefacts. In the context of this thesis, a
visual design artefact is seen as an artefact whose value depends on
functional and aesthetical criteria. Considering this research hypothe-
sis, the following research questions arise:
a. Can we automate the evolution of visual design artefacts in
different domains? — Evolution should improve the functional

1.2 research statement 5
and aesthetical value of the artefacts being evolved, both in terms
of ﬁtness and of real value. For the evolutionary process to be
autonomous, the ﬁtness of the candidate designs needs to be
assigned automatically. Although automated ﬁtness assignment
has already been investigated (see, e.g., [11]), we question how to
develop autonomous design generators that promote usefulness
and value. We also question which techniques are adequate to
automate ﬁtness assignment.
b. How can we allow users to express goals and preferences? —
We question how the evolutionary process can be automated
and yet allow users to inﬂuence the results in a meaningful way.
Users should be able to express preferences without repeated
manual evaluations, as in IEC approaches. Therefore, we inquire
what information from the users is necessary to obtain outputs
consistent with their preferences.
c. Which limitations may arise from automated ﬁtness assign-
ment? — These limitations can be related to several factors,
including: the ability of the automated ﬁtness functions to cap-
ture designs’ quality; and the ﬂexibility of automated evolution-
ary design generators to create valuable visual design artefacts
for different domains.
To answer the research questions mentioned above, we attend to the
following objectives:
a. Study the state of the art in the automated generation of visual
design artefacts;
b. Identify a set of design domains to apply automated evolution;
c. Conceive and develop evolutionary approaches for the genera-
tion of visual design artefacts;
d. Design adequate ﬁtness functions for the evolution of visual
design artefacts;
e. Propose and develop tailor-made design tools, i.e. author tools,
to address speciﬁc design challenges and explore new creative
processes;
f. Provide methods to express personal preferences and goals
through ﬁtness functions;
g. Apply the developed approaches in real use cases.

6 introduction
The scope of this thesis is narrowed to the autonomous evolution of
two-dimensional visual design artefacts. We identiﬁed and focused on
three challenges in three different domains: type design (see Part ii),
human face image generation (see Part iii), and non-photorealistic
rendering (see Part iv). For these three challenges, we proposed evo-
lutionary generators to evolve visual design artefacts, while using
Artiﬁcial Neural Networks ( ANNs) and hardwired ﬁtness functions to
assign ﬁtness based on the speciﬁc requirements of each challenge and
the preferences of the users. The evolution of other types of artefacts
is out of the scope of this thesis. Nevertheless, we consider that our
contributions, namely the key principles, may be applied to other do-
mains. Furthermore, questions related to the user experience provided
by the developed systems are out of the scope of this thesis.
1.3 contributions
This thesis presents novel contributions to the domains of GD, EC and
ML. Next, we highlight the key contributions following an order that
is, in general, aligned with the structure of this thesis.
literature review We review the state of the art on the topics of
generative design (see Section 3.2), evolutionary visual art and design
(see Section 3.2), and automated ﬁtness assignment in evolutionary
visual art and design (see Section 3.3). We also analyse related work
on the three topics of our practical research: evolutionary type design
(see Section 4.2), automatic generation of photorealistic human face
images (see Section 9.2), and computational visual art inspired by ant
colony approaches (see Section 12.2).
autonomous generators of type designs We develop dif-
ferent evolutionary approaches for the automatic generation of glyph
designs, or letterforms. These approaches form collectively a work
titled Evotype (after evolutionary type) and can quickly provide alter-
native glyph designs from scratch. Each approach explores a way of
designing and evaluating glyphs. We explore the design of glyphs
by combining line segments (see Chapter 5), combining shapes (see
Chapter 6), and creating stencils composed of line segments that allow
the design of several glyphs (see Chapters 7 and 8). Each glyph is
automatically evaluated according to features such as legibility and
recognisability. In addition to these features, we allow the users to
specify other desired visual characteristics that they want to see in

1.3 contributions 7
the evolved glyphs (see Chapter 8). The different iterations of Evotype
resulted in several publications [ 189–191, 193].
autonomous generators of face images We develop evo-
lutionary approaches to the autonomous generation of new photore-
alistic human face images (see Chapters 10 and 11). Each new face
image is created by recombining, or blending, the elementary parts
(eyebrows, eyes, nose and mouth) of different existing face images.
The developed system, titled X-faces (after eXploit faces), enables the
generation of convincing face images that can fool computers, de-
ceive humans, and exploit the differences between computer vision
and human vision. Therefore, the generated face images can unlock
scientiﬁc and artistic applications. We can use X-faces to exploit the
vulnerabilities of face detectors by generating false negatives, i.e. im-
ages containing faces that are not detected as such. This approach
can be used to create new face instances that may enrich the training
dataset and improve the performance of the resulting detector. The
two developed iterations of X-faces resulted in two publications [ 47,
49]. These technical contributions to the domain of ML are the basis
for the development of the interactive media art installation that is
summarised in the following paragraph.
interactive media art installation We create an interac-
tive media art installation titled Portraits of No One , which is based
on the work X-faces (see Section 11.3). Through this installation, we
enable the public to interact with the generative system of X-faces. The
installation captures the faces of everyone who interacts with it and
generates new faces which are different from the captured ones. The
boundaries between natural and artiﬁcial, as well as the concept of
identity, is explored by the realism of the generated faces that make
people wonder about the authenticity of what they are seeing. This
installation was exhibited at the National Museum of Contemporary
Art – Museu do Chiado 1, in Lisbon, Portugal, as part of the Sonae
Media Art Award 2019 exhibition, from November 2019 to Febru-
ary 2020. Later, a new version of the installation Portraits of No One
was exhibited at Círculo de Artes Plásticas de Coimbra 2, Portugal,
from April 2021 to June 2021. The development of Portraits of No One
resulted in three publications [ 50, 194, 195].
1 http://www.museuartecontemporanea.gov.pt
2 http://capc.com.pt

8 introduction
autonomous generator of artificial painting ants We
automate the evolution of artiﬁcial painting ant species that pro-
duce Non-Photorealistic Renderings ( NPRs) of input images consis-
tent with the aesthetic goals and preferences speciﬁed by the user
(see Chapter 13). These ants are operated by the evolutionary sys-
tem Photogrowth. Inspired by swarm intelligence and ant colony ap-
proaches, this system produces emerging paintings by simulating
artiﬁcial ants that travel across an input image, feed on it, and leave
their trails on a canvas, like paintbrushes. An early version of this
system [ 179] used IEC to allow users to evolve the parameters that
control the behaviour of the ant species. A later version [171] employed
hardwired ﬁtness functions to automate the evolutionary process. In
the scope of this thesis, we take a further step in enabling the user
to design ﬁtness functions in a more intuitive way and thus auto-
matically evolve ant species with characteristics consistent with their
preferences. The development of automated ﬁtness assignment based
on the interactive design of ﬁtness functions, in Photogrowth, resulted
in two publications [183, 185].
computer graphics algorithm We develop an algorithm to
enable the rendering of the artiﬁcial ant trails of Photogrowth with
continuous organic lines that vary in width and allow self-intersection
with transparency (see Subsection 13.1.5). During its simulation, each
ant trail is encoded as a series of circles representing the position and
size of an ant. The novel algorithm transforms these circles into one
continuous line depicting the organic motion of the ants along with
the intertwinement and superimposition of their trails. The implemen-
tation of this algorithm allows us to achieve the visual effect that we
envisioned for the ant trails and to produce detailed and expressive
artworks. The algorithm allows the speciﬁcation of some rendering
options, e.g. ﬁll colour, ﬁll opacity, outline colour, outline opacity and
outline width. Also, the algorithm outputs vector images, which allow
us to create resolution-independent paintings that can be printed as
large-format artworks and inspected at high zoom levels.
online art system We create a system titled Insta.ants to allow
Instagram users to experiment online with Photogrowth and have their
images painted by the artiﬁcial ants (see Section 13.3). Users only need
to use the hashtag #instaants in the caption of their Instagram posts.
The system will ﬁnd the Instagram images containing this hashtag,
render each image with an ant species selected at random, and upload
the resulting paintings to their Instagram proﬁle with a tag to the

1.4 roadmap 9
users who posted the original images. The proﬁle of the artiﬁcial paint-
ing ants can be found at https://www.instagram.com/insta.ants/.
Insta.ants has been featured in exhibitions such as xCoAx, in 2017
(Lisbon, Portugal), Romaria Cultural, in 2017 (Gouveia, Portugal), and
Growing Forms: Natural Born Mathematics, in 2018 (Coimbra, Portugal).
automated fitness assignment We develop automated ﬁt-
ness assignment approaches to guide the evolutionary process, with
no user in the loop, for each area addressed in this thesis. For the
evolution of glyphs (see Part ii), we automate ﬁtness assignment using
a combination of ML techniques, visual similarity to target designs,
novelty search algorithms, and visual features extracted from the re-
sulting designs. For the evolution of human face images (see Part iii),
we automate ﬁtness using the conﬁdence level given by a pre-trained
face detector. For the evolution of artiﬁcial painting ant species (see
Part iv), we automate ﬁtness using behavioural statistics, calculated
during the simulation of the ants, as well as image features, extracted
from the resulting ant paintings.
fitness function design interfaces We design and imple-
ment custom interfaces to allow users to express their goals and
preferences through the interactive design of ﬁtness functions, which
are used to automatically guide the evolutionary process. The idea
is to take users out of the evolutionary loop and allow them to con-
trol the evolutionary process at a higher level. This is achieved by
designing ﬁtness functions that ideally guide evolution towards re-
sults that are consistent with the goals and preferences of the users,
without the need to perform individual assessments. We present the
interfaces for ﬁtness function design in the scope of evolutionary
type design (see Subsection 8.1.3) and evolutionary ant paintings (see
Subsection 13.1.4).
1.4 roadmap
The development of this thesis follows a bottom-up methodology.
First, to investigate and test our research hypothesis, we identify a
set of challenges, each in a different area and with speciﬁc charac-
teristics and requirements. We focus on the areas of (a) type design
(Part ii), (b) human face generation (Part iii), and (c) non-photorealistic
rendering (Part iv). Next, we develop evolutionary approaches with
automated ﬁtness assignment for each of these areas. This strategy
provides us with the opportunity to investigate the autonomous evo-

10 introduction
lution of form, function, and the combination of both. The proposed
approaches build the experimental core of this thesis and determine
the structure of this document.
Part i contains three Chapters, including the present Chapter 1. In
Chapter 2, we provide background concepts needed to frame this
thesis, which includes works in the areas of GD, EC, and ML. Then, in
Chapter 3, we review the state of the art on the core topics of this
thesis: GD, evolutionary visual art and design, and automated ﬁtness
assignment in evolutionary visual art and design.
Part ii presents our investigation in type design. In Chapter 4, we
introduce the topic of evolutionary type design and analyse related
work. Then, in Chapters 5, 6, 7 and 8, we present four approaches for
the autonomous evolution of type designs, each employing different
evolutionary and/or evaluation processes. For each approach, we
explain the framework, describe the experimentation, and discuss the
results obtained. Finally, we present the application of evolved type
designs in the creation of typographic portraits.
Part iii relates to our investigation in human face generation. In
Chapter 9, we introduce the area of automatic generation of photore-
alistic human face images and analyse related work. In Chapters 10
and 11, we present two approaches to the autonomous evolution of
face images. We explain, for each approach, the proposed framework,
describe the experimentation, and discuss the experimental results.
Then, we present an application that involves the creation of an inter-
active media art installation.
Part iv describes our investigation in non-photorealistic rendering.
In Chapter 12, we introduce ant colony inspired visual art and analyse
related work. Then, in Chapter 13, we present our approach to the
autonomous evolution of artiﬁcial painting ant species. We explain the
proposed framework, describe the experimentation, present the use
of evolved ant species by a web-based agent, and discuss the results
obtained.
Part v comprises Chapter 14, where we synthesise the work done in
the scope of this thesis, presenting the overall conclusions and answers
to our research questions, highlighting the main contributions and
achievements, and indicating future work.

2
B A C K G R O U N D
This Chapter introduces concepts needed to frame this thesis. Al-
though most information presented in this Chapter can be found in
the literature, we consider it important to gather and provide the
reader with some background before moving on to the experimental
parts of this thesis. We begin by introducing GD in Section 2.1. Then,
we overview the basics of EC in Section 2.2, with focus on Genetic
Algorithms (GAs). Finally, we provide a brief introduction to ML in
Section 2.3.
2.1 generative design
GD is a way of designing. In GD, the designer shifts his/her focus from
the creation of individual designs to the development of a system that
can generate numerous designs.
We consider there is a certain analogy between GD and nature, more
speciﬁcally genetics, that may help to explain GD. This analogy is
described as follows: the inputs of a GD system represent the genotype;
the output represents a phenotype; the mapping process that describes
how inputs drive the design of outputs can be seen as themorphogenesis
process; and ﬁnally, the design concept that originated the system
represents the species. By changing the GD system inputs (genotype),
designers can obtain a wide range of designs (phenotypes) for a
particular design concept (species). These genetic concepts are further
described in Section 2.2.
The roots of GD can be traced back to several centuries ago to manual
art forms that used rule-based approaches (see Section 3.2). These
early GD approaches, developed long before the arrival of the digital
computer, show that the application of generative logic in different
art forms is not new, and that it does not necessarily imply computer
programs or programming code, as it is often assumed.
Regardless of how it is executed, manually or automatically, the
goal of GD is the creation of an algorithm to produce multiple designs
that instantiate a concept. Algorithms can be executed by people
or machines, certainly with different levels of ease, accuracy, and
speed. Take for example the series of artworks Wall Drawings by the
conceptual artist Sol LeWitt, started in 1970 (see Section 3.1). In each
11

12 background
Wall Drawing, LeWitt encoded the concept of the artwork into a series
of instructions written in one page. These instructions were intended
to be carried out by draftsmen. By detaching the concept (conceived
by the artist) from the execution (produced by the draftsmen), LeWitt
was able to model the design of numerous artworks based on the same
initial concept.
In the computational sphere, creating a GD system involves the
development of a computer system. Algorithm 1 idealises the main
steps of a computational GD process.
Algorithm 1 Idealisation of a Generative Design process
1: designer comes up with a design concept
2: designer translates concept into an algorithm
3: designer implements the algorithm by creating a computer system
4: while designer not satisﬁed do
5: designer modiﬁes inputs and/or system
6: computer runs system
7: designer judges output
8: designer curates and uses selected output(s)
One of the most notable beneﬁts of computational GD approaches
is the automatic creation of design alternatives, or variations, for a
particular design concept. The execution of a GD system with some
level of randomness, e.g. through random inputs or inner variables,
can provide unpredictability to the design process and to the resulting
outputs. This provides the designer with valuable artefacts that can
promote their creativity.
The adoption of GD has impacted how designers conceive and pro-
duce forms. It gives designers new creative possibilities and pushes
design processes into new directions. For instance, in the design of
dynamic visual identities (see, e.g., [ 192]), GD has allowed the cre-
ation of logotypes and symbols that react to external data and “logo
generators” that create a continuous feed of designs.
GD also poses challenges to the designer. In some cases, GD systems
can have several inputs, making it unfeasible for the designer to test
all combinations of inputs in an acceptable time. In addition, GD
systems with complex input-output mapping processes tend to make
the designer tweak inputs without realising their full implications
on the outputs. As a result, the designer may have to test different
combinations of inputs, in a trial-and-error process, until satisfying
outputs are obtained.
Finding combinations of inputs to a GD system that result in ad-
equate outputs can be addressed using EC. Considering the task of
ﬁnding inputs as a search problem, EC can be used to ﬁnd favourable

2.2 evolutionary computation 13
combinations of inputs and in this way help the designer exploring
the design space in a more practical and effective way. This approach
can also help designers discover designs that, possibly, would never
have occurred to them. The following Section introduces EC.
2.2 evolutionary computation
EC is a subﬁeld of AI and involves a family of algorithms inspired
by mechanisms of biological evolution, such as the Darwinian nat-
ural selection [ 54] and Mendelian genetics [ 202]. These algorithms,
named EAs, consist in stochastic search heuristics that are typically
implemented on computer systems and applied to a variety of search
and optimisation problems that are difﬁcult to solve using traditional
techniques. An EA can be understood as a search process that aims
to ﬁnd solutions to a given problem by maintaining a population of
possible solutions that are gradually changed and selected according
to principles of natural evolution.
Different EAs exist. The most common ones include GAs [68, 122,
123], Evolutionary Programming (EP) [77], Evolution Strategies (ESs)
[240, 260], and Genetic Programming (GP) [13, 142]. In the scope of this
thesis, we use GAs. Therefore, the remainder of this Section focuses on
the behaviour of GAs. One may ﬁnd information about the different
EA families, for example, in the work of Bäck, Hammel, and Schwefel
[9].
A GA evolves solutions to a given problem by performing a multi-
directional search through the space of potential solutions. To do
so, the GA maintains a set of candidate solutions to the problem to
solve. This set of solutions is called population and each candidate
solution contained in it is called individual. The genotype is the ge-
netic code that, once expressed through a mapping function, becomes
a phenotype i.e. individual. Therefore, when the mapping process is
non-deterministic, one genotype may result in several different phe-
notypes, i.e. candidate solutions. Likewise, several genotypes may
result in the same phenotype. Each genotype consists of a sequence of
attributes, called genes, encoding information that will be used during
the genotype-phenotype mapping.
The process of mapping, or growth, from genotype to pheno-
type is called morphogenesis, deriving from the Greek “ morpho-” and
“-genesis”, meaning form and emergence, respectively. Thus, morpho-
genesis consists in the process of growth of form in nature. Morpho-
genesis can be understood as a design model that generates an output
(phenotype) given a set of inputs (genes). Each combination of inputs

14 background
identiﬁes a point in two separate conceptual spaces: (i) a space that
contains every conﬁguration of inputs of the design model, named
search space; and (ii) a space that contains every output that the design
model can generate, named solution space. The goal of the GA is to
navigate the search space and ﬁnd combinations of inputs that are
mapped to outputs in the solution space with high ﬁtness scores.
Depending on the problem to solve, different representations can
be used to encode individuals. Each representation induces a search
space with a dimension determined by the number of genes and the
number of possible values for each gene. This way, the representation
has a large impact on the characteristics of the search space.
Although some mechanisms of the GA are speciﬁc to the problem
to solve, the general behaviour remains the same. Algorithm 2 sum-
marises the main steps of a GA [277].
Algorithm 2 Generic Genetic Algorithm
1: initialise individuals’ genotypes
2: map individuals’ genotypes to phenotypes
3: evaluate individuals
4: while termination condition is not satisﬁed do
5: select individuals for breeding
6: breed new individuals using variation operators
7: form new generation of individuals
8: map new generation individuals’ genotypes to phenotypes
9: evaluate new generation individuals
The GA begins by creating the initial population of individuals.
Different strategies can be used for forming the initial population,
e.g. ﬁll the population with individuals from random genotypes or
individuals previously evolved.
To evolve solutions to a given problem, the ﬁtness of each individual
must be determined or estimated. This evaluation process is also known
as ﬁtness assignment. In this process, a ﬁtness function is applied to a
solution to measure its quality and assign a ﬁtness value to it. In
this context, the quality of a solution means how well it meets the
speciﬁcations valued by the ﬁtness function. The higher the quality of
the solution, the higher the ﬁtness value. The ﬁtness value enables the
comparison of different solutions in terms of quality, i.e. determine
whether one solution is better than another. This, in turn, can show
potential search directions towards solutions with higher quality.
There are several approaches to assign ﬁtness, some more au-
tonomous than others, and choosing an approach depends on the
problem to solve. For instance, in some cases, it is complicated to come
up with a ﬁtness function that can automatically evaluate the individ-

2.2 evolutionary computation 15
uals. In these cases, one of the most commonly used approaches is IEC
(see, e.g., [273]). In IEC, users are asked to evaluate the individuals in
an interactive way. Thus, the subjective evaluation of the user plays
the role of the ﬁtness function. This approach is common, for example,
in the evolution of visual artefacts. However, in this thesis, we focus
on automated ﬁtness assignment (see Section 3.3).
With all the individuals in the population evaluated, a selection
process follows. In this process, individuals are selected as parents for
reproduction to generate offspring, i.e. new individuals. The probability
of each individual to be selected for reproduction is determined by
its ﬁtness value. Individuals with higher ﬁtness values have more
chances to be selected and thus pass their characteristics to the next
generation.
New individuals (offspring) are produced using variation operators.
Two variation operators are typically applied: crossover and mutation.
Crossover operators randomly exchange the genetic information from
two parents to create new offspring. This way, crossovers operators
are usually applied to every two parents selected for reproduction.
Although crossover enables the inheritance of genetic characteristics
and the exploitation of characteristics present in the population by
creating new combinations, it does not add new genetic information.
This is done through mutation operators, which are applied to some
offspring to randomly modify the values of some of their genes. These
operators create genetic variability, promoting diversity within the
population.
The newly created offspring are evaluated and then used to form
a new population, i.e. a new generation, by replacing individuals in
the current population. There are two main approaches: generational
and steady-state. In a generational approach, most of the individuals
of the current population are replaced by the descendants. In steady-
state approaches, new individuals are added to and deleted from
the population incrementally. There are also different replacement
strategies. For instance, one may opt to use an elitist approach, which
implies that a given percentage of the population is preserved from one
generation to the next. This prevents the accidental loss of the ﬁttest
individuals over the course of the evolutionary process. In steady-state
approaches, one may choose to replace the worst individuals, random
individuals, etc.
By repeatedly applying the sequence of steps described above, the
GA is eventually able to gradually derive better and better performing
individuals over the course of the evolutionary process. The evolu-

16 background
tionary process keeps on iterating until a preset condition is satisﬁed,
e.g. when a number of generations has been performed.
All the mechanisms described above impact the search efﬁciency of
an EA. Fitness assignment, in particular, is one of the most important
parts of the evolutionary process, and also one of the most challenging,
particularly in domains such as design or the arts. The design and
implementation of a ﬁtness function that can automatically evaluate
visual artefacts tends to be challenging. We believe ML can play a role
here in automating ﬁtness assignment. For that purpose, the following
Section introduces ML.
2.3 machine learning
ML is a subﬁeld of AI concerned with the creation of computer pro-
grams that automatically improve with experience [ 204: p. xv]. The
term machine learning was coined in 1959 by the computer scientist
Arthur Samuel [ 254], who deﬁned it as «a ﬁeld of study that gives
computers the ability to learn without being explicitly programmed».
Back in 1952, Samuel developed, perhaps, the ﬁrst-ever self-learning
computer program, which learnt to play the game of checkers.
Nowadays, ML brings together statistics and computer science with
the goal of developing algorithms that give to machines the ability to
automatically learn from data, generalise from it, and build models
capable of making classiﬁcations, predictions, or inferences, on new
data.
ML is widely applied, especially in tasks where manually developing
computer programs with good performance is difﬁcult, e.g. predic-
tion of stock market ﬂuctuations, fraud detection, analysis of user
behaviour, social networking, computer vision, and computational
creativity.
There are different types of problems being solved with ML. In
each problem, the ML model plays a different role. For instance, in a
classiﬁcation problem, the model has to predict the class of a given
data example ( e.g. is this a photo of a door or a window?). In a
regression problem, the model has to predict the numerical value
of a given data example ( e.g. what will be the price of this stock in
one minute?). In a clustering problem, the model has to group given
data examples into classes ( e.g. group this set of patients with similar
symptoms).
There are also different ways of training a ML model, such as super-
vised learning, unsupervised learning, and reinforcement learning. In
supervised learning, the machine learns from labelled data, i.e. each

2.3 machine learning 17
data example is labelled, for example, with a class in the case of a
classiﬁcation problem or with a numerical value in the case of a regres-
sion problem. In unsupervised learning, the machine learns from data
that is not labelled, e.g. in a clustering problem the data examples are
not labelled. In reinforcement learning, the machine learns through
a trial-and-error process as it tries to perform a task. Essentially, the
machine learns by repeatedly observing the environment, taking an
action to interact with it, and receiving a positive reward if the action
result was good or a negative reward if the action result was bad.
A data-driven ML approach typically involves the following steps:
gather data, prepare data, choose a model, train the model, evaluate
the model, tune hyperparameters, and, ﬁnally, deploy the model. Each
step is overviewed in the following paragraphs.
Gathering data is a key task in a data-driven ML approach. The
training of a ML model requires a dataset containing several exam-
ples, or samples, from which the model may learn. The quality and
quantity of data examples directly determine how good the model can
be. Although in many ML approaches raw data is used for training,
others reduce the complexity of that data into features, i.e. attributes
that are likely to characterise the examples that the model should
learn. Therefore, in some ML approaches, gathering data also involves
measuring features for the gathered examples and determining or
engineering the features to be measured.
Data preparation follows. In this step, all the gathered data is put
together. In some cases, the order of the data examples is randomised
to ensure the order in which the examples were collected does not
affect the learning process. Also, the preparation of the data often
involves its visualisation at different levels, e.g. visualise any relevant
relationship between different attributes.
Generally, the dataset is divided into three subsets: training, vali-
dation, and test. The training subset is used to train the model and
typically consists of the majority of the dataset. The validation subset is
used to validate the performance of the trained model and determine
when to stop training. The test subset is used to assess the expected
performance in the real world application of the model. Splitting the
dataset into three parts, thus ensuring that the test examples are not
used during training, allows one to evaluate the ability of the trained
model to generalise to unseen examples.
Many different ML models have been created over the years, e.g. sup-
port vector machines, decision trees, and artiﬁcial neural networks.
Choosing a model depends heavily on the problem, and no model
performs optimally in all problems. When choosing a model, one

18 background
should consider, e.g. the characteristics of the data ( e.g. size, type, and
complexity), the problem (e.g. classiﬁcation, regression, or clustering),
and the available resources (e.g. computational power and time).
Conceptually, training a model consists in ﬁnding values, itera-
tively, for its internal parameters that maximise the performance of
the model. This means that the training process occurs over multiple
steps, wherein each step tends to improve the internal parameters
of the model. Typically, each step involves: (i) using the model, with
the current set of internal parameters, on some data examples; (ii)
comparing the outcomes of the model to the expected ones; (iii) calcu-
lating the error; and (iv) using the error to update the internal model
parameters.
Before the training process begins, some parameters need to be
set. These parameters are called hyperparameters and deﬁne high-
level properties of the model being used and of the training process,
e.g. batch size, number of epochs, and learning rate. Using the term
hyperparameter is also a way of distinguishing these high-level pa-
rameters from the internal model parameters that are trained.
Once the training is complete, the model is evaluated to assess
its performance. Evaluation consists of (i) using the model on the
validation dataset, which contains examples that the model has never
seen, and (ii) evaluating the outputs using a set of performance metrics.
Different performance metrics exist to evaluate a model, including
metrics for classiﬁcation problems ( e.g. accuracy, precision, recall,
logarithmic loss, or area under curve) and metrics for regression
(e.g. mean absolute error or mean squared error). Regardless of the
metrics used during evaluation, the purpose of this step is to assess
how the model performs against data that it has never seen.
Trying different hyperparameters is crucial, as the performance of
the model can be highly dependent on them. Tuning hyperparameters
involves trying different values for them, (re)training the model, evalu-
ating it, and keeping the model that performs best. There are no rules
for how to ﬁnd the optimal hyperparameters, so different values must
be tested to see which ones work best. The tuning of hyperparame-
ters can be accomplished manually ( e.g. rules of thumb, values from
other problems, or by trial and error). Nevertheless, this process is
increasingly achieved using automated methods that aim to ﬁnd near
optimal hyperparameters with little manual effort ( e.g. grid search,
random search, Bayesian optimisation, gradient-based optimisation,
or evolutionary optimisation).
With a model trained and performing well, it is time to use it and
apply it to unseen data examples. Suppose we have a generative EC

2.3 machine learning 19
system that can evolve images and an ML model that can classify im-
ages as containing or not containing a human face. If we use the model
output, e.g. the conﬁdence value, to assign ﬁtness to the images being
evolved, we could be able to automatically evolve images that contain
human faces, at least from the perspective of the model. For more
information on this topic, see, for example, the works of Machado,
Correia, and Romero [ 175] and Correia et al. [ 48].

3
S TAT E O F T H E A R T
This Chapter concerns major contributions to the computational gen-
eration of visual artefacts related to the work developed in this thesis.
We organised this Chapter into three Sections related to three nested
subjects, as illustrated in Figure 3.1. First, in Section 3.1, we overview
GD approaches. Then, in Section 3.2, we present applications of EC
in visual art and graphic design. Finally, in Section 3.3, we overview
automated ﬁtness assignment approaches in evolutionary design.
Generative Design
Evolutionary Design
Automated Fitness Assignment
in Evolutionary Design
Figure 3.1: Topography of the State of the Art. Illustration by Catarina Maçãs.
Each of the following Sections provides a historical perspective for
each subject mentioned above. We made an effort to survey the origins
and pioneers of each subject.
3.1 generative design
In this Section, we overview key approaches in the ﬁeld of GD. First,
in Subsection 3.1.1, we present early generative approaches prior to
the advent of computing technology. Then, in Subsection 3.1.2, we
provide a historical perspective on computer visual art, focused on its
pioneers. Finally, in Subsection 3.1.3, we describe a series of generative
graphic design processes, with focus on three major areas of graphic
design: poster and book cover design; visual identity design; and type
design.
21

22 state of the art
3.1.1 Precursors of Generative Design
GD reaches back to the ﬁrst algorithmic design approaches, from many
centuries ago, employed in ﬁelds such as architectural design, visual
art, and musical and literary composition. These approaches involved
the creation of algorithmic systems that, through randomisation and
combinatorial methods, enabled the systematic creation of multiple
designs.
One of the earliest uses of combinatorial thinking for design pur-
poses occurs in 350 bce performed by the Greek philosopher and
scientist Aristotle [205, 41: p. 140]. In the text On the Parts of Animals
of his political philosophy work Politics, Aristotle discusses different
methods to create plausible designs. He used a biological analogy
to explain a generative design model for plausible animals and, in a
similar way, for plausible cities. The design method described by Aris-
totle consisted of the recombination of interchangeable parts. Thus,
it involved breaking down the animals, or cities, into elementary
parts, identifying the alternatives for each part, and making valid
combinations of them.
In the 15th century, the Italian polymath Leonardo da Vinci em-
ployed combinatorial methods to aid him in the creation of designs for
central-plan churches (see Figure 3.2a) [205, 41: p. 142]. First, Da Vinci
started with the simplest spatial forms (square, octagon, dodecagon,
or circle). Then, he would systematically add circular, semi-circular,
or octagonal spaces to the design, according to preset geometric rules.
This process could allow him to arrive at every conceivable design for
a central-plan church without requiring much effort or imagination.
In the 16th century, the Italian architect Andrea Palladio describes,
implicitly, in his work I Quattro Libri Dell’Architettura (The Four Books
of Architecture), grammatical rules for the design of Italian villas
(houses) [41: p. 142]. This grammar establishes rules of architectural
composition in a similar way as a traditional grammar establishes
rules of Latin usage [ 269: p. 5]. This way, Palladio architectural style is
modelled and characterised by these grammatical rules. According to
Stiny and Mitchell [269: p. 6], the design of almost every villa designed
by Palladio involves the following steps: grid deﬁnition; exterior-wall
deﬁnition; room layout; interior-wall realignment; principal entrances;
exterior ornamentation; windows and doors; and termination.
An ancient example of recombination design systems is a German
landscape game from the 18th century [34: pp. 103–104]. This game
consists of a set of 24 cards that can be assembled horizontally, with
no intrinsic order, to form landscapes with varying composition and

3.1 generative design 23
(a) Da Vinci, 1488
 (b) Durand, 1821
(c) Sullivan, 1923
Figure 3.2: Examples of early generative architectural design: (a) Detail of
a centralised church by Leonardo da Vinci, 1488; (b) Plate 20 by
Jean-Nicolas-Louis Durand, 1821; (c) Plate 2 – Manipulation of the
Organic by Louis Henry Sullivan, 1923.
size. Therefore, the exploration of permutations of cards allows the
building of numerous continuous landscapes. Figure 3.3 shows two
distinct landscapes built from the same set of seven cards.
The French architect Jean-Nicolas-Louis Durand used an analogue
generative approach to the creation of neoclassical architecture from
the combination of different building elements (see Figure 3.2b) [109:
p. 261]. In 1821, Durand wrote the Partie Graphique Des Cours D’ Archi-
tecture (Graphic Portion of the Lectures on Architecture) describing
two complementary design processes to generate neoclassical architec-
ture. The ﬁrst process consists of a bottom-up approach to the creation
of an architectural skeleton; while the second process consists of a
top-down approach to the addition of details [ 70: p. 13].
In 1924, the American architect Louis Henry Sullivan produced a
series of plates titled A System of Architectural Ornament According with
a Philosophy of Man’s Powers (see Figure 3.2c). These plates describe
processes for reproducing ﬂoral ornamentation based on geometrical
constructs [70: p. 13].

24 state of the art
Figure 3.3: Two landscapes built with the same set of cards, 18th century.
The series of objects 3 Standard Stoppages, created in 1913–1914 by
the French-American artist Marcel Duchamp, is an early example of
his ambition to develop a system capable of creating several images
rather than a single one [ 235: p. 101]. To make these objects, Duchamp
dropped three threads of one meter long from the height of one meter
onto three canvas strips. After the threads landed, the artist adhered
them to the canvases to preserve their random curves and cut three
wood slats into their shapes. Duchamp then used these wood slats as
templates in other images [ 235: p. 101].
In 1926, the Swiss-French architect Charles-Édouard Jeanneret, known
as Le Corbusier, wrote Les Cinq Points d’une Architecture Nouvelle (The
Five Points of a New Architecture) describing a set of architectural
principles that he developed to deﬁne his technique. These principles
included: (i) pilotis elevating the mass off the ground; (ii) the free
designing of the ground plan; (iii) the free design of the façade; (iv)
long horizontal windows; and (v) roof gardens. The Villa Savoye is a
clear example of a building incorporating these ﬁve points. According
to El-Khaldi [70: p. 14], Le Corbusier’s ﬁve points of architecture can
be considered as an example of generative systems. They not only
describe an architectural style but also establish speciﬁc goals to guide
the design of a building.
The Swiss graphic designer Karl Gerstner pioneered programmatic
and systematic approaches to design. In 1963, Gerstner [ 87] pub-
lished his most inﬂuential book Programme entwerfen (Designing pro-
grammes), where he describes and exempliﬁes systematic approaches
to graphic design, product design, architecture, literature, music, and
art. Gerstner referred to these approaches as programmes. The idea of
permutation is central to his work [ 124]. For instance, Gerstner [ 87:

3.1 generative design 25
p. 12] developed the “morphological box of the typogram”, a table
that lists typographical attributes and possible options for each at-
tribute. The systematic combination and permutation of these graphic
attributes allow the designer to create variations of a logotype.
In 1968, the American conceptual artist Sol LeWitt began to write
his Wall Drawings. Each Wall Drawing consists of a set of instructions
outlining a visual structure intended to be painted on a wall by drafts-
people [236]. Since the instructions are open to interpretation by the
draftsperson, many different drawings are possible when executing
each Wall Drawing [235: p. 21]. LeWitt [149] emphasises in his Para-
graphs on Conceptual Art the separation of the concept of the work
from its perceptual manifestation [ 236]. As a result, instead of mak-
ing a drawing, the artist encodes his idea as instructions from which
many drawings can be created [ 235: p. 101]. LeWitt [149] states that
“the idea becomes a machine that makes the art”. This statement was
corroborated by Reas et al. [ 236], by comparing the relation between
LeWitt and the draftsperson who executes his Wall Drawings to the
relation between a programmer and the machine that executes his pro-
grams. Nevertheless, it is important to notice that while a draftsperson
may subjectively interpret instructions, a computer will be, by default,
objective.
3.1.2 Early Computer Visual Art
The advent of computing technology propelled in many people the
interest to explore its potential to generate form. This resulted in a
new era of experimentation with the automatic algorithmic creation
of visual art.
The development of artistic experiments with cathode-ray oscillo-
scopes, by the 1950s, can be seen as precursors of computer art, which
emerged in the following decade. In 1947, the American journal of sci-
ence and technology Popular Science Monthly [88] published an article
titled Even Necktie Designers Can Use Electrons. In this article, General
Electric engineers suggest the use of oscilloscopes to sketch patterns
(see Figure 3.4a) that can be useful to designers as a resource of ideas
for textile patterns. This article is one of the ﬁrst references to the use
of an electronic device for design purposes.
In the early 1950s, the American mathematician and artist Ben
Laposky [147] created some of the earliest electronically generated
graphics (see Figure 3.4b). Laposky used an oscilloscope to create the
series of abstract graphics titled Oscillons, or Electronic Abstractions.
These graphics consist of long exposure photographs capturing the

26 state of the art
(a) Unknown, 1947
 (b) Laposky, 1952
(c) Franke, 1961–1962
Figure 3.4: Images created with cathode-ray oscilloscopes: (a) Crop of the
article Even Necktie Designers Can Use Electrons by Perry Githens,
1947; (b) Oscillon 40 by Ben Laposky, 1952; (c) Elektronische Graﬁk 2
by Herbert Franke, 1961–1962.
movement of electronic waveforms projected on the luminescent screen
of an oscilloscope. By manipulating the electrical signals generated
by the oscilloscope, Laposky was able to control the shape of the
waveforms. In the mid- 1950s, the Austrian scientist Herbert Franke
used a similar process to produce the series Elektronische Graﬁken
(Electronic Graphics) (see Figure 3.4c) [264: p. 25].
In addition to the experiments with oscilloscopes, there were also
artists working with mechanical devices and analogue computers. In
1960, the British artist Desmond Paul Henry constructed the ﬁrst of
a series of three mechanical drawing machines from components of
analogue bombsight computers [ 119]. These machines were able to
produce abstract drawings consisting in sequences of overlapping
curves (see Figure 3.5).
In the early 1960s, access to computers was still very restricted.
Only some research laboratories, universities and large corporations
could afford such equipment. As a result, the ﬁrst people who used

3.1 generative design 27
Figure 3.5: Drawings created with mechanical machines built by Desmond
Paul Henry, 1962–1963.
computers creatively were computer programmers, scientists or math-
ematicians who had access to this technology and the expertise needed
to operate it.
The interaction with computers was difﬁcult. There was no graphical
interface and few pre-existing software tools were accessible to the
artists. Artists had to either collaborate with engineers to program
their ideas or write their own computer programs. Therefore, early
computer art practitioners programmed the computers themselves,
developing some of the ﬁrst graphical libraries.
The available output devices were also limited. The most used
outputs were plotters and impact printers, which could be controlled
by computer programs. Therefore, early computer art focused on form
and structure, mostly using black and white linear shapes.
Despite the technological limitations, computer artists explored
different methods to create visual art. In the following paragraphs,
some of which we describe in the following paragraphs.

28 state of the art
(a) Noll, 1963
 (b) Nees, 1964
(c) Nake, 1965
 (d) Mohr, 1969
Figure 3.6: Computer art with random lines: (a) Vertical-Horizontal No. 3 by
Michael Noll, 1963; (b) 23-Corner by Georg Nees, 1964; (c) Ran-
dom Polygon by Frieder Nake, 1965; (d) P-021/A, Scratch Code by
Manfred Mohr, 1969.
Pioneering computer artists, including Michael Noll (see, e.g., [242:
p. 74]), Georg Nees (see, e.g., [ 242: p. 79]), Frieder Nake (see, e.g.,
[242: pp. 77–78]) and Manfred Mohr (see, e.g., [ 206]), explored the
creation of geometric drawings, often linear. These artists, who were
interested in probability theory and random numbers, used computers
connected to plotters to systematically draw lines whose vertices were
positioned at random. Different methods were employed to choose
the coordinates of the vertices, resulting in numerous drawings with
distinct visual structures and styles (see Figure 3.6).
There were also those who used randomness to (dis)place form. For
example, Charles Csuri used a random number generator to position
400 images of toy soldiers in his work Random War (see Figure 3.7a)
[19: p. 18, 242: pp. 81–84]. Csuri also used a random number generator
to determine the following information for each soldier: colour of the
side of the battle (red or black), rotation, name, military rank, army

3.1 generative design 29
(a) Csuri, 1968
 (b) Nees, 1968–71
Figure 3.7: Computer art with random (dis)placement: (a) Random War by
Charles Csuri, 1968; (b) Cubic Disarray by Georg Nees, 1968–1971.
serial number, and status (killed, wounded, missing, awarded a medal
or survived). Another example of the application of randomness in
the arrangement of forms is the work Cubic Disarray by Georg Nees,
in which the artist renders order and chaos through the incremental
variation of the position and rotation of squares (see Figure 3.7b) [265:
p. 17].
(a) Peterson, 1964
 (b) Knowlton and Harmon, 1966
Figure 3.8: Computer art with image digitisation: (a) Digital Mona Lisa by
Philip Peterson, 1964; (b) Studies in Perception I by Kenneth Knowl-
ton and Leon Harmon, 1966.
Some computer artists explored automatic methods to represent
digitised images. These artists scanned the input image, converted
the resulting greyscale values into a grid of symbols, and then drew
the symbols with a plotter, depicting different shades of grey. In 1964,
Philip Peterson created a digital version of the Mona Lisa painting
using pairs of decimal digits (see Figure 3.8a) [226]. Peterson designed
the digits so that the larger their magnitude, the larger their thickness.
As a result, all pairs of digits can render a total of 100 shades of
grey. Later, in 1966, Kenneth Knowlton and Leon Harmon created

30 state of the art
probably the ﬁrst computer-generated nude using electronic symbols
(see Figure 3.8b) [ 242: pp. 86–87]. The symbols were also selected
based on the visual emphasis created with their shape.
Another method used in early computer art is the composition of el-
ementary shapes, or modules, on rectangular grids. Manuel Barbadillo
used a series of modules as an alphabet to build images. These images
consisted in the placement of these modules in a grid in various posi-
tions (see Figure 3.9a) [120: p. 11]. Edward Zajec created algorithms
to distribute a set of linear modules on a rectangular grid according
to a set of combination rules and varying probabilities of occurrence
(see Figure 3.9b) [248: p. 392]. Paul Brown, who demonstrated an early
interest in artiﬁcial life and cellular automata, developed a system
to generate tile-based images by propagating individual elements,
according to a set of simple rules (see Figure 3.9c) [28].
(a) Barbadillo, 1968
 (b) Zajec, 1969
 (c) Brown, 1975
Figure 3.9: Computer art with modular composition: (a) BIONA by Manuel
Barbadillo, 1968; (b) RAM 10/4 by Edward Zajec, 1969; (c) Untitled
Light Gray Ground by Mark Wilson, 1973; (d) Untitled Computer
Assisted Drawing by Paul Brown, 1975.
Computer artists also explored the transformation of digitised line
drawings. Charles Csuri experimented with different methods to trans-
form and morph different drawings. For example, Csuri manipulated
the drawings by applying sine functions to some coordinates of their
points (see Figure 3.10a) [19: p. 8]. Leslie Mezei experimented with
geometric translations to displace the points of line drawings (see
Figure 3.10b) [150: pp. 23–26]. The Japanese computer art group CTG
(Computer Technique Group), founded by Masao Kohmura, Haruki
Tsuchiya, Kunio Yamanaka, and Junichiro Kakizaki, explored other
techniques for deformation and metamorphosis (see Figure 3.10c) [242:
pp. 75–76]. Vera Molnar also transformed line drawings using random
translations (see Figure 3.10d) [264: p. 26].
In 1973, Harold Cohen developed the ﬁrst version of AARON, a
computer system that simulates his drawing style [ 60: p. 162]. AARON
is one of the earliest AI systems capable of drawing autonomously.

3.1 generative design 31
(a) Csuri, 1967
 (b) Mezei, 1967
(c) CTG, 1968
 (d) Molnar, 1974
Figure 3.10: Computer art with line transformation: (a) Sine Curve Man by
Charles Csuri and James Shaffer, 1967; (b) Bikini Shifted by Leslie
Mezei, 1967; (c) Running Cola is Africa by Computer Technique
Group, 1968; (d) Transformations by Vera Molnar, 1974.
During the following decades, this system went through a sequence
of stages, from early drawings resembling children’s drawings (see
Figure 3.11a) to more recent colour drawings depicting persons in
gardens. Ed Burton [ 30]developed the computer program ROSE (Rep-
resentation Of Spatial Experience) to emulate children’s drawings (see
Figure 3.11b). This program creates two-dimensional representations,
visually inspired by the drawings made by young children, from the
general form and structure of three-dimensional objects.
In the 1980s, the ﬁeld of computer art experienced a major shift
[133: pp. 1, 18]. Much of the computer-generated art started to have
a more digital appearance and the algorithmic logic, which charac-
terised this ﬁeld, began to dissolve. This shift can be explained by the
broad adoption of personal computing combined with the develop-
ment of off-the-shelf graphic software ( e.g. for image editing) and the
emergence of user-friendly interfaces. These developments simpliﬁed

32 state of the art
(a) AARON, 1979
 (b) ROSE, 1995
Figure 3.11: Drawings created by artiﬁcial artists: (a) Untitled Drawing by
Harold Cohen’s program AARON, 1979; (b) Drawing of three
animals by Ed Burton’s program ROSE, 1995.
the creation of images using computers. Artists and designers started
to use the computer as a graphical tool, with little need to learn to
programme or understand how to write a computer program. Addi-
tionally, computer-generated graphics started to be applied in other
art-related ﬁelds, such as cinema, television programs, as well as in
video and computer games. This growing application changed the
way artists and people, in general, saw computer art.
There are other sources of information about the history of computer
art and its early practitioners (see, e.g., [29, 150, 242, 248, 264, 278]).
3.1.3 Generative Graphic Design
The collaboration among computer programmers and artists, at the
beginning of the second half of the 20th century, preceded a new
generation of programming graphic designers and artists.
What began as a collaboration among computer programmers and
artists at the beginning of the second half of the 20th century, became
a new specialised generation of programming graphic designers and
artists.
Over the past decades, a growing number of designers and artists
have mastered visual thinking along with algorithmic knowledge.
Nowadays, there is a generation of designers who know programming
languages and whose creative process is often built around program-
matic approaches to design. For these designers, programming is a
natural tool for solving design problems and for creating customised
designs for, e.g. posters, book covers, visual identities, or typefaces.
The designer and educator Muriel Cooper promoted the union of
arts and programming to a new generation of designers who have
inﬂuenced contemporary media, technology and graphic design. In

3.1 generative design 33
1976, Cooper co-founded the Visible Language Workshop at MIT,
which later, in 1985, became a part of the MIT Media Lab [ 45: p. 2].
Through the Visible Language Workshop — positioned in the rich
technological environment of MIT, Cooper and her students explored
the possibilities created by the emerging electronic media to pioneer
new forms, methods and techniques of graphic communication. In
some cases, this was achieved through the creation of custom software
design tools.
An example of the projects developed at the Visible Language Work-
shop is a series of dynamic digital compositions designed by Cooper,
in 1988, and created with software tools developed by Suguru Ishizaki
[45]. This series explores the digital representation of multi-layered
information, namely texts and images. The different compositions
are generated through dynamic variations of size, placement, colour
and opacity of texts and images. This series resembles a collection of
posters based on dynamic layouts.
Figure 3.12: Posters of the Morisawa 10 series by John Maeda, 1996.
The designer John Maeda is another pioneer of computer-generated
designs. In 1996, Maeda [186] designed a series of ten posters for the
Japanese type foundry Morisawa Company using Adobe PostScript
language (see Figure 3.12). Similarly to Cooper’s work, these posters
were created through the variation of visual attributes and the repeti-
tion of Morisawa’s logo [187: pp. 208–219, 245: pp. 14–15].
From 2002 to 2013, Boris Müller [ 212], in collaboration with the
design studio one/one [ 221], created code-based designs for the in-
ternational literature festival Poetry on the Road , held every year in
Bremen, Germany. For each edition of the festival, they designed a gen-
erative system which transforms input texts into abstract images. The
resulting computer-generated images were then used to produce vi-

34 state of the art
Figure 3.13: Posters for the Poetry on the Road festival by Boris Müller, 2002–
2013.
Figure 3.14: Covers of Faber Finds’ books generated by Karsten
Schmidt, 2008.
sual materials for the festival, including book covers and promotional
posters (see Figure 3.13) [74: pp. 197–204, 245: pp. 110–111].
From 2001 to 2014, Andreas Gysin [ 107] and Sid Vanetti created
a series of generative promotional posters and ﬂyers for The Puddle,
an electronic music festival held in Zürich, Switzerland. The main
visual elements of these designs consisted of visual patterns generated
through parametric processes [ 245: p. 37].
In 2008, Faber & Faber publisher launched the print-on-demand
service Faber Finds, for which Karsten Schmidt developed an auto-
mated generative design system that creates several unique designs,
one for every single book cover (see Figure 3.14) [ 5: pp. 54–55, 26:
pp. 136–137]. The program assembles a set of ornamental elements
designed by Marian Bantjes to create a decorative frame around the
book title and author, typeseted with the bespoke typeface B-HMMND
designed by Michael C. Place. For each cover, the graphical elements

3.1 generative design 35
Figure 3.15: Screenshot of Poster Wall by LUST design studio, 2008.
are selected according to the genre of the book (ﬁction, non-ﬁction,
arts, or children).
LUST design studio [ 146] developed, in 2008, the Poster Wall for
the 21st Century for the Graphic Design Museum in Breda, Nether-
lands. This poster wall, which existed both online and as part of the
museum’s installation 100 Years of Graphic Design in the Netherlands ,
automatically generated a feed of unique posters using content gath-
ered from various internet sources (see Figure 3.15) [5: pp. 118–119,
22: pp. 106–107].
Carvalho Bernau’s design studio [35] employed a generative design
process, in 2008, to create the covers for the main paperback book
collection by Octavo Publicaties. The books’ data, including authors,
genre, subject and original publication date, are translated into vi-
sual variables. As a result, this data determines the placement of
typography, coordinates of shapes, and colour scheme.
In 2010, Cruz, Machado, and Bicker [ 51] developed a tool that helps
designers build visual data-driven artefacts to be applied in book cover
designs. This tool maps the occurrences of selected words across the
book’s text into graphics that represent that data. The user can select
different visualisation models that result in different types of visual
artefacts.
In 2011, FIELD design studio [73] generated a series of 10 000 unique
cover artworks for G.F Smith’s paper brochures. Each cover features a
different close-up view on a virtual sculpture created through a gener-
ative process [296]. This series of generative covers aim to illustrate
the possibilities of generative design and digital print.
In 2012, Duro, Machado, and Rebelo [ 66] generated book covers
based on the book’s content. Each cover consists in a composition
of rectangles, in which the number, arrangement, and size of the
rectangles are determined according to the structure of the book
(e.g. number of chapters, or text length of each chapter).

36 state of the art
Figure 3.16: Covers of the Eye magazine (issue 94) designed by MuirMcNeil
design studio (Paul McNeil and Hamish Muir), 2017.
In 2017, Variable studio [287] implemented a series of layout systems
to generate covers for Creative Commons-licensed books available
at Otwórz Ksi ˛ a˙zk ˛ e NGO web portal. These systems use the books’
metadata (e.g. number of pages, publication year and category) and
transform them into graphics to compose covers.
MuirMcNeil design studio [ 199] used, in 2017, a systematic process
to generate 8 000 unique covers for the issue 94 of the Eye magazine
(see Figure 3.16). To create the covers, MuirMcNeil used HP Mosaic, a
program that enables the creation of numerous unique compositions
from a set of input designs, referred to as “seed” image ﬁles. In this
work, the seed images consist of typographic compositions in which
the letters of the word ‘eye’ are repeated in ﬁxed increments, in three
layers, and using different fonts of their TwoPoint and TwoPlus type
families. Using the Mosaic program, MuirMcNeil set systematic rules
to scale, position, crop and modify the colours of sections of each seed
ﬁle to produce the 8 000 unique covers.
The idea of creating visual materials through generative processes
was also applied in the design of visual identities. In recent years,
there has been a growing demand for visual identities characterised
