by variability, context-relatedness, processuality, performativity, and
non-linearity [74: p. 13, 106: p. 122]. Many organisations, institutions,
museums, and even places are adopting dynamic visual identities.
See, for example, the work of Martins et al. [ 192] for a comprehensive
survey on this type of visual identities.
Some dynamic visual identities employ GD processes to automati-
cally create one or more elements of the visual identity system, e.g. the

3.1 generative design 37
logotype. This way, designers can create numerous variations of a
visual identity and achieve tailor-made designs. Designers can also
provide autonomy to a visual identity, allowing it to evolve over time
and be open-ended, or deﬁne how it behaves in a speciﬁc context, how
it interacts with people, and/or how it reacts to input data in real-time
[192: p. 20].
Figure 3.17: Rhizome visual identity (different logos found online) by Sur-
face.de design studio, 2001.
In 2001, Surface.de design studio designed one of the ﬁrsts genera-
tive logotypes for the media art blog Rhizome [ 244]. The logotype is
generated each time it is viewed and consists of a set of coloured lines
with different lengths diverging from the same point (see Figure 3.17).
The length of lines depends on the IP addresses of the last four people
who visited the website [ 188].
Figure 3.18: Casa da Música visual identity by Stefan Sagmeister, 2007.
In 2007, Stefan Sagmeister [252] designed a generative visual identity
for the Portuguese concert hall Casa da Música. This visual identity
explores the architectural shape of the building as a container that
is coloured according to a custom software (see Figure 3.18). This
software implements a colour-picking mechanism to sample an input
image and generate a palette for ﬁlling in the facets of the logo object
with colour [21: p. 200, 213: pp. 146–147, 26: pp. 128–131].
In 2008, Catalogtree design studio [ 36], in collaboration with the
programmer Lutz Isslez, developed a dynamic visual identity for the
architects Monadnock. The logotype consists in the random combina-
tion of two or more characters of the company’s name. Each time the
logotype is saved, exported, or printed, a new version is automatically
generated from the postscript ﬁle.
In 2010, Neue design studio [ 214] created a dynamic visual identity
for the Scandinavian peninsula of Nordkyn (see Figure 3.19). The de-
veloped logotype changes in real-time according to live meteorological
data, namely wind and temperature [ 213: pp. 190–191].

38 state of the art
Figure 3.19: Visit Norkyn visual identity by Neue design studio, 2010.
GD has also been playing a role in the domain of type design. In
1979, Donald Knuth [ 138, 139] introduced the ﬁrst version of Metafont,
a programming language for parametric typeface design. In Metafont,
glyphs, or letterforms, are made up of pen strokes designed by ge-
ometric equations. This enables the generation of different fonts by
varying a set of parameters. Knuth developed Metafont as part of his
typesetting system TeX. It is considered as one of the most inﬂuencing
works in parametric type design and is the basis for some recent
works, such as Metaﬂop, in 2012 [243], and Metapolator, in 2015 [67].
Figure 3.20: Glyphs of Beowolf font designed by LettError studio, 1990.
In 1989, LettError studio [ 23] developed Beowolf (originally called
Random Font), the ﬁrst typeface with random programmed behaviour (see
Figure 3.20). Each point of the glyphs moved at random when printed
using PostScript language. As a result, the glyphs always had an
uncertain and unique shape on the page [ 24, 201: p. 546].
In 1992, Gary McGraw [ 200] and Douglas Hofstadter proposed
Letter Spirit, a system for the automatic generation of the lowercase
letters of the roman alphabet in different but internally coherent styles
(see Figure 3.21). Starting with one or more seed grid-based letters,
the system attempts to create the rest of the alphabet in such a way
that all letters share that same style, or “spirit”.
In 2009, Michael Flückiger [ 76] and Nicolas Kunz developed a
system in which a typeface, named Laika, can dynamically change its
shape according to multiple inputs. This way, users can interactively
control different characteristics of the typeface such as weight, contrast,
size of serifs and slope.
In 2010, Mary Huang [ 126] developed TYPEFACE, a computational
system that translates facial expressions into typeface design. This
system detects the face of the user and uses speciﬁc regions of it to
determine type characteristics such as x-height and slant. This process

3.2 evolutionary design 39
Figure 3.21: Letter Spirit glyphs for different letters (columns) with different
“spirits” (rows) by Mcgraw, 1992.
occurs in real-time, allowing the user to make different faces at the
camera and visualise the typeface adjust itself.
In 2019, Maçãs, Palma, and Rebelo [ 168] presented typEm, a system
that generates glyphs that adapt their shape according to the emotion
of an input text (see Figure 3.22). This system receives a text as input,
extracts the emotions of it (happiness, sadness, anger, fear, disgust,
and surprise) and outputs the input text composed with glyphs whose
shapes adapt throughout the text according to this information. This
work, initially developed in 2013 [165], provides the reader a way to
visually understand the emotion(s) of a text.
Figure 3.22: Glyphs created with typEm system by Maçãs, Palma, and Re-
belo, 2019.
In addition to the works presented in this thesis, one can ﬁnd more
examples of generative graphic design in several publications (see, e.g.,
[26, 235, 245]).
3.2 evolutionary design
EC has been applied to various ﬁelds in the arts. In this Section, we
present applications of EC to visual art and graphic design.
Richard Dawkins’sBiomorphs is ine of the earliest examples of using
EC to create form. It was conceived for his book The Blind Watchmaker,

40 state of the art
in 1986 [55], and presented at the ﬁrst Artiﬁcial Life conference, in
1987 [56]. To demonstrate the power of artiﬁcial evolution, Dawkins
developed a computer program that allowed the evolution of the
morphology of artiﬁcial creatures, or biomorphs (see Figure 3.23). Each
biomorph consists in a tree-like graphical structure whose form depends
on a set of values encoded in its genotype. The biomorphs of each
population were displayed on the screen, so users could guide the
evolutionary process by indicating their favourite ones,i.e. selecting the
ones that would survive and reproduce to create the next generation.
Figure 3.23: Safari park of black-and-white biomorphs by Dawkins, 1986.
Not long after, in the early 1990s, Karl Sims [ 266] and William
Latham in collaboration with Stephen Todd [ 279] followed the foot-
steps of Dawkins [ 55] by combining evolutionary techniques and
computer graphics to create artistic images [ 155: p. 3]. Sims used
user-guided evolution to evolve abstract images, three-dimensional
shapes, and animations. While Todd and Latham presented evolution-
ary techniques to create biomorphic forms. The work of Todd and
Latham popularised evolutionary art by making it available to the
general public [170].
The early work of Dawkins [ 55], Sims [266], Todd and Latham [279],
and Steven Rooke [300] inspired many researchers to explore, during
the succeeding decades, possible applications of EC to aesthetic design
and, consequently, contributed to the emergence of Evolutionary Art

3.2 evolutionary design 41
as a research area [ 155: p. 3, 170]. In the following paragraphs, we
overview evolutionary approaches to create various types of two-
dimensional visual artefacts and designs.
(a) Sims, 1991
 (b) Baluja, Pomerleau,
and Jochem, 1994
(c) Machado and Cardoso,
2000
Figure 3.24: Evolved expression-based images: (a) Primordial Dance by
Sims, 1991; (b) Image evolved by Baluja, Pomerleau, and
Jochem, 1994; (c) Image evolved with NEvAr tool by Machado
and Cardoso, 2000.
In 1991, Sims [ 266] introduced the expression-based approach to
evolve images (see Figure 3.24a). Sims used GP to evolve symbolic Lisp
expressions that, when interpreted, result in images. Each expression
is represented as a tree structure, with mathematical functions and
operators at the internal nodes, and constants or variables at the leaves.
This tree (genotype) is then used to calculate the pixel values of the
resulting image (phenotype). The expression-based approach of Sims
has become popular for the evolution of raster images and inspired
many researchers to explore expression-based image evolution ever
since [155: p. 7].
Examples of expression-based image evolution include, for example,
the works of Baluja, Pomerleau, and Jochem [ 11] (see Figure 3.24b),
Rooke [300], Unemi ( SBART — Simulated Breeding for ART) [ 283],
Greenﬁeld [ 93–95], Machado and Cardoso ( NEvAr — Neuro Evolu-
tionary Art) [173, 174] (see Figure 3.24c), Hart [110], and Secretan et
al. (Picbreeder) [261, 262]. Other researchers have explored the auto-
mated evolution of expression-based images to match target images,
e.g. DiPaola [58]. Machado, Correia, and Romero [ 175] and Correia
et al. [ 48] explore the automated evolution of images that match a
given class of objects, e.g. lips, breasts, leaves and faces. More recently,
Machado et al. [ 184] explore the automated evolution of ambiguous
images, i.e. images that can resemble more than one possible object,
e.g. faces and ﬂowers.
Other researchers have focused on the evolution of fractal images.
For instance, Rowley [ 251] developed a generic toolkit capable of
evolving, for example, fractal images. Angeline [2] presented a method

42 state of the art
(a) Chapuis and Lutton, 2001
 (b) Draves, 2019
Figure 3.25: Evolved fractal images: (a) Image by Chapuis and Lutton, 2001;
(b) Image created with Scott Draves’s Electric Sheep system, 2019.
for evolving fractal movies. Chapuis and Lutton [ 37, 163] created
the ArtiE-Fract system to explore how interactive GP can be used
to generate artistic fractal images (see Figure 3.25a). Ventrella [290,
291] generated images by evolving variations on the Mandelbrot set
against target images, e.g. portrait images. Draves [63, 64] created the
Electric Sheep project, one of the most widely used evolutionary design
projects [155: p. 10]. It consists of a distributed computer system that
evolves fractal animations and displays them as computer screensavers
(see Figure 3.25b). Draves uses a GA to evolve parameterisations of
fractals “ﬂame” with evaluation provided by thousands of people
over the internet. Brown et al. [ 27] used an evolutionary algorithm to
generate fractal images to create tiles of photomosaic images.
Some evolutionary art approaches use swarm and ant behaviour
as a metaphor for the development of computational agents that are
evolved and explored artistically to generate images. One pioneer of
such approaches is Mauro Annunziato [ 3, 4], who generated images
of emergent patterns using populations of evolved drawing agents
(see Figure 3.26a). Each agent is represented as a black point moving
around on a white canvas and described by a set of genetic charac-
teristics that deﬁne its behaviour. Annunziato evolves these agents
through genetic mutations on their characteristics. Aupetit et al. [ 7]
and Monmarché et al. [ 208] used interactive GAs to evolve parameters
for “ant paintings”. These paintings are produced by artiﬁcial ants
that deposit pheromone in the form of one colour while searching
for pheromone of another colour. The evolution of the ant paintings
involves the evolution of the parameters that control the behaviour of
the artiﬁcial painting ants. Gary Greenﬁeld [ 98–100] also used artiﬁcial
evolution to evolve ant paintings (see Figure 3.26b) but using auto-
mated ﬁtness functions based on behavioural statistics of the ants. Jon
McCormack [197, 198] developed evolutionary agent-based swarms

3.2 evolutionary design 43
(a) Annunziato, 1998
 (b) Greenﬁeld, 2004
 (c) Maçãs et al., 2019
Figure 3.26: Evolved agent-generated images: (a) Neuro-Society by Annunzi-
ato, 1998; (b) Ant Painting #21529 by Greenﬁeld, 2004; (c) Data-
driven visual artefact by Maçãs, Lourenço, and Machado, 2019.
to create Niche Constructions. Agents produce line drawings, similar
to Annunziato’s, by leaving a trail of ink as they move around on a
canvas. As agents evolve their drawing style and seeking preferences,
they create diverse and unusual drawings. Maçãs et al. [ 166] use IEC
to evolve parameterisations for swarming agents that create abstract
representations of input time-series data, namely consumption pat-
terns (see Figure 3.26c). Several examples of swarm- and ant-inspired
computer art, evolutionary and non-evolutionary, are presented, for
example, in the work of Greenﬁeld and Machado [ 104, 105].
Some researchers use EC to create different kinds of graphics. Baker
and Seltzer [ 10] developed an IEC system to produce line drawings
by evolving the coordinates of their lines (see Figure 3.27a). Nishio
et al. [217] developed a system to evolve cartoon faces. Lewis [153] pre-
sented Metavolve, an interactive platform for evolving design artefacts,
including cartoon faces (see Figure 3.27b). Dudek [65] presented a GP
approach to interactively evolve movement and drawing commands
that produce line graphics, or turtle graphics as they are widely known.
This work was intended to introduce evolution to children. Heijer and
Eiben [115, 118] evolved abstract and representational vector graphics
images, using Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) as genetic representation.
They automatically generated images with visual styles reminiscent of
Pop Art [116] and Glitch Art [ 112]. Greenﬁeld [ 101] used GP to evolve
the controllers of simulated drawing robots. Bergen and Ross [ 18]
presented JNetic, which uses a GA to evolve vectorial graphics to de-
pict a target image. Vega et al. [ 288] presented the EvoSpace-Interactive,
a web framework that integrates IEC with a distributed cloud-based
approach to enable the collaborative evolution of artistic images.
In recent years, we have observed the emergence of evolutionary
approaches for graphic design purposes, e.g. to generate elements of
visual identities such as logotypes. Yamada and Onisawa [ 301] used

44 state of the art
(a) Baker and Seltzer, 1993
 (b) Lewis, 2000
Figure 3.27: Evolved drawings: (a) Face drawings by Baker and Seltzer, 1993;
(b) Cartoon faces by Lewis, 2000.
IEC to create logotypes composed of shapes and typography through
the evolution of their visual features, such as position, size, and colour.
Michael Schmitz developed the Evolving Logo that adjusts itself to the
state of the company where it is implemented, namely the Max Planck
Institute of Molecular Cell Biology and Genetics, in Germany [ 259,
213: pp. 165–166]. Schmitz uses EC to create logo variations, which
are represented using cellular automata (see, e.g., [44]). Saikawa and
Onisawa [253] present an IEC system that creates logotypes based on
input sketches provided by the user. The Emblemmatic [ 72] collective
developed the online logo generator MarkMaker that allows the inter-
active evolution of logotypes based on text input by the user. Rebelo,
Bicker, and Machado [ 237] used EC to automatically evolve logotypes
intended to communicate feelings selected by the user.
Artiﬁcial evolution has also been used to generate letterforms. One
of the ﬁrst researchers to evolve letters was Dawkins [ 56], who occa-
sionally evolved biomorphs that resemble letters. Dawkins used the
biomorphic letters to sign his name and pay tribute to the microcom-
puter Macintosh that he used to make this work (see Figure 3.28).
Other evolutionary approaches followed speciﬁcally to evolve let-
terforms. For example, Lund [ 161] evolved settings of a parametric
typeface design system. Butterﬁeld and Lewis [ 31] evolved deforma-
tions of typefaces. Levin, Feinberg, and Curtis [ 152] evolved alphabets
of abstract letter shapes. Wada and Hagiwara [ 295] evolved Japanese
fonts. Unemi and Soda [ 284] evolved Japanese Katakana alphabets.
Schmitz [258] generated new fonts through the genetic recombination
of existing ones. Kuzma [ 144] Kuzma, Jakša, and Sinˇ cák [145] evolved

3.2 evolutionary design 45
Figure 3.28: Biomorphs resembling letters by Dawkins, 1987.
Figure 3.29: Evolved human pictograms representing (from left to right)
anger, fear, joy and sadness by Dorris et al., 2004.
parameters that control the design of typefaces. Yoshida, Nakagawa,
and Köppen [304] evolved elementary parts of letters.
Evolutionary approaches were also employed in the generation
of representational graphic symbols. Dorris et al. [ 61] used IEC to
generate human pictograms to visually represent different emotions
(see Figure 3.29). Dozier et al. [ 62] developed a system that allows
the interactive, collaborative, evolution of emojis representative of
facial expressions. Piper, Davis, and Boelhouwer [ 227] used the same
approach to evolve warning symbols. Cunha et al. [ 52] presented an
online evolutionary system for creating new emojis representative of
an input concept speciﬁed by the user. This system evolves emojis
through the blending of existing ones.
EC has also been employed to create new images from other input
images through the evolution of image processing methods or ﬁlters.
Some researchers have evolved recombinations of portions of existing
images to generate image composites of ﬁgurative objects such as hu-
man faces, e.g. Caldwell and Johnston [32], Takagi and Kishi [275], and
Hancock and Frowd [108], or more abstract compositions, e.g. Bache-
lier [8]. Other researchers evolved image variations from input images
using warping and morphing transformations, e.g. Lim [160], who cre-
ated images of human faces, and Graf and Banzhaf [ 92], who created
images of cars. Most expression-based evolutionary systems could
use images as terminal symbols [ 174, 266]. As such, they allow the
execution of ﬁlters that can be applied to any image [ 181, 266, 283,

46 state of the art
300]. There are evolutionary approaches to create image enhancement
and colouring algorithms, e.g. Poli and Cagnoni [ 228], Machado, Dias,
and Cardoso [177], and Greenﬁeld [ 97]. Also, researchers are evolving
artistic ﬁlters for NPR that can transform an input image into a stylised
artwork, e.g. Semet, O’Reilly, and Durand [263], Collomosse and Hall
[43], Collomosse [42], Ross, Ralph, and Zong [ 249], Neufeld, Ross, and
Ralph [215], Barile, Ciesielski, and Trist [ 14], and Baniasadi and Ross
[12].
There are a few applications of EC for interface design. For example,
Quiroz et al. [ 231] developed a system to evolve user interface designs
based on user feedback and a series of interface design guidelines.
Birtolo, Pagano, and Troiano [ 20] evolved colour palettes for user
interface panels. Troiano and Birtolo [ 280] experimented with an
evolutionary approach to design hierarchical menu layouts, including
their colours, considering accessibility, user preferences and standard
guidelines.
Some researchers have employed EC to design how graphic elements
appear on the users’ screen, e.g. through web pages. Monmarché et al.
[209] used IEC to evolve Cascading Style Sheets ( CSS) for web pages.
Oliver, Monmarché, and Venturini [219] extended this work to also
evolve the position of each object on the web page (see Figure 3.30).
Park [223] developed Evo-Web, an IEC-based generator of web pages.
Sorn and Rimcharoen [ 267] presented a web system that employs IEC
to generate templates for web pages, including the HyperText Markup
Language (HTML) ﬁles and the CSS. This system allows the user to rate
individual sections of the evolving web page.
In the related task of layout design, one can ﬁnd some evolutionary
approaches speciﬁcally to create photo compositions. Geigel and Loui
[85, 86] developed an evolutionary system that automatically designs
page layouts for photo albums according to criteria deﬁned by the
user, e.g. page balance, spacing or emphasis. Fuchs [ 80] presented an
evolutionary approach to the automatic picture placement, on web
pages, to minimise the blank space. Sandhaus, Rabbath, and Boll [ 255]
evolved photo compositions, automatically, according to aesthetic
design principles.
The application of EC in layout design goes far beyond the arrange-
ment of photos. EC has been used to evolve layouts of different graphic
elements. Gatarski [83, 84] employed EC to evolve designs for web ad-
vertising banners, in an automatic fashion using the number of clicks
on the evolving banners (see Figure 3.31). Goldenberg [ 89] used EC
to automatically generate page layouts that minimise the blank space.
Purvis [229, 230] also evolved document layouts that satisfy content

3.2 evolutionary design 47
Figure 3.30: Evolved web page layouts by Oliver, Monmarché, and Ven-
turini, 2002.
Figure 3.31: Evolved banners by Gatarski, 2002.
and layout constraints, as well as some desired visual characteristics.
Quiroz et al. [ 232] evolved brochure documents according to user
preferences and design guidelines. Morcillo et al. [ 210] and Önduygu
[220] presented the IEC-based systems GAUDII and Gráphagos, respec-
tively, to create page layout designs. Kitamura and Kanoh [ 135] used
EC to generate poster designs. Klein [ 136] developed Crossing, Mixing,
Mutating, a tool that generates variations of a layout design using
genetic operators. Later, a plug-in for Adobe InDesign was developed,
named Evolving Layout, implementing a similar approach [ 137]. More
recently, Rebelo et al. [238] used EC to generate designs of typographic
posters.
In addition to the approaches presented in this Section, one can ﬁnd
further reading on evolutionary art and design, for example, in the
works of Takagi [274], Bentley and Corn [ 17], Johnson and Cardalda
[130], Whitelaw [298], and Lewis [ 155].

48 state of the art
3.3 automated fitness assignment in evolutionary de -
sign
One key component of EC algorithms is ﬁtness assignment, i.e. the pro-
cess of evaluating the quality of the individuals in the population (see
Section 2.2). In the arts domain, the development of appropriate ﬁtness
assignment strategies is a major challenge [ 170, 180]. One approach to
assigning ﬁtness is to use users in the evolutionary loop to manually
select or rank individuals of the population. Another approach is to
develop an automated ﬁtness function to computationally assign a ﬁtness
value to each individual. In some cases, these two approaches are used
together, creating a hybrid approach where computer automation and
user feedback are combined (see, e.g., the pioneering work of Ventrella
[289]).
Many evolutionary approaches in visual art and design rely on
human interaction to assign ﬁtness [ 274]. Although these approaches
have been able to evolve a wide variety of artefacts and designs,
they present limitations [ 169, 170, 274]. The main problem is the user
fatigue caused by the evaluation of a large number of individuals until
adequate results are achieved. One way to avoid user intervention and
thus save human effort is to automate ﬁtness assignment. However,
automating ﬁtness assignment in visual design domains is difﬁcult
and poses more challenges [ 82, 155]. Human aesthetic evaluation is
often subjective and thus difﬁcult to express in a computational form.
This challenge may explain not only the popularity of IEC but also the
emerging interest by researchers in investigating automated ﬁtness
assignment in evolutionary art and design.
The study of contributions in this topic led us to the identiﬁcation
of two major types of automated ﬁtness functions: (i) hardwired
ﬁtness functions; and (ii) ﬁtness functions based on ML techniques. The
following paragraphs overview several automated ﬁtness strategies in
the context of image evolution.
Hardwired ﬁtness functions are built by a human who decides what
should be taken into account to assign ﬁtness. In the context of visual
art and design, varied criteria have been encoded in hardwired ﬁtness
functions, including aesthetic measures, image analysis metrics, visual
features, design guidelines and other attributes.
Ross et al. [ 250, 299] extracted visual features from input images,
such as colour, shape and smoothness, and used them as a target to
guide the evolution of expression-based texture images with character-
istics similar to the input ones (see Figure 3.32). Later, Ross et al. [ 249]
evolved images using a model of aesthetics that favoured a bell curve

3.3 automated fitness assignment in evolutionary design 49
Figure 3.32: Textures evolved to exhibit characteristics similar to input images
(left column), by Wiens and Ross, 2002.
distribution of colour gradients, along with a feature analysis that
compared the colour distribution histograms of evolved images and
input images. Machado and Cardoso [ 174] used measurements to
assign ﬁtness in the evolution of expression-based images, namely
image complexity, estimated using the JPEG compression ratio and
error, and processing complexity estimated using fractal compression.
Greenﬁeld has investigated varied strategies to automate ﬁtness for
image generation, from the co-evolution of digital image ﬁlters [ 95, 96]
to the analysis of the behaviour of simulated ants and robots [ 98, 100].
Ekárt, Sharma, and Chalakov [ 69] employed four aesthetic measures
of other authors to analyse users’ preference in IEC and consequently
used a combination of two of these measures to automatically evolve
images starting from the result of the interactive evolutionary process.
Heijer and Eiben [113, 114, 117] employed different aesthetic measures
of other authors as ﬁtness functions in the evolution of expression-
based images, namely Benford’s law [ 132], fractal dimension [ 268],
global contrast factor [ 196], information theory [ 246], relation between
image complexity and processing complexity [ 172], bell curve [ 249]
and reﬂectional symmetry [ 112]. Additionally, they used the measures
of bell curve and global contrast factor to automatically evolve abstract
SVG [115, 118] and explored another measure, which favours contrast
in hue, to evolve SVG with a pop art style [ 116, 118]. Maçãs, Lourenço,
and Machado [ 167] evolved the parameters of a swarm-based data
visualisation model using a ﬁtness function that estimates the image
complexity of the output visualisation.
Still in the context of image evolution, another common method
used in hardwired ﬁtness functions is to calculate the similarity or
error between evolved images and target images. This process can

50 state of the art
Figure 3.33: Evolved progression of portraits that try to resemblance Charles
Darwin portrait (top left image), by DiPaola, 2005.
be performed, for instance, by computing the pixel-by-pixel colour
distance between images. An early example of this ﬁtness strategy was
presented by Ventrella [290–292], who evolved Mandelbrot set images
against target images. Over the years, more evolutionary approaches
with similar ﬁtness functions have been explored. DiPaola [58, 59] used
an expression-based system to evolve images to match a target one (see
Figure 3.33). This is a symbolic regression task that has been tried by
many authors, including Sims, without success, in the sense that they
failed to reproduce the target image. Using a different representation,
Johansson [128] evolved renderings of the Mona Lisa painting using
semi-transparent polygons; and Bergen and Ross [18] evolved vectorial
graphics to represent target images. In the same line of work, Aguilar
and Lipson [1] employed a ﬁtness function that compares simulated
brush strokes against a given photograph to guide an evolutionary
system that controls a physical painting machine. Heijer [ 112] evolved
image glitch operations using as ﬁtness their impact on input images,
which is calculated using the difference, in greyscale, between the
original image and the modiﬁed version.
Automated hardwired ﬁtness has also been employed in graphic
design to evolve solutions that comply with explicit design constraints
and aesthetic goals. For instance, Fuchs [ 80] evolved web page layouts
using a ﬁtness based on the aesthetic goal of minimising blank space.
Goldenberg [89] used a similar ﬁtness function to evolve page layouts.

3.3 automated fitness assignment in evolutionary design 51
Figure 3.34: Evolved poster designs by Rebelo et al., 2018.
Purvis [229, 230] also evolved page layouts using a ﬁtness function
that measured different aspects of the document such as alignment,
balance, legibility and compactness. Geigel and Loui [ 85] evolved
photo book layouts using ﬁtness based on different criteria, including
balance, spacing, chronology, emphasis and unity. Photo book layouts
were also evolved by Sandhaus, Rabbath, and Boll [ 255], who used
a ﬁtness function based on metrics and rules of layout design. Some
researchers [ 20, 231, 280] presented evolutionary approaches with
automated ﬁtness to support the design of graphical user interfaces,
namely the selection of colours according to contrast ratios. More
recently, Rebelo et al. [ 238] evolved typographic posters using as
ﬁtness the size of the text boxes and space occupied by the text placed
on them (see Figure 3.34).
The idea of automating ﬁtness assignment in visual design domains
using ML can be traced back to the seminal work of Baluja, Pomerleau,
and Jochem [11] (see Figure 3.24b). In this work, the ﬁtness is calcu-
lated with an ANN trained to learn human aesthetic preferences. In a
ﬁrst stage, an IEC algorithm was used to interactively evolve images,
and then the classiﬁcations were used to train an ANN. This work
prompted the emergence of other contributions to the automation of
ﬁtness assignment in evolutionary art that have been presented over
the years.
Romero et al. [ 247] suggested the idea of combining a general-
purpose evolutionary art system with a ML image classiﬁer trained
to detect faces, or other objects, to evolve autonomously such type
of images. This idea was then explored by Machado, Correia, and
Romero [175] using an off-the-shelf face detector to guide the evolution
of expression-based images evocative of human faces. Later, Correia
et al. [48] combined a conventional expression-based evolutionary art
system with ML classiﬁers to automatically evolve recognisable images

52 state of the art
Figure 3.35: Evolved ambiguous images, containing faces and ﬂowers, by
Machado et al., 2015.
of faces, lips, breasts and leaves. A similar approach was explored
by Tanjil and Ross [276], who used a pre-trained deep Convolutional
Neural Network (CNN) to provide ﬁtness in the evolution of images
that seek to maximise its conﬁdence score for a target image label,
e.g. “banana”. In the same line of work, Machado et al. [ 184] combined
pairs of object detectors to evolve ambiguous images, i.e. images
that may induce multistable perceptions. In such evolved ambiguous
images, it is possible to simultaneously recognise two objects in the
same region, for example, a face and a ﬂower (see Figure 3.35).
Another way to automate ﬁtness assignment using ML is by learning
human aesthetic preferences and using the obtained model as ﬁtness
function to guide the evolution of images. In such approaches, ML
models learn aesthetic preferences by analysing features of images
considered as aesthetically appealing by users, either through the
interaction with an IEC system or the selection of external images. See,
for example, the work of Baluja, Pomerleau, and Jochem [ 11] (already
mentioned above), Hornby and Bongard [ 125] and Li et al. [ 156].
Other researchers build ﬁtness functions, using ML techniques, to
promote novelty during the evolution of images. An early example is
the work of Saunders and Gero [256], who employed a Self-Organising
Map (SOM), a type of ANN, to determine novelty in the evolution of
expression-based images by using as ﬁtness function the dissimilarity
of the evolved images to the clusters in the SOM. Machado et al. [ 180,
182] used the competition between an evolutionary artist and an adap-
tive critic to promote style change in the evolution of images. The critic
consists of an ANN that tries to discriminate between images created
by the evolutionary artist and external images created previously. The
artist consists of an EC engine that tries to create images similar to
external images and, this way, tries to fool the critic into considering
them as external. In each iteration, the critic is re-trained with images
it fails to discriminate and, consequently, the ﬁtness changes. This
iterative process causes the artist to search for novelty and explore new
paths. One could say this work implements an adversarial paradigm

3.3 automated fitness assignment in evolutionary design 53
that is in a sense analogous to the behaviour of Generative Adversarial
Networks (GANs) [90].
While automated ﬁtness assignment has achieved notable results,
allowing the automatic generation of results, it can also present some
limitations. For instance, when using ANNs to assign ﬁtness, the evolu-
tionary process tends to ﬁnd ways of exploiting the ANN’s limitations
to fool them [ 11, 169, 216, 271]. Another shortcoming of fully au-
tomated evolutionary systems is the difﬁculty of users to express
themselves through such systems [169], i.e. to evolve designs that meet
their goals or preferences.
The automation of ﬁtness assignment for visual design domains
poses many interesting problems and challenges. Ultimately, it re-
quires general AI since it implies the consideration of aesthetic, func-
tional, and, even, cultural criteria. Nevertheless, we consider that
several improvements can be made without general AI, for instance,
the creation of hybrid automated ﬁtness functions that combine sev-
eral techniques, including data-driven ML, theoretical knowledge, and
hardwired functions.
Additional reading on ﬁtness assignment in evolutionary art and
design can be found, for example, in the works of Galanter [ 82] and
Johnson [129].

Part II
T Y P E D E S I G N

4
E V O L U T I O N A R Y T Y P E D E S I G N
This part of the thesis concerns our investigation into the autonomous
evolution of glyphs, or letterforms. Our goal is to enable the quick
generation of glyph designs from scratch with a low effort from the
user. The results of these research efforts are materialised through
the computer system Evotype (after evolutionary type). This system
takes advantage of the exploratory capabilities of EC combined with
autonomous ﬁtness assignment. This way, Evotype can evolve pop-
ulations of several glyph designs that are automatically evaluated
according to varied criteria, e.g. legibility. To this end, we resort to a
variety of techniques including ML.
We present and test different iterations of Evotype, with focus on the
evolution of glyphs for the uppercase letters of the Roman alphabet
(see Figure 4.1). In these iterations, we explore different approaches to
evolve glyph designs, e.g. varying the genetic representation, and test
different approaches to automatically evaluate them.
In the ﬁrst Evotype iteration, glyphs are designed using combinations
of line segments positioned in a rectangular grid and evaluated using
a ML model trained with glyphs of several existing typefaces. In
the second iteration, glyphs are designed through the combination
of shapes supplied by the user and evaluated using a combination
of visual similarity to existing typefaces and an ML model. In the
third iteration, we explore a stencil-based approach, wherein stencils
composed of line segments are evolved to allow the design of all letters
in a coherent way. In this last iteration, we allow the user to express,
through a custom interface, a set of visual preferences to be pursued
during the evolutionary process.
The series of Evotype iterations allowed us to experiment with new
forms of typographic expression and investigate different compro-
mises between legibility, visual coherence and expressiveness. With
this work, we contribute to show how AI can inform contemporary
design practices, address challenges in type design and expand the
tools for the optimisation of the design process.
57

58 evolutionary type design
Figure 4.1: Screenshots of different software tools developed for the Evotype
system.

4.1 context 59
4.1 context
Typefaces are an essential resource employed by graphic design-
ers [162]. Although most designers use typefaces selected from vast
libraries of existing examples, some opt to create custom typefaces or
letterings. Several factors may contribute to the continuous creation
of new typefaces: (i) the need to meet requirements and goals that
are speciﬁc to each design project; (ii) the need to create designs that
establish an identity or draw attention, e.g. logotypes; (iii) the desire
to experiment with type design and to tailor typefaces to personal
preferences; and (iv) the desire to explore new creative possibilities
enabled by emerging technologies.
However, designing a typeface is a laborious task that involves the
creation of several glyphs. In typography, a glyph consists of a shape
intended to represent, graphically, a character, digit or punctuation
mark. Some people use the term letterform interchangeably to refer to
a type-glyph. Figure 4.2 shows lowercase letters embodied by different
glyphs.
Figure 4.2: Original drawings for the typeface Futura by Paul Renner, 1920s.
The demand for new typefaces, along with the effort required to
design them, creates a demand for tools that can assist the designer in
this process. Although existing computational design tools are effective
for precise design tasks during the later phases of the design process,
they offer little support to design exploration during the earliest,
essentially conceptual, stages of the design process. Designers tend to
diverge in terms of ideas, in the design exploration process, to come
up with different design options. Yet, ﬁnding the initial inspiration is
one of the most difﬁcult parts of the type design process [ 40: p. 8].
We consider that the process of designing typefaces can be improved
with the development of new computational tools for rapid design
exploration. Such tools should automatically provide to the designer a
wide variety of adequate, and possibly thought-provoking glyphs, thus
amplifying the range of design options to be explored and fostering
new ideas.

60 evolutionary type design
4.2 related work
Some researchers have employed EC to generate type designs. As
previously mentioned, one of the ﬁrst researchers to evolve letters was
Dawkins [56], with his biomorphs (see Section 3.2). Later, Butterﬁeld
and Lewis [31] employ IEC to present populations of deformed letters.
In this work, letters of a given typeface are individually deformed
by surface primitives encoded in the genotype of each candidate in-
dividual. Lund [161] uses IEC to evolve the settings for a parametric
typeface. Each parameter controls a particular visual characteristic of
the typeface. Levin, Feinberg, and Curtis [ 152] present the interactive
system Alphabet Synthesis Machine that allows the user to generate
abstract letterforms. The system employs a GA to evolve a population
of letterforms according to ﬁtness metrics obtained from an initial
seed glyph provided by the user. Unemi and Soda [ 284] developed an
IEC-based system for the design of Japanese Katakana glyphs. It allows
the construction of glyphs using simple elements that are controlled
by parameters encoded in the genotype and drawn along a preset
skeleton. Schmitz [258] presents the interactive program genoTyp, in
which typefaces can be generated according to genetic operations.
The program allows the user to experiment with the breeding of
existing typefaces as well as the manipulation of their genetic infor-
mation, i.e. vertices. Kuzma [144] investigates the potential of IEC by
implementing the Font Evolving System, which allows the user to inter-
actively evolve fonts. Yoshida, Nakagawa, and Köppen [304] present
the system Personal Adapted Letter. The user starts by manually deﬁn-
ing the parts that make the glyphs. Then, IEC is employed to modify
those parts, presenting different options for the user to choose from in
each iteration.
The existing EC systems for type design have some limitations and
drawbacks. Nearly all need continuous human interaction. The user
is asked to manually guide the evolutionary process by selecting the
favourite solutions in each generation until an acceptable result is ob-
tained. Therefore, these approaches suffer from the known limitations
of IEC, such as user fatigue and, consequently, lead to an inefﬁcient
exploration of the search space. Additionally, some approaches require
pre-existing typefaces (e.g. [31, 144, 258]) or skeletons (e.g. [284]), while
others require the drawing of initial seed glyphs ( e.g. [152]) or the
identiﬁcation of letter parts ( e.g. [304]). Some need the creation of
parametric typefaces (e.g. [161]).
Taking into account these shortcomings, we extract two major obser-
vations that guided the research presented in the following Chapters:

4.2 related work 61
(i) Evotype should be autonomous by using automated ﬁtness functions
and (ii) it should be ﬂexible through a modular implementation that
enables the exploration of different design and evaluation methods.

5
E V O T Y P E I : E V O LV I N G G LY P H S W I T H L I N E
S E G M E N T S
This Chapter describes our ﬁrst effort to computationally evolve glyph
designs. We present the ﬁrst iteration of the Evotype system, which
evolves glyphs made of line segments with automated ﬁtness as-
signment based on an Optical Character Recognition ( OCR) approach.
Figure 5.1 shows typical glyphs evolved with the system.
Figure 5.1: Alphabet generated with Evotype.
5.1 framework
The system presented herein relies on a GA to evolve glyph designs for
various characters in a parallel and autonomous way. To evolve glyphs
for the different characters, we implement an island model to evolve
various populations of glyph designs in parallel with the occasional
exchange of individuals among populations through a migration
mechanism. Each population lives on its own island and is composed
of individuals, i.e. glyph designs, that intend to represent the same
character. Thus, to evolve, for instance, glyphs for 26 characters, we
use 26 populations in 26 islands.
The system framework is illustrated in Figure 5.2 and behaves as
described in the next sentences. The evolutionary process begins with
the initialisation of all populations with randomly created glyphs. The
individuals are evaluated and then selected for mating according to
their ﬁtness. Recombination and mutation operators are applied to
generate offspring. The selection stage follows, determining which
individuals proceed to the next generation. The next step is migration,
where individuals may be moved to or received from other islands.
63

64 evotype i : evolving glyphs with line segments

/arrow_right
/arrow_right
Figure 5.2: Framework overview.
This whole process is repeated until the termination criterion is met,
in this case, a given number of generations.
We structured the system as a modular framework composed of
two parts: (i) the generation module that implements a GA to create
candidate glyphs and (ii) the evaluation module that employs an
automatic ﬁtness assignment approach to evaluate the glyphs provided
by the ﬁrst module.
5.1.1 Representation
The genotype consists of a set of genes encoding a glyph. Each gene
encodes a two-dimensional line segment and is composed of a se-
quence of ﬁve numbers, wherein the ﬁrst four are the coordinates of
the endpoints and the last one corresponds to the thickness (see Fig-
ure 5.3). The genotype length may vary from individual to individual.
Thus, different individuals can be composed of a different number of
line segments.

Figure 5.3: Genotype structure containing a set of genes that codify line seg-
ments (component 1) deﬁned by their endpoints (components 2
and 3) and their thickness (component 4).
The phenotype is the graphical representation of the genotype,
i.e. an image of the glyph created from the encoded parameters. The
expression process consists in a drawing of dark line segments, deﬁned
by the genotype, on a white canvas, as illustrated in Figure 5.4.
A rectangular grid is used to constraint the coordinates of the
endpoints of the line segments to the grid points. The density of the
grid is conﬁgured by the user.

5.1.2 Initialisation and Variation
The initial populations are seeded with randomly generated glyphs.
Each glyph of the ﬁrst population is composed of a single line segment,
with all the gene values set by uniform random selection over the
admissible interval for each parameter. All islands receive identical
initial populations. This initialisation setup provides equality and
simplicity among all initial populations, enabling us to assess the
ability of the system to evolve glyphs for different characters, starting
from the same set of random glyphs.
We consider that the genetic operators should be coherent with
the semantics of the candidate solutions [ 13]. Although we could use,
for example, a two-point crossover, this could result in exchanges of
distant line segments between parents, since line segments placed
side by side in the genotype can be on opposite sides of the canvas.
Therefore, to promote evolvability, we propose a crossover operator
that exchanges line segments according to their position on the phe-
notype, regardless of their position in the genotype (see Figure 5.5).
This operator proceeds as follows: (i) randomly select a rectangular
area of the grid; (ii) determine, for both parents, the line segments
whose middle points are inside the rectangle; and (iii) exchange those
line segments between parents preserving their integrity. Note that
the crossover can be asymmetric, i.e. the number of line segments a
genotype “receives” may be different from the one it “donates”.
The mutation operation changes the values encoded in the genes by
a value of one (see Figure 5.6). At the phenotype level, this variation
results in the minimum translation of one of the endpoints of the line
segment in one of the four possible directions (up, down, left or right)
or the minimum variation of its thickness. This way, small variations
on the genotype result in small variations on the phenotype. Still, the
impact of a translation at the phenotype level depends on the density
of the grid, being that a denser grid allows smaller visual variations.
The probability of mutation is deﬁned per gene, meaning that multiple
genes can be mutated.

will be exchanged (genes 1, 2, and 3). On the right, the resulting
individuals after crossover operation.

Figure 5.6: Mutation process. On the left, an individual to be mutated in

During mutation, genes can also be deleted and inserted. This
allows the variation of the genotype length, i.e. the number of genes.
Therefore, there are two other mutation probabilities, one for deleting
an existing gene selected at random and another for inserting a new
random gene.
Some variations are prevented during mutation, including transla-
tions of coordinates that (i) go beyond the grid limits, (ii) create line
segments with null length, or (iii) create two line segments deﬁned by
the same endpoints.
5.1.3 Evaluation
When designing, or in this case, evolving glyphs for characters, one
important precondition is to ensure their legibility, i.e. they should
be easily recognised as the intended character. Although legibility
depends on human perception, we implement a ﬁtness function based
on the computational recognition of each evolved design as a given
letter. Since we are evolving multiple populations, one for each target
character, each individual should be evaluated as a candidate glyph
for the character represented by its population. Therefore, the ﬁtness
assignment approach should be able to provide ﬁtness in each popu-
lation and guide their evolution towards different target characters.
Taking the above into account, we use an OCR system to assign
ﬁtness and this way evolve glyphs whose phenotypes, i.e. images,

5.1 framework 67
are computationally recognised as the characters represented by their
populations. Each evolved glyph is processed as a bitmap image by the
OCR system and evaluated based on intermediary values calculated
by the ML classiﬁcation model. Details about the ML model and the
ﬁtness function are presented in Section 5.2.
5.1.4 Migration
A migration process is used to occasionally move glyphs between the
different populations. This can allow the exchange of characteristics
between populations that may be useful for the evolution of glyphs
for different letters and that may promote visual coherence between
evolved glyphs. Migration can occur once per island in each generation.
The probability of occurrence is determined by a parameterised rate.
To study the inﬂuence of migration in the evolutionary process, we
considered three migration strategies:
• migrationNone — No migration is used. The islands are isolated.
• migrationRand — When migration occurs, each island randomly
selects one individual from another island. A copy of the selected
individual is added to the population on the island, replacing
the individual with the worst ﬁtness.
• migrationFit — When migration occurs, each island evaluates its
individuals according to the environments of the other islands,
i.e. using their ﬁtness functions. A copy of the individual that at-
tains the highest ﬁtness is added to the queue of that destination
island. When this process is completed for all islands, each is-
land checks its immigration queue and, if it is not empty, selects
the ﬁttest individual. This individual is added to the population
on the island, replacing the individual with the worst ﬁtness.
5.1.5 Implementation
The system is implemented in Java, using the open-source library
Processing1. The system is able to ﬂuidly show the evolutionary pro-
cess through a simple graphical user interface, conceived to allow
the user to visualise the evolved glyphs of all islands (see Figure 5.7).
The islands (each corresponding to a character) are arranged hori-
zontally in different columns, in alphabetical order from left to right.
The individuals (glyphs) of the current generation on each island are
1 https://processing.org

68 evotype i : evolving glyphs with line segments
Figure 5.7: Screenshot of Evotype.
depicted vertically, in descending order of ﬁtness. At any moment of
the evolutionary process, the user can export the evolved glyphs as
vector ﬁles to make design reﬁnements. A demo video can be seen at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype1_demo.mov.
The ﬁtness value of each evolved glyph is visualised through a
horizontal line that overlays the column of the corresponding island.
These lines are then vertically positioned according to the mapped
value of the ﬁtness value to the height of the interface (higher ﬁtness
values on top). We consider that this ﬁtness visualisation technique
is functional for the purposes of this work, in the sense that we are
not particularly interested in seeing the speciﬁc ﬁtness values. Instead,
we want to visualise the distribution and progress of ﬁtness over time
and compare ﬁtness values among islands.
5.2 experimentation
We conduct experiments to assess the ability of the approach to evolve
glyphs for different characters and study the impact of the three
migration strategies in the evolutionary process. This way, we tested
three experimental setups, one for each migration strategy. We evolve a
total of 26 islands, one for each uppercase letter of the Roman alphabet.
The experimental parameters of the GA are summarised in Table 5.1.
To calculate the ﬁtness of the evolved glyphs, we perform OCR
using a series of ML classiﬁers, one per target character. The type of
classiﬁer that we use is a Sparse Network of Winnow ( SNoW) [6, 33],
a sparse network of linear units. A SNoW classiﬁer is characterised
by having two layers: the input layer and n output nodes linked

5.2 experimentation 69

Table 5.1: Experimental parameters of the evolutionary engine.

Threshold (θ) 200

Training iterations 1000
Examples per island 78
Table 5.2: Experimental parameters of the classiﬁers.
through weighted edges. We trained 26 different classiﬁers. The input
examples are bitmap images of the glyphs of 78 different typefaces.
These are pre-processed to extract a set of features, which is then
used for training and classiﬁcation. The process of feature extraction
is based on the work of Artan et al. [ 6]. The overall parameters of the
classiﬁers are summarised in Table 5.2.
We use a “one versus all” strategy for training to deal with the OCR
multi-class classiﬁcation problem. Therefore, we train each classiﬁer by
treating all the instances (glyphs) of the character as the positive class
and all instances of the other characters as the negative class. Thus,
each classiﬁer has two output nodes: one for the positive class (the
character in question) and one for the negative class (the other charac-
ters). The activation of each node indicates the degree of certainty that
the input image belongs to the respective class.
To assign ﬁtness, each input image is mapped into a two-dimensional
space where the x coordinate corresponds to the activation value of the
positive class node of the classiﬁer, and the y coordinate corresponds
to the activation value of the negative class node. Thus, the ideal

70 evotype i : evolving glyphs with line segments
scenario would be maximising x while minimising y. Unfortunately,
this is typically not possible since character glyphs share typograph-
ical characteristics among them. For instance, the input pattern that
maximises the x coordinate for the letter Q yields a high y value due to
the presence of examples of the letter O in the negative class. Therefore,
as is typical in multi-objective optimisation problems, a compromise
between x and y is needed to obtain good results.
We establish a target activation point empirically. We begin by
calculating the input pattern that minimises the y coordinate. The
activation value of the output node corresponding to the negative class
for this pattern becomes yt. Then, we calculate the input pattern for
which the absolute difference between the activation values between
x and y is minimal. Such input, typically, has the features needed
for the image to be classiﬁed as a member of the positive class, but
still possesses features that are common with other characters. The
activation value of the output node corresponding to the positive class
for this pattern becomes xt. The ﬁtness of a new input glyph image g
is based on the following distance formula:
dist(g) =‖(xt, yt)− (xg, yg)‖ ,
where xg and yg are the activation values of the output nodes for the
input image g. Since we want to minimise the distance between these
two points, the ﬁtness function becomes:
ﬁtness(g) = 1
1 + dist(g)
Using this ﬁtness function to automatically guide the evolutionary
process, we run each experimental setup 30 times, with different
random seeds, and analyse the experimental results in terms of ﬁtness
progression, migration paths, and visual appearance of the evolved
glyphs.
We begin the analysis of the experimental results by discussing the
visual outputs. Figure 5.8 presents typical glyphs evolved in different
evolutionary runs. First, based on the visual results, the system is
arguably able to generate legible designs. Second, the evolved glyphs
exhibit visual diversity. While the evolutionary process was converging
to suitable glyphs, we observed interesting designs created along the
way, i.e. in intermediate generations, that test the limits of legibility.
Also, we can say the system can evolve glyphs both as individuals (in
relation to the target letter to increase their individual recognisability)
and as a species (in contradistinction to other letters to expand the
diversiﬁcation in the alphabet as a whole).

5.2 experimentation 71
Figure 5.8: Glyph designs evolved in different runs.

72 evotype i : evolving glyphs with line segments
When inspecting the visual results obtained during the evolutionary
processes, we observed evolved glyphs that are recognised by the
classiﬁers as the target character but, from a human point of view,
they do not resemble the expected target character.
This can be explained as follows. These misclassiﬁed examples can
be seen as shortcomings of the classiﬁers that can be explained by an
issue related with the “one versus all” approach used in the training
of the classiﬁers. For example, the classiﬁer for letter Q is trained
against examples of letters C and O. This way, the generalisation of
letter Q tends to focus on what is not common with the other two
letters, which in this case is mostly the tail ( i.e. the descending stroke).
This promotes the evolution of designs for letter Q that are overly
focused on the tail, sometimes even without the bowl ( i.e. the closed
rounded part). As a result, in some evolutionary runs, this can cause
the evolution to be stuck in a local maximum for some generations.
One possible solution for this issue is the use of domain knowledge
during the training of the classiﬁers to avoid training each classiﬁer
against letters with similar visual features.

Figure 5.12: Average number of migrations between islands when using
migrationFit. Origin islands are positioned horizontally and des-
tiny islands vertically. Frequent migrations are highlighted with

Figure 5.12, we visualise the average number of migrations among
islands when using migrationFit. For the island of letter Z, the migration
results indicate that it does not receive individuals from other islands,
so the performance of migrationNone and migrationFit are comparable.
The same explanation applies to the results obtained for the islands of
letters A and V. An explanation for the results observed in the island
of letter W is harder to provide. In this case, migrationFit seems to be
detrimental to the evolutionary process. The migration of individuals
from island A to island W, although helpful in the early generations,
appears to lead to premature convergence to suboptimal glyphs.
Islands corresponding to visually similar letters tend to exchange
more individuals among them. As shown in Figure 5.12, examples
of these migrations include: A→W, B→D, E→BF, F→P, KX→N, L→E,
HNV→M, C↔G, D↔O, I↔T and P↔R.

5.3 summary 75
5.3 summary
The experimentation with the system allowed us to assess its ability
to autonomously evolve legible glyph designs for different letters. The
experimental results also show that migration of glyphs among islands
is beneﬁcial, provided that their ﬁtness in the destination population
is taken into account. We highlight the ability of the system to create
glyphs starting with no seed designs or knowledge, i.e. starting from
random initial individuals, and being guided throughout the evolu-
tionary process only by an automated ML-based ﬁtness function. The
obtained results indicate that we successfully used an ML approach to
automatically guide the evolution of glyph designs.
The architecture of the system, particularly the independence of the
generation and evaluation modules that comprise it, can be suitable for
other applications. For example, by changing the evaluation module,
we could evolve other graphics besides glyph designs, including icons
or pictograms.
Based on the results obtained, we identiﬁed some aspects for im-
provement. We consider that the visual coherence among the glyphs
evolved can be improved. Although migration of glyphs between is-
lands helps in the exchange of genetic information that is useful for
the evolution of glyphs for different letters, the glyphs are still evolved
as individuals and not as a family of glyphs that form a coherent type-
face. As a result, the glyphs evolved may have different visual styles.
The glyphs evolved could be improved with the presence of common
visual characteristics between glyphs of different letters, forming a
coherent typeface. We also consider that the visual expressiveness
of the glyphs can be increased. The glyphs evolved with the system
could beneﬁt, for example, from other genetic representations that
could allow a wider range of graphic primitives, promoting a more
diversiﬁed appeal in the resulting glyphs.
These considerations guided the development of the next Evotype
iteration that is presented in the following Chapter.

6
E V O T Y P E I I : E V O LV I N G G LY P H S W I T H S H A P E S
This Chapter contains another approach to the automated evolution
of glyph designs. We present the second iteration of Evotype that
evolves glyph designs built from shapes provided by the user. We test
three automated ﬁtness functions to guide the evolutionary process.
Figure 6.1 shows typical glyphs evolved with the system.
Figure 6.1: Alphabet generated with Evotype.
6.1 framework
For this iteration of Evotype, we use a GA to evolve different popula-
tions of glyphs in parallel, one for each target character. The design
approach explored by this system is inspired by the idea of combining
visual elements, such as vector shapes, to build letters. We took the
typeface Found Font by the British artist and designer Paul Elliman
as reference (see Figure 6.2). This typeface consists of an ongoing
collection of typography made from found objects [ 71].
Figure 6.2: Found Font by Paul Elliman, 1989–ongoing.
77

78 evotype ii : evolving glyphs with shapes
6.1.1 Representation
Each evolved glyph is a combination of shapes that are positioned on
a rectangular grid, rotated, scaled, and/or mirrored. We encode each
individual using a set of genes, each one representing a shape and the
corresponding attributes. This way, each gene consists of a vector of six
values: the index of the shape in the list of input shapes, x-coordinate
of the centre point, y-coordinate of the centre point, angle, scale and
whether or not the shape is mirrored (see Figure 6.3).
Each glyph is rendered by drawing the shapes encoded in its geno-
type, ﬁlled in black, on a white canvas with size deﬁned by a rectangu-
lar grid that is used to constrain the coordinates of the shapes. Since
the shapes are drawn on top of each other, all in black, their order in
the genotype is irrelevant to the resulting glyph.
{ ( E,X,Y,A,S,M ), (E,X,Y,A,S,M ), ... (E,X,Y,A,S,M ) }
Gene 1 Gene 2 Gene N 
Figure 6.3: Genotype structure containing a set of genes, each one encoding
the attributes of a shape: index of the shape ( E), x-coordinate (X),
y-coordinate ( Y), angle ( A), scale ( S), and whether the shape is
mirrored or not ( M).
6.1.2 Variation
For the recombination of glyphs, a uniform crossover operator is
used. This way, two offspring are generated by sequentially copying
genes from one of the two parents with the same probability. Since
the genotypes of the parents can have different lengths, the crossover
operator shufﬂes the genes inside each genotype before proceeding to
recombination.
The mutation of a glyph involves three procedures: deletion, re-
placement and insertion of genes. Each procedure can occur separately
with a certain probability. Therefore, the mutation operator can start
by deleting a gene selected at random from the genotype. Then, it
can proceed with the replacement of genes. Lastly, it can insert a new
random gene. The deletion and insertion of genes cause the variation
of the number of genes encoded in the genotype, thus allowing the
evolution of glyphs with a different number of shapes. The mutation
operator ensures each genotype remains valid after mutation. A geno-
type is considered valid if (i) the number of genes and the values of
the encoded attributes remain within preset ranges; (ii) all genes are

6.1 framework 79
different; and (iii) the shapes represented by the genes are located
inside the bounds of the grid.
6.1.3 Evaluation
We use an automated ﬁtness assignment approach to guide the evo-
lutionary process. We test automated ﬁtness functions that were de-
signed to promote the exploration of different compromises between
legibility and visual expressiveness of the evolved glyphs.
root mean square error The ﬁrst ﬁtness function evaluates
a candidate glyph based on its pixel-based similarity to a preset
target glyph. The similarity between two glyphs is calculated using
a Root-Mean-Square Error ( RMSE) method that measures how close
the candidate glyph is to the reference glyph on a pixel-by-pixel basis.
Thus, for a candidate image c and a target image t, we have:
RMSE(c, t) =
√
∑s
i=1(ci− ti)2
s ,
where s is the size, i.e. the number of pixels, of images c or t, which
must be equal. To guide evolution, we use the following ﬁtness func-
tion that is suitable for maximisation and employs a logarithmic
scaling operation:
ﬁtRMSE(c, t) = log2
(
1 + 1
1 + RMSE(c, t)
)
convolutional neural network The second ﬁtness function
uses CNN classiﬁers for character recognition. A CNN is a type of
Deep Neural Network (DNN) that has been successfully used in image
classiﬁcation and recognition tasks [ 148]. The main characteristic of
a CNN is the use of convolutional and pooling layers, which provide
feature extraction and dimensionality reduction in training [ 81]. Each
layer can be seen as a ﬁlter from which features are extracted and
learnt. In recent years, CNNs have consistently obtained good results
in several supervised image related classiﬁcation tasks [ 143, 272].
We develop and train CNN classiﬁers to recognise each target charac-
ter and use their output to assign ﬁtness. The output of each classiﬁer
indicates its conﬁdence in recognising a given input image as its de-
sired character. An output of 1 indicates total conﬁdence while an
output of 0 indicates the opposite. As such, to calculate the ﬁtness of

80 evotype ii : evolving glyphs with shapes
a candidate image c, we use the following ﬁtness function which also
includes a logarithmic scaling operation:
ﬁtCNN(c) = log2
(
1 + CNN(c)
)
hybrid The third ﬁtness function is a weighted sum of the values
returned by the previously described ﬁtness functions. The hybrid
ﬁtness function becomes:
ﬁtHybrid(c) = ﬁtRMSE(c, t)× wRMSE + ﬁtCNN(c)× wCNN ,
where wRMSE and wCNN specify the weights given to each component.
The theoretical optimum is a glyph that simultaneously maximises
ﬁtRMSE and ﬁtCNN. However, this may be impossible and, in those
circumstances, we want evolution to ﬁnd compromises between these
two components, avoiding solutions that focus only on one of the
ﬁtness measures. The inclusion of the logarithmic scaling operation is
intended to address this issue. This approach promotes the increasing
of ﬁtness through the simultaneous improvement of both criteria.
6.1.4 Implementation
The system, which is implemented in Java, takes as input a SVG ﬁle
containing a set of shapes that are used as the building blocks for the
glyphs. The user interface is designed to be simple and to provide the
user with fundamental features to import shapes, evolve glyphs, and
export evolved glyphs to vector ﬁles (see Figure 6.4). While glyphs
are being evolved, the user can browse throughout all the alternative
designs and even write a text composed with them. This allows the
user to test the evolved glyphs and assess their functionality. A demo
video can be seen at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype2_demo.
mov.
6.2 experimentation
We perform experiments on the proposed system with three major
goals in mind. First, we assess if, and to what extent, the system can
generate glyphs for different characters using a given set of shapes.
Second, we investigate the impact of the use of different ﬁtness func-
tions on the quality of the resulting glyphs. Finally, we analyse the
variety of glyphs that can be generated.

6.2 experimentation 81

82 evotype ii : evolving glyphs with shapes
As classiﬁer for ﬁtCNN, we train CNNs based on the AlexNet model
by Krizhevsky, Sutskever, and Hinton [ 143]. This model consists of
a CNN with ﬁve convolutional layers, with three max-pooling layers,
and three fully-connected layers with a ﬁnal 1000-way softmax. It is
known for attaining the best result on the competition ImageNet Large
Scale Visual Recognition Challenge 2012 and for its contribution to
the development and training of CNNs.
For training, we isolate each target character and train against
random images. The underlying idea is to create a general-purpose
character classiﬁer using CNNs that learn the features of each character
against other types of images and not only against the other characters,
as performed in the previous iteration of this work (see Chapter 5).
This way, we train 26 classiﬁers, one for each target letter, with two
classes: positive and negative. For the positive class dataset, we gather
a total of 537 fonts available from Google Fonts 1. For the negative
class dataset, we use random images from Flickr. The images of both
datasets are resized to 32-by-32 pixels and converted to greyscale. The
remaining relevant training parameters are presented in Table 6.2. For
each character, 200 instances of the positive and negative datasets are
randomly selected for training purposes. After training, we test the
networks against the remaining instances of the positive and negative
datasets: 337 and 3 871, respectively. The average accuracy obtained
for the training dataset is 0.998 and for the test dataset is 0.9975.
parameter setting
Solver stochastic gradient descent
Number of epochs 1 000
Learning rate 0.01
Learning rate policy drop 0.1 every 500 epochs
Momentum 0.9
Positive samples 200
Negative samples 200
Table 6.2: Experimental parameters of the classiﬁers.
For ﬁtHybrid, we use the same weights for both components. There-
fore, the value of wRMSE is 0.5 and the value of wCNN is also 0.5.

(see Figure 6.6b) and ﬁtHybrid (see Figure 6.6c). The ﬁtness values
that these glyphs would obtain with the remaining ﬁtness functions

are also calculated and visualised. This allows us to understand, for

affects ﬁtCNN and ﬁtHybrid.
Based on the line charts of the maximum ﬁtness over generations, we
can say the system is able to evolve glyphs that optimise each ﬁtness
function. The charts also reveal that maximising ﬁtCNN is easier than
maximising the other ﬁtness functions. Analysing the behaviour of
ﬁtCNN and ﬁtHybrid in the evolutionary runs guided by ﬁtRMSE
allows us to state that using ﬁtRMSE is sufﬁcient to attain, in the long
run, high values for all ﬁtness functions. The same can not be stated
for the runs where ﬁtCNN is used to guide evolution. In these runs,
the evolutionary process quickly ﬁnds glyphs that the CNN classiﬁes,
with certainty, as belonging to the desired class, which causes early
convergence. Although ﬁtRMSE and ﬁtHybrid attain relatively high
scores, they never reach their maximum values. Finally, as expected,
when ﬁtHybrid is used to guide evolution, high values for all ﬁtness
functions are obtained in a few generations, and, in the long run,
values close to their respective maximums are achieved.
In what concerns the ﬁtness of the best glyphs in the last genera-
tion, the differences among the evolutionary runs performed using

ﬁtRMSE and ﬁtHybrid are almost imperceptible for all ﬁtness functions.
However, differences can be perceived in terms of the dynamics of the

both in terms of dynamics and ﬁnal ﬁtness values between the runs
guided by ﬁtCNN and the remaining ﬁtness functions.
The different ﬁtness functions have an impact on the length of
the genotype, i.e. the number of shapes, of the evolved glyphs. In
Figure 6.7, we compare for each ﬁtness function how the length of
the genotype of the best glyph progresses over generations. We can
observe that ﬁtRMSE and ﬁtHybrid promote the evolution of glyphs
built from more shapes than ﬁtCNN. This is due to the need for
matching a given target glyph as precisely as possible when using
ﬁtRMSE or ﬁtHybrid. In contrast, glyphs evolved using ﬁtCNN only
need to be recognised as the desired character, meaning that they do
not necessarily require so many shapes.
We can also analyse the diversity of shapes used by the evolved
glyphs by calculating the percentage of input shapes, among those
provided by the user, that are used to build the best glyphs. As
Figure 6.8 shows, the evolutionary runs that use ﬁtCNN result in
glyphs using a less diversiﬁed set of shapes. The runs using ﬁtRMSE
result in glyphs with an intermediate degree of shape diversity. The
runs using ﬁtHybrid promote the use of a more diversiﬁed set of
shapes. A possible explanation for the observed differences follows.
When using ﬁtCNN, the evolutionary process is only required to
evolve glyphs recognised as the target character; there is no reason to
believe that such glyphs need to be composed of a varied set of shapes
since repetition, positioning, scaling and rotation of a single shape
is sufﬁcient to meet this requirement. When ﬁtRMSE is employed,

it becomes advantageous to use a more diversiﬁed set of shapes to
precisely match the target glyph, since different parts of the target
image may require the use of different shapes. The use of ﬁtHybrid to
provide ﬁtness seems to result in glyphs that use a higher diversity
of shapes. The explanation may be related to the dynamics of the
evolutionary process. As we have previously mentioned, it is easier for
the evolutionary process to improve ﬁtCNN than ﬁtRMSE. Therefore,
in the early generations, the best glyphs tend to be the ones that
optimise ﬁtCNN. In the following generations, as ﬁtHybrid also takes
ﬁtRMSE into account, more shapes gradually make their way into
the genotype, thus increasing the diversity of the shapes used in the
construction of the glyphs. In other words, when using ﬁtHybrid to
guide evolution, the component of ﬁtCNN promotes the evolution of
the skeleton in the glyphs, while the component of ﬁtRMSE promotes
the inclusion of visual detail.
When comparing the typical visual results obtained with the dif-
ferent ﬁtness functions, the difference is noticeable (Figure 6.9). The
visual appearance of glyphs evolved with ﬁtRMSE emphasises the
idea that the evolutionary process is trying to use and place as many
shapes as needed to match the target glyph and, this way, maximise
ﬁtness. In contrast, glyphs evolved with ﬁtCNN are more schematic.
They use fewer shapes and some glyphs are incomplete, i.e. some
parts are missing. Glyphs evolved with ﬁtHybrid exhibit a compromise
between the abstraction favoured by ﬁtCNN and the glyphs’ com-
pleteness promoted by the ﬁtRMSE. These considerations about the
visual appearance of the evolved glyphs are consistent with the re-
sults obtained in terms of ﬁtness, genotype length and shape diversity
discussed earlier.

The system can create varied glyph designs for the same character.
Figure 6.10 shows the visual diversity of typical glyphs evolved for
letter E in different evolutionary runs using each ﬁtness function. We
can see how each ﬁtness function inﬂuences the diversity of the result-
ing glyphs. In the ﬁtRMSE, although a target glyph is used, evolution
is able to attain visual variability by using different compositions of
shapes to construct the glyphs. On the other hand, the glyphs obtained
using the ﬁtCNN tend to repeat the same shapes.
