6.3 summary 89
During experimentation, we also tested the evolution of glyphs
using other sets of input shapes and parameters. In Figure 6.12, we
show some of the alphabets we obtained. They demonstrate the wide
range of typefaces that can be created with the system, each one
with its own visual style and identity. These alphabets also show
that using the same set of shapes to construct different glyphs can
provide visual coherence. More visual results can be visualised at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype2_runs.mov.
6.3 summary
The experimental results demonstrate the ability of the system to
create a wide variety of glyphs that push the boundaries between
expressiveness and legibility. The system only needs input shapes to
automatically build glyphs from them. We consider that this approach
may aid users to get unexpected designs, in a short period of time,
and may encourage experimentation in the design of typefaces. By
allowing the input of any set of shapes to build the glyphs, the user
may personalise and tweak the outcome by creating different sets of
shapes. Also, the evolved glyphs can be exported to most common
design software tools, facilitating their reﬁnement and application in
typographic compositions (see Figure 6.13).
Figure 6.13: Text composed with evolved glyphs.
Glyphs evolved with this system can be suitable for design projects
that take advantage of typefaces or letterings capable of conveying a
certain concept or idea. This feature can be useful, for example, in the
design of posters, to draw attention while communicating messages,
and in the creation of logotypes, where having a distinct typeface
can potentiate their differentiation and recognisability. The presented
system has also a potential for open-ended applications, allowing, for
example, the on-demand generation of personalised typefaces.

90 evotype ii : evolving glyphs with shapes
We think one of the main limitations of the presented system is
the lack of a common visual structure, or “skeleton”, that relates the
evolved glyphs. It could be interesting to generate glyphs that share
anatomical parts among them. This way, for example, the glyphs for
the letter E could use parts of the glyphs for the letter F. This ambition
led us to the development of the third iteration of Evotype, which is
described in the following Chapter.

7
E V O T Y P E I I I : E V O LV I N G T Y P E S T E N C I L S
This Chapter presents the third iteration ofEvotype. In this iteration, we
evolve stencils capable of generating legible and coherent glyphs for
different characters. We perform experiments in terms of evaluation
and decoration of the evolved stencils. Figure 7.1 shows a typical
stencil evolved with the system along with letters drawn using the
line segments of that stencil.
Figure 7.1: Stencil generated with Evotype.
7.1 framework
The approach is based on the idea of a stencil that allows the creation
of every letter of the alphabet in a coherent manner. In 1876, the
American engineer Joseph A. David developed the stencil Plaque
Découpée Universelle (see Figure 7.2). This stencil consists of a complex
grid that allows the construction of all uppercase and lowercase letters,
digits, punctuation, accents, among others [ 134]. A few decades later,
the seven-segment display was invented, employing a similar approach
as the Plaque Découpée Universelle by lighting its segments in different
combinations to represent letters and digits (see Figure 7.3).
These stencils can be seen as design systems that use patterns, or
structures, to establish where lines can be drawn. We consider that
stencils can be suitable to handle the typical repetition of structural
parts among glyphs of the same typeface. This way, some glyphs are
created using parts of others. For example, a glyph for the letter P can
be used as a basis for the design of glyphs for the letters R and B.
91

92 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
Figure 7.2: Plaque Découpée Universelle, Joseph A. David, 1876
Figure 7.3: All 128 symbols that a seven-segment display can show.
We use a GA to evolve a population of stencils. Each stencil allows
drawing glyphs for different characters, resulting in glyphs that share
common parts.
7.1.1 Representation
Each evolved stencil consists of an arrangement of two-dimensional
line segments, which can be used to draw different glyphs. Therefore,
each gene contained in the genotype of a stencil encodes a line segment.
Each gene consists of a 4-tuple with values corresponding to the
coordinates of the endpoints of the line segment: ( x1, y1, x2, y2). The
position of each endpoint is constrained by a square grid with a given
density. Since different stencils can have a different number of line
segments, the size of the genotype, i.e. number of genes, may vary
from stencil to stencil.
7.1.2 Variation
In this work, the crossover operator exchanges line segments between
two stencils. We have performed some preliminary experiments to
study the impact of different crossover operators on the evolution of
stencils. We ended up choosing the area-based crossover operator used
in the ﬁrst iteration of Evotype, which uses a randomly selected area
in the phenotype to determine which genes are exchanged between

7.1 framework 93
the parents (see Subsection 5.1.2). Analysing the results of these exper-
iments, we observed, for example, that the one-point crossover tended
to produce stencils with similar elements positioned over the same
areas of the phenotypes. This was not observed using the area-based
crossover operator, which avoided this accumulation of line elements
in the phenotype. This is perhaps because this crossover operator
exchanges line segments between stencils according to their position
on the phenotype regardless of their position in the genotype. Note
that the order of the genes in the genotype is irrelevant when express-
ing it into a phenotype. This way, we concluded that the area-based
crossover operator performs better in this problem and, for this reason,
we adopted it in our system.
The area-based crossover operator proceeds as follows: (i) select a
random rectangular area of the phenotype; (ii) determine, for each
parent stencil, the line elements whose middle points are inside the
selected area; and (iii) exchange those line elements between the
parents. This crossover may be asymmetric as the number of genes
it moves from the ﬁrst parent to the second may be different from
the number of genes it moves from the second parent to the ﬁrst.
This results in new stencils with a different number of elements in
comparison with their parents.
Mutating an evolving stencil involves random modiﬁcation of some
parts of its genotype. The mutation operator comprises three proce-
dures: deletion, modiﬁcation, and insertion of genes. Each can occur
independently with preset probabilities. The deletion procedure selects
a gene at random and removes it from the genotype. The modiﬁcation
procedure changes one of the endpoints of one or more line segments
by moving it by the minimum translation in the grid in one of the
eight possible directions. Finally, the insertion procedure inserts a new
randomly generated gene into the genotype.
The deletion and insertion procedures cause the variation of the
number of genes, thus allowing the evolution of stencils with a differ-
ent number of elements. Also, we implement a mechanism that boosts
the mutation rate when the individuals in the population are too simi-
lar. This mechanism promotes diversity throughout the evolutionary
process and prevents stagnation of the evolution.
The crossover and mutation operators preserve the validity of the
stencils and the integrity of their line segments. We consider a stencil
valid if: (i) all its line segments are different; (ii) all lines segments are
located inside the limits of the grid; (iii) the number of line segments
remains within preset ranges; (iv) all line segments have no null length;
and (v) no line segment contains, or overlaps, another line.

94 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
7.1.3 Evaluation
To assess the ﬁtness of a stencil, one must take into consideration the
adequacy of the glyphs it allows us to generate. The evaluation of
each stencil takes a couple of steps. As already mentioned, each stencil
has a set of line segments that can be activated (drawn, in black, on a
white canvas) to express glyphs. First, the system chooses a character
for which the stencil has to produce glyphs. Next, among the line
segments of the stencil, we search which combination of segments
should be activated to better express glyphs for the chosen character.
We use binary masks to store which line segments of the stencils are
activated (1), or not (0), to generate a given glyph (see Figure 7.4).
5
3 4
21 21
5
3 4
00111 11000
Figure 7.4: Expression process of a stencil (centre) to two glyphs (left and
right) according to two binary masks that specify which line
segments should be activated, or drawn.
We use a hill-climbing algorithm to search the binary mask, i.e. com-
bination of line segments, that results in the best glyph for each target
character. This search begins with all line segments deactivated. Then,
in each search step, the neighbouring binary masks are calculated by
toggling each line segment. All newly generated masks are evaluated
as a glyph for the target character. The search stops when no improve-
ment in terms of evaluation is achieved, storing the best mask and the
evaluation value of the resulting glyph. This process is repeated for
all the target characters.
Each glyph is evaluated based on its visual similarity with a preset
glyph of the target character. First, we render the stencil’s activated
line segments, and the target glyph, to an image representation. Then,
we compute the similarity between the two images through a pixel-
by-pixel comparison using an RMSE method. Since each stencil must
produce glyphs for different characters, its ﬁtness is based on the
following equation:
ﬁtBase(s) = ∑
p∈P,bs∈Bs
(
1− RMSE(bs, p)
)/
| P| , ( 7.1)
where s is the stencil to evaluate, bs is the image of the best glyph
found for the target character generated using stencil s, Bs is the set
of images of the best glyphs generated using stencil s, p is the preset

7.2 experimentation 95
glyph image of the target character, P is the set of preset glyph images,
and| P| is the cardinality of P. This function tends to 1 if the stencil
is able to generate glyphs similar to the target ones.
7.1.4 Implementation
We developed a simple graphical user interface with the basic function-
alities to evolve and export type stencils. Figure 7.5 shows a screenshot
of the system. While stencils are being evolved, the user can visualise
and inspect the results in different ways: (i) browse throughout all
stencils of the current generation, which are arranged vertically by
descending order of ﬁtness; (ii) visualise, for each character, the differ-
ent alternative glyphs drawn with the selected stencil; (iii) experiment
with the glyphs of the selected stencil by writing a text with them and,
this way, assess the resulting readability; and (iv) visualise a series of
measurements that characterise the results in different aspects, e.g. ﬁt-
ness of the stencils, ﬁtness of the glyphs, and the complexity of the
selected stencil. Furthermore, the user can export any evolved stencil
to make further reﬁnements and use it. A demo video of the system
can be seen at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype3_demo.mov.
Figure 7.5: Screenshot of the system.
7.2 experimentation
We performed a series of experiments to test and analyse the possibili-
ties created with the system. The experimental parameters used in the
experiments are summarised in Table 7.1. We focus on the evolution

96 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
of stencils to draw sans serif glyphs for the uppercase letters of the
Roman alphabet, starting from initial populations seeded with random
stencils.
In the ﬁrst experiment, we focus on the ability of the system to
evolve stencils capable of drawing legible glyphs. In the second ex-
periment, we add two components to the ﬁtness function to take into
consideration (i) the number of segments used by each evolved stencil
and (ii) the gaps between those segments. Then, in the third experi-
ment, we study the sharing of stencil elements among the resulting
glyphs. Finally, in the fourth experiment, we test the visual coher-
ence provided by the evolved stencils when replacing their segments
with different shapes. These four experiments are presented in the
following Subsections.
parameter setting
Number of generations 5 000
Population size 100
Elite size 1
Selection method tournament
Tournament size 3
Crossover rate 0.5
Delete gene mutation rate 0.05
Insert gene mutation rate 0.05
Change gene mutation rate 1 / genotype size
Glyph grid size 25 × 25
Max. number of genes 40
Phenotype size 32 × 32
Stroke weight 4
Target typeface Roboto Mono
Number of hill-climbers 1
Table 7.1: Experimental parameters.
7.2.1 Maximising Legibility
We begin our experiments by analysing the ability of the system to
evolve stencils that maximise the ﬁtness function ﬁtBase. In Figure 7.6,
we visualise the progression of the ﬁtness of the best stencil over
generations. As one can see, the system is able to guide evolution and
optimise the ﬁtness of the evolved stencils. Regarding the rate of ﬁtness
progression, high ﬁtness values are obtained in a few generations. In
the early generations of the evolutionary process, stencils tend to
contain elements that overlap the area of the target glyphs. After

the maximum number of elements is reached, only adjustments to
or replacements of existing elements can improve the ﬁtness of the
stencils. As a result, the ﬁtness values increase faster at the beginning
of the evolutionary process.
One can observe that the maximum ﬁtness values obtained (∼0.785)
do not reach the theoretical maximum value ( i.e., 1). This can be
explained by the following factors: (i) the stencils have to satisfy many
requirements speciﬁc to each target character, which is a difﬁcult
problem to solve; (ii) the maximum number of elements per stencil
may be insufﬁcient for an exact match and more elements are needed
to draw certain parts of the glyphs (see Figure 7.7); (iii) the positioning
resolution enabled by the grid is too low to allow some details of the
glyphs to be represented; and (iv) the use of just line segments makes
the rendering of the curved parts of some glyphs difﬁcult.

98 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
The alphabets shown on the top of Figure7.8 demonstrate the typical
results evolved in different runs. More results can be visualised at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype3_results.mov. The results
obtained show the ability of the system to evolve stencils that produce
glyphs similar to the targets. Nevertheless, the system can produce a
wide range of distinct stencils, thus generating glyphs with different
features. Also, one can observe the reuse of stencil elements among
the glyphs. This is essential as it creates coherence and unity among
the ﬁnal glyphs.
Analysing the evolved stencils (see top of Figure 7.8), it is possible
to see many gaps between the line segments that compose the ﬁnal
glyphs. Furthermore, it seems that the stencils strive for drawing
some parts of the target characters, including curves and other parts
with thickness different from the preset stroke weight of the stencil
elements. Regarding the appearance of the evolved stencils, most of
them use the maximum number of elements, resulting in complex
tangles of lines. Consequently, it is difﬁcult to identify which elements
of the stencil are used by each glyph.
7.2.2 Maximising Legibility and Simplicity
In this experiment, we approach two issues identiﬁed in the results
obtained in the previous experiment: (i) the high number of elements
that compose the evolved stencils and (ii) the several gaps that appear
between those elements. To do so, we use the ﬁtness function ﬁtSimple
to ﬁnd a compromise between the legibility provided by the evolved
stencils and their simplicity. With simplicity, we mean fewer elements
with fewer gaps. This ﬁtness function uses ﬁtBase as a component (see
equation 7.1) and is deﬁned by the following equation:
ﬁtSimple(s) = ﬁtBase(s)× reduceSize(s)× reduceGaps(s) , ( 7.2)
where s is the stencil to evaluate, reduceSize returns a value between
0.95 and 1 (values chosen empirically), inversely proportional to the
number of elements of s, and reduceGaps returns a value between 0.975
and 1 (values chosen empirically), directly proportional to the per-
centage of endpoints of elements that are contained in other elements.
In other words, the component reduceSize penalises stencils with a
high number of elements (up to 5% of penalisation) and reduceGaps
penalises stencils composed of elements that are not connected (up to
2.5% of penalisation).
The progression of the maximum ﬁtness values over generations is
visualised in Figure 7.6. The behaviour of the ﬁtness progression is

7.2 experimentation 99
Figure 7.8: Typical results evolved in different runs using the ﬁtness functions
ﬁtBase (top), ﬁtSimple (middle) and ﬁtSubset (bottom). To better
identify each element of the evolved stencils (left) in the resulting
glyphs (right), a random colour is used for each element.

100 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
similar to the one observed when using the ﬁtness function ﬁtBase but
with a lower magnitude. When ﬁtSimple is guiding evolution, stencils
do not achieve ﬁtness values as high as when using ﬁtBase. This is
related to the pressure that reduceSize and reduceGaps have in the eval-
uation of the stencils. With ﬁtSimple, stencils have to produce glyphs
similar to the targets (maximise the component ﬁtBase) while using
a low number of elements (maximise the component reduceSize) and
with minimal gaps between the elements (maximise the component
reduceGaps).
Figure 7.7 compares, for ﬁtBase and ﬁtSimple, the progression of the
number of elements used by the best stencils over generations. The
progression rates regarding the number of elements and ﬁtness are
similar. However, the difference in the number of elements used in the
two approaches is clear. On average, when ﬁtSimple is guiding evolu-
tion, stencils use roughly two-thirds of the elements in comparison
with when ﬁtBase is guiding (26.8 versus 39, respectively).
With ﬁtSimple guiding evolution, the system continues to generate
stencils that are able to produce glyphs similar to the targets (see
Figure 7.8). However, it is noticeable the differences at the visual level
in comparison with the results obtained when using ﬁtBase. The result-
ing glyphs are built with a reduced number of elements. Regarding
the gaps, the visual results demonstrate the capacity of ﬁtSimple in
guiding evolution towards stencils with elements whose endpoints are
contained in other elements, creating connections between them. This
is reﬂected in the appearance of the stencils, which are more clean and
elementary than the ones evolved using ﬁtBase. As a result, it is easier
to identify which elements of the stencil are used by each glyph.
The simplicity of the glyphs produced in this experiment makes the
reuse of the stencil elements among the glyphs more noticeable. How-
ever, the reduced number of elements forces the evolutionary process
to ﬁnd a compromise between the abstraction and the completeness
of the ﬁnal glyphs, This way, some glyphs can have missing parts.
In most of these cases, we notice that the missing parts are typically
speciﬁc to one glyph.
7.2.3 Testing the Reuse of Stencils’ Elements
In this experiment, we further investigate one feature we consider
fundamental in the evolved stencils: the reuse of elements to draw
different glyphs. We begin by visualising data on this matter. Figure7.9
shows the average number of elements shared by the best glyphs
produced with stencils evolved using ﬁtSimple. The visualised data

7.2 experimentation 101
Figure 7.9: Number of elements shared by the different glyphs. The visu-
alised data correspond to the average of the best stencils evolved
in 30 runs using ﬁtSimple, which on average are composed of
26.8 elements. Each value is rounded to its closest integer.
demonstrates that the stencils its elements to draw the different glyphs,
predominantly the ones that are visually similar, e.g. GO, GQ, OQ, PR, BD,
BH, CG, CO, CQ, CS, DO and GS.
Based on this data, we set one last evolutionary experiment: evolve
stencils to draw all glyphs but with only a subset being evaluated by
the ﬁtness function. This way, we designed the ﬁtness functionﬁtSubset,
which is based on ﬁtSimple (see equation 7.2), to guide evolution in
this experiment. The difference to ﬁtSimple is that instead of using the
evaluation of all glyphs from the set G (see equation 7.1), it uses a
subset L, where L⊆ G, L ={B, I, Q, V, W, X}. We resorted to the data
visualised in Figure 7.9 to test different combinations of glyphs for the
subset L until we got a small set that could provide most parts needed
to draw all glyphs from the set G, i.e. all uppercase letters.
The progression of the average ﬁtness of the glyphs that are being
evaluated by the ﬁtness function, along with the progression of the
average ﬁtness of the remaining glyphs, is visualised in Figure 7.10.
One can see that ﬁtSubset is able to improve the average ﬁtness of

7.2 experimentation 103
all glyphs although only a small subset is considered by the ﬁtness
function. This demonstrates that stencils evolved to draw only a few
glyphs can be used to draw other glyphs.
Typical stencils evolved in different runs are shown in Figure 7.8.
As one can see, the resulting glyphs use few elements and are quite
simple. Furthermore, most glyphs are recognisable. It is evident the
reuse of elements among the glyphs. The number of elements used
by the evolved stencils stabilises at 23 (see Figure 7.11), thus being
more compact than the ones evolved using ﬁtBase (39 elements) and
ﬁtSimple (26.8 elements).
7.2.4 Replacing Stencils’ Elements
After evolving stencils, we now explore their decoration. In this ex-
periment, we replace the elements that compose evolved stencils with
custom shapes. This way, we can test graphic changes in the sten-
cils that automatically propagate to the resulting glyphs. We asked
two graphic designers to create their own shapes to embody the line
segments of an evolved stencil. This is achieved using a Processing
program created speciﬁcally for this purpose (see Figure 7.12). It takes
as input a stencil and renders its line segments using parametric
shapes coded by the designers. They can choose which shape replaces
each stencil element, opt for a random conﬁguration, etc. The program
assembles the ﬁnal glyphs in real-time, so the user can quickly preview
what sort of effect any change in the stencil elements has on the ﬁnal
glyphs.
The designers explored a wide range of shapes and strokes to give
body to elements of the stencil and consequently to the ﬁnal glyphs
Figure 7.12: Screenshots of the Processing programs created by the graphic
designers who experimented with the decoration of an evolved
stencil.

104 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
Figure 7.13: Glyphs for letter R created by replacing the elements of an
evolved stencil with different parametric shapes.
(see Figure 7.13). Some elements are drawn with dots, squares, lines,
circles, or dashes; others consist of solid shapes or repetitions of
different shapes with random sizes. When randomness is employed
in the parametric design of the shapes, the approach can generate
ever-changing glyphs. Yet, the stencil is able to maintain coherence
among the glyphs, which exhibit a similar style (see Figure 7.14). More
visual results can be visualised at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/
evotype3_results.mov.
The resulting glyphs reveal that, despite the rigidity of the evolved
stencils, there is space for visual creativity and experimentation. That
is, although the skeleton of the stencil is preserved to ensure the
legibility of the glyphs, this same skeleton can be embodied using
a wide variety of visual styles and shapes. This allows the creation
of new kinds of glyph designs that feature function (legibility) and
form (expressiveness). The possibilities enabled by this approach are
vast. One can, for example, evolve a stencil and then render it using
different sets of shapes; or evolve different stencils and render them
using the same set of shapes. In both approaches, visual coherence
could still prevail among the ﬁnal glyphs. Also, there is the possibility
of evolving the shapes. This was already explored in the work of
Parente et al. [ 222].
7.3 summary
The presented system can automatically evolve type stencils to draw
legible and coherent glyphs for different characters. While in previous
iterations of Evotype we evolve glyphs separately as different individ-
uals, here we evolve a set of glyphs as a single individual, i.e. a stencil.
The creation of glyphs by activating some lines of the evolved stencils

7.3 summary 105
Figure 7.14: Text composed using an evolved stencil decorated with different
sets of parametric shapes.

106 evotype iii : evolving type stencils
seems to be a suitable approach to establish visual coherence and
unity among the resulting glyphs, which is a desirable outcome when
designing glyphs to form a new typeface.
The experimentation allowed us to verify the legibility of the glyphs
created with the evolved stencils, as well as the ability of the system
to generate stencils with different degrees of simplicity and detail.
Moreover, we demonstrated the ability of the stencils to reuse their
elements to express different glyphs. Therefore, these stencils reveal
and exploit the redundancy of the parts that generally form letters
and other characters. We went beyond the experimentation of the
system. We explored the rendering, or embodying, of the lines of the
evolved stencils using custom parametric shapes created by graphic
designers. This way, we were able to provide different visual styles to
the resulting glyphs while maintaining their unity.
We identify varied applications for the obtained results. The evolved
stencils, along with their glyphs, can be used to produce bespoke
typefaces featuring unusual designs that can help in the creation of
eye-catching letterings, e.g. headlines for books, magazines or adver-
tisements. Similarly, the evolved glyphs can be suitable to develop
strong visual identities through the design of unique logotypes (see,
e.g., the visual identity for the graphic arts festival Pick Me Up 2016,
designed by the design agency Hato [ 111], and the visual identity for
the model agency Tess Management, designed by Mind Design [ 203]
and Simon Egli). The evolved stencils can also be used as a master grid
to design other visual elements, including signage, icons, pictograms
and other types of symbols. This approach could ensure the visual
coherence between those designs and the glyphs found during the
evolution of the stencil.
Although rigid, the skeleton that deﬁnes each evolved stencil can
provide valuable ﬂexibility. For example, we can generate, with little
effort, extended and condensed variations of the glyphs by applying
transformations to their stencil or create light and bold styles of the
glyphs by varying the thickness of their lines. This has the potential
to enable ﬂexible and responsive typography that can adapt to better
present the content to the reader. Besides, we could explore data-
driven design methods to implement glyphs that automatically react
to external data. That is, we could customise the glyphs according to
input data, e.g. sound, movement, light, temperature or interaction,
by modifying the parameters of the process that generates the glyphs
from an evolved stencil. This approach can add some reactiveness to
the letters as they reﬂect, in real-time, external conditions. This feature
can be suitable for design projects and other applications that can take

7.3 summary 107
advantage of open-ended designs. Finally, we consider that the results
can be instructive by allowing a deeper understanding of the anatomy
of letters and the way each type element relates to each other.
We think there is space for further investigation on this stencil-
based approach to evolving type designs, e.g. in terms of genetic
representation and ﬁtness assignment. Regarding representation, we
observed that the approach struggles to represent some parts of the
target characters, including curves and other parts with a different
thickness than the preset stroke weight for the stencil elements. One
possible solution for this issue can be the integration of other graphic
primitives in the stencils, e.g. curves, and the encoding of the stroke
weight of each element in the genotype of the stencil.
In what concerns ﬁtness, a possible idea to explore is to use EC to
ﬁnd the smaller set of glyphs evaluated by the ﬁtness function that
allows the evolution of stencils capable of drawing all glyphs properly
(see Subsection 7.2.3). On top of this, we could evolve stencils using a
ﬁtness function based on the smaller set of glyphs found previously
and then, as evolution goes on, it begins to consider more and more
glyphs. This way, the ﬁtness function would become more demanding
with the progress of the evolutionary process, thus evolving stencils
that are gradually more complete and capable of creating more glyphs.
Also on the subject of ﬁtness assignment, we think the presented sys-
tem can beneﬁt from further investigation with the goals of (i) creating
more diversity among the evolved stencils and (ii) guiding evolution
towards solutions that meet preferences and requirements speciﬁed
by the user. This way, the user could have more control over the evolu-
tionary process and go beyond the ﬁnal modiﬁcation and stylisation
of the outputs.
These considerations led us to the fourth, and ﬁnal, iteration of
Evotype that is presented in the next Chapter.

8
E V O T Y P E I V: D E S I G N I N G F I T N E S S F U N C T I O N S
In this Chapter, we present the fourth, and last, iteration of Evotype
where we further develop our stencil-based approach to evolve glyph
designs. In this iteration, we allow the users to design ﬁtness functions
and use them to automatically guide the evolution of stencils with cer-
tain preferred features. The ﬁtness functions can measure behavioural
features, which are related to the ability of the stencil to create legible
glyphs, and morphological features, which are related to the structure
and the composition of the stencil. We perform experiments with
different ﬁtness functions and test a novelty search approach designed
to increase the diversity of evolved stencils. In the end, we apply the
evolved stencils in the generation of typographic portraits. A typi-
cal stencil evolved in this work is presented in Figure 8.1. Note the
presence of curves in the evolved stencil.
Figure 8.1: Stencil generated with Evotype.
8.1 framework
Similarly to the previous iteration of Evotype, we use a GA to evolve a
population of type stencils capable of generating every letter of the
alphabet. The system is schematically overviewed in Figure 8.2 and
behaves as follows. The evolutionary process begins with the initiali-
sation of the population with randomly created stencils. The ﬁtness of
each stencil is calculated according to a ﬁtness function designed by
the user. With the stencils evaluated, a new generation of stencils is
created using an elitist strategy, i.e. a preset number of ﬁttest stencils
proceed unchanged. This step does not affect the ﬁrst generation. Af-

Figure 8.2: Framework overview.
ter the population is evaluated, stencils above a preset threshold are
considered feasible stencils. The feasible stencils are compared with
the stencils on the archive and if they are dissimilar to the existing
stencils they are added to the archive. A stop criterion is tested to
determine whether the evolution proceeds or stops. If the evolution
continues, the system determines based on the number of feasible
stencils whether novelty search is adopted, determining the criteria
for stencils’ selection. If novelty search is adopted, the tournament is
based on the ﬁtness and novelty of the individuals of the population.
If novelty search is not adopted, stencils are selected by tournament
based on their ﬁtness. Variation operators, i.e. crossover and mutation,
are applied to the stencils selected as parents to generate offspring
stencils. The offspring stencils are evaluated and a new generation
is again formed. The whole evolutionary process is repeated until
the stop criterion is satisﬁed. The following Subsections detail some
mechanisms of the system.

8.1 framework 111
8.1.1 Representation
Each evolved stencil is a composition of two-dimensional line seg-
ments and Bézier curves with varying thicknesses. Therefore, we
implemented an encoding that enables the representation of both
types of lines. Each gene consists of a 9-tuple (X1, Y1, X2, Y2, A1, A2, L1,
L2, T) representing the attributes of a line, respectively, the coordinates
of the two endpoints, the angles of the two control points, the lengths
of the two control points, and the thickness value (see Figure 8.3).
Genes with angles and/or lengths of the control points set to zero
represent straight lines. The position of the endpoints is constrained
by a square grid with a preset density.

(X2,Y2)

Figure 8.3: Line encoding.
The expression process of each genotype into its phenotype consists
in the drawing of black lines, encoded in the genotype, on a white
canvas. This process uses binary masks, one for each target glyph, to
deﬁne which lines of the stencil are used to generate each glyph (see
Figure 8.4).

Figure 8.4: Expression process of a stencil (top) to two glyphs (bottom) ac-
cording to two binary masks that specify which line segments
should be activated, or drawn.

112 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
8.1.2 Variation
For the initial population, we create random stencils with a random
number of lines. We perform variation operations on the stencils
using crossover and mutation. For crossover, we use the area-based
crossover operator employed in the ﬁrst iteration of Evotype, which
uses a randomly selected area in the phenotype to determine which
genes are exchanged between the parents (see Subsection 5.1.2).
The mutation of a stencil involves three procedures: deletion, modi-
ﬁcation, and insertion. Each mutation procedure can occur indepen-
dently with preset probabilities. The deletion procedure selects a line
at random and removes it from the stencil. The modiﬁcation proce-
dure changes one or more lines of the stencil by performing one of the
following options, each with a preset probability: (i) moving one of the
endpoints by the minimum translation in the grid in one of the eight
possible directions; (ii) varying the angle of one of the control points;
(iii) varying the length of one of the control points; or (iv) varying
the thickness value. Finally, the insertion procedure inserts a new
randomly generated line into the stencil. The deletion and insertion
procedures cause the variation of the number of lines in the evolved
stencils.
The crossover and mutation operators preserve the validity of the
stencils. A stencil is considered valid if: (i) all lines are located inside
the limits of the grid; (ii) all lines have a length equal to or greater
than a preset minimum length; (iii) all lines are different; (iv) no line
contains another one; and (v) the number of lines remains within a
preset range.
8.1.3 Evaluation
We implement an automated ﬁtness function to evaluate the stencils
and, this way, automatically guide the evolutionary process. The eval-
uation of each stencil is based on the measurement of (i) behavioural
features, which are related to how the stencil performs in drawing
glyphs for the target characters, and (ii) morphological features, which
are related to the structure and components of the stencil. We con-
ceived the ﬁtness assignment to enable any combination of features
to be pursued by the evolutionary process. Furthermore, in a combi-
nation of features, each feature can have more or less importance, or

8.1 framework 113
weight, in comparison to the other features. As a result, the ﬁtness
function consists of a weighted product:
ﬁtness(s) =
n
∏

(
wi× (satisfactioni(s)− 1) + 1
)
, ( 8.1)
where s is the stencil to evaluate, wi is the weight of the feature i,
and satisfactioni is deﬁned by the following equation:
satisfactioni(s) = 1−| featurei(s)− ti| , ( 8.2)
where featurei is the value measured for the feature i, and ti is the
desired target value for the feature i. The evolutionary process aims at
maximising the value of equation 8.1, whose theoretical optimum cor-
responds to a stencil that simultaneously matches all features to their
target values according to equation 8.2. All features and weights are
normalised to the [0, 1] interval. Therefore, maximising or minimising
a given feature consists in setting its target value to 1 or 0, respectively.
Below, we overview all the behavioural and morphological features
that can be measured by the ﬁtness function.
8.1.3.1 Behavioural Features
One of the requirements for the evolved stencils is their ability to
create glyphs that are legible. The system evaluates this ability by
measuring features based on the glyphs that each stencil can generate.
We refer to these features as behavioural features (see Table 8.1).
To ﬁnd the best glyphs that each evolved stencil can create, we
implement a hill-climbing algorithm to search the combination of
line segments that, when activated or drawn, better produces each
target glyph. Each combination of activated line segments is stored as a
binary mask. This process is detailed in Subsection 7.1.3. Each resulting
glyph is evaluated based on how it matches the target similarity
feature decription
Recognisability Character recognition using a CNN (conﬁdence value of the
classiﬁer in recognising the potential glyph as the target
character).
Similarity Visual similarity to existing glyphs using SOMs (RMSE pixel-
by-pixel similarity between the potential glyph and the most
similar neuron in the SOM of the target character).
Table 8.1: Behavioural features.

114 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
and recognisability values. In a typical evolutionary run, all target
characters are equally considered, i.e. the evolved stencils should be
able to draw glyphs for all characters. However, the user can specify
different importance levels for the target characters. This enables the
user, for example, to evolve stencils that can generate glyphs for a
subset of characters, or to improve the legibility of some glyphs of an
evolved stencil.
recognisability We use a CNN classiﬁer to calculate “how much”
a glyph is recognised as a given character. CNNs are a type of DNNs
that have been used successfully in image classiﬁcation and recogni-
tion tasks [ 143, 272]. The main characteristic of a CNN is the use of
convolutional and pooling layers, which provide feature extraction
and dimensionality reduction in training [ 81]. Each layer can be seen
as a ﬁlter from which features are extracted and learnt.
The architecture of the CNN is based on the Lenet-5 network for digits
recognition [148] but trained as a multi-class supervised classiﬁer for
the 26 uppercase letters of the Roman alphabet. Our approach needs
to perform several evaluations of several glyphs per generation, so the
chosen network was a trade-off between computational power and
efﬁciency. The classiﬁer is trained on the 32-by-32 pixel representation
of the typefaces served by Google Fonts. In addition to the typefaces,
we added a negative class represented with random images generated
by our approach that do not resemble any character, yielding a total
of 27 classes. The value of the recognisability feature of a stencil glyph
is the output of the classiﬁer, indicating its conﬁdence in recognising
the image of the glyph as its target character. An output of 1 indicates
total conﬁdence while an output of 0 indicates the opposite. Note that
the ML model used is based on data available and off-the-shelf architec-
tures with its inherited limitations and exploits [ 11, 176]. Furthermore,
the models employed are not able to harness the full potential of the
human-like visual system. With this in mind, this feature is used to
evaluate the recognisability of the glyph images.
similarity A series of SOMs, one for each target character, is used
to calculate the similarity feature. SOMs [140, 141] are among the most
well-known unsupervised learning and clustering approaches. The
architecture of a SOM consists of a feed-forward ANN that reduces
information while preserving the most important topological relation-
ships of the data elements. This enables the calculation of the visual
similarity of the glyphs generated by each stencil with existing glyphs.

8.1 framework 115
Each SOM is composed of 64 neurons and is trained with 32-by-32
pixels images of several glyphs of the corresponding target character.
The glyphs used for training were gathered from the typefaces of the
Google Fonts platform. To calculate the similarity feature of a stencil
glyph, we create an image representation of it, with the same size as
the SOM neurons, and then compare with each neuron of the target
character SOM. In this comparison, the similarity between the glyph
image and each neuron in the SOM is calculated using the RMSE, which
measures how close, or far, the candidate glyph is to a reference glyph,
which in this case is a neuron of the SOM, on a pixel-by-pixel basis.
Therefore, the value of the similarity feature consists of the normalised
distance between the glyph generated by the stencil and the most
similar neuron of the SOM, which is typically called the best matching
unit.
8.1.3.2 Morphological Features
In addition to the behavioural features, the system measures a series
of other features related to the structure and composition of the stencil.
We refer to these features as morphological features (see Table 8.2).
By employing morphological features, one can promote the evolu-
tion of stencils that exhibit particular structural characteristics. The
possibilities are vast. For instance, one can conﬁgure the morphologi-
cal features of the ﬁtness function to reward stencils with only curved
lines that intersect little, or horizontally symmetric stencils with only
straight lines with great continuity, or stencils with long curved lines
that intersect little.
The combination of morphological features with behavioural fea-
tures enables the evolution of stencils that match particular visual
characteristics while ensuring the legibility of the glyphs. This way,
the user can explore different compromises between the legibility and
style of the evolved glyphs.
8.1.4 Archive and Selection
Based on experimental results obtained in the previous Evotype itera-
tion (see Section 7.2), as the evolutionary process unfolds, the stencils
being evolved tend to converge towards a single solution, resulting
in similar stencils. The lack of diversity problem is not new in the
domain of arts and design and has been addressed, for example, by
novelty search algorithms (see, e.g., [158, 211]).
Similarly to the work of Vinhas et al. [ 293], we use an archive to
compare stencils during the evolutionary process and to prevent the

Table 8.2: Morphological features.
algorithm from searching areas of the search space that have already
been visited. Thus, the archive holds the set of diversiﬁed stencils
found during the evolutionary process. Therefore, the size of the
archive indicates the ability of the algorithm to generate diversiﬁed
stencils.
The archive comes into play after the ﬁtness assignment. At this
stage, a candidate stencil has its ﬁtness assigned and it has to meet
two requirements in order to be added to the archive: (i) its ﬁtness
must be greater than or equal to an adequacy threshold fmin and
(ii) it needs to surpass a dissimilarity threshold when compared to
the stencils contained in the archive. This process is performed by
computing the average dissimilarity between the candidate and a set
of k-nearest neighbours. When the average dissimilarity is above a
preset dissimilarity threshold, dissimmin, the individual is added to
the archive. We compare stencils based on the glyphs they generate,
i.e. the stencils are analysed in the form of images of their glyphs.

8.1 framework 117
Based on the works of Machado et al. [ 184] and Vinhas et al. [ 293], the
dissimilarity metric for an image i is computed as:
dissim(i) = 1
maxarch
maxarch
∑
j
dist(i, j) ,
where maxarch is the number of most similar images to consider when
comparing with image i, and dist(i, j) is a distance metric that mea-
sures how different the images i and j are. There are two exceptions to
the application of this measure. First, when the archive is empty, the
ﬁrst stencil with a ﬁtness value above the fmin is added to the archive.
Then, when the number of images in the archive is less than maxarch,
we use the number of existing images instead of maxarch.
The similarity between stencils is calculated using an image simi-
larity metric that is applied to the images of their glyphs. For each
stencil we want to compare, we create an image that concatenates all
its glyphs, in alphabetic order from left to right. Then, we can calcu-
late the similarity between candidate stencils and archived stencils
based on these images containing the stencils they generate. As for the
similarity metric, we use the RMSE between the pixels. Since we are
comparing a high number of images per generation, the fast calcula-
tion of RMSE is advantageous (see, e.g., [91, 297] for more information
on similarity and dissimilarity metrics).
When a stencil is added to the archive, it counts as a feasible so-
lution. The number of feasible solutions in each generation is used
to determine which criteria are used by the tournament selection
mechanism. We employ the novelty search approach proposed by
Vinhas et al. [ 293], which relies on a custom tournament selection
mechanism that can switch between a ﬁtness-guided strategy and a
hybrid strategy that considers both ﬁtness and novelty (see Figure 8.2).
The selection mechanism switches between ﬁtness-based selection
and hybrid selection according to the following decision rules: if the
number of feasible solutions in the current generation is lower than a
threshold tmin, it changes to ﬁtness-guided selection; or if the number
of feasible solutions in the current generation is above a threshold tmax,
it changes to hybrid selection.
In ﬁtness-guided selection, the tournament selection is based on
the ﬁtness values of the candidate solutions. In hybrid selection, it
is necessary to compute the novelty of each selected individual and
perform a Pareto-based tournament selection using the novelty and
ﬁtness of each individual as two different objectives to maximise.
The novelty computation process is inspired by the work of Lehman
and Stanley [ 151], with one small change: it considers the k most

118 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
similar images from the selected individuals and archive, instead of
considering the entire population and archive. At this stage, each
selected individual has its ﬁtness and novelty values calculated, and
there is the need to determine the winner of the tournament. We use
a Pareto-based approach to select the best individuals based on their
dominance or non-dominance when compared to the other individuals
in terms of both ﬁtness and novelty. After the set of non-dominant
individuals are computed, we have the so-called Pareto front. In the
hybrid selection mechanism, the tournament winner is selected by
randomly picking one of the solutions of the Pareto front.
8.1.5 Implementation
The framework is implemented in two modules: (i) the evolutionary
system (see Figure 8.5) and (ii) the ﬁtness function design interface (see
Figure 8.6). The ﬁrst module, the evolutionary system, operates the
GA guided by an automated ﬁtness function. The second module, the
ﬁtness function design interface, enables the user to adjust parameters
of the ﬁtness function and other options of the ﬁrst module. The video
at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype4_demo.mov demonstrates
the interaction with the framework to design ﬁtness functions to guide
the evolutionary system.
The ﬁtness function design interface communicates with the evo-
lutionary system through a JavaScript Object Notation ( JSON) ﬁle.
Technically, the user can manually adjust the parameters stored in this
ﬁle and, this way, use the evolutionary system alone to evolve stencils.
However, this could hinder the design of ﬁtness functions and the
conﬁguration of the evolutionary process.
A typical use of the framework could be described as follows. The
user launches the evolutionary system and selects the source of the
setup parameters: (i) from a setup ﬁle or (ii) from the ﬁtness function
design interface. When the ﬁrst source is selected, the user imports a
setup ﬁle stored in the computer, e.g. a setup ﬁle previously exported
using the ﬁtness function design interface. This approach is useful
to test a series of experimental setups. When the second source is
selected, the evolutionary system activates a mode in which it listens
for new parameters coming from the ﬁtness function design interface.
In this approach, the user uses the ﬁtness function design interface
to adjust the setup parameters, which are directly communicated to
the evolutionary system. This enables the user, for example, to modify
the ﬁtness function during the evolutionary process. After selecting
the source of the setup parameters, the user is in position to evolve

8.1 framework 119
Figure 8.5: Screenshot of the evolutionary system.
Figure 8.6: Screenshot of the ﬁtness function design interface.

120 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
stencils. Below, we overview some of the key functionalities of the two
modules.
8.1.5.1 Evolutionary System
The evolutionary module provides the necessary means to evolve,
browse, test, and export type stencils. After selecting the setup pa-
rameters, one can command the evolution of stencils by setting the
random seed and instructing the system to generate a given number
of generations. During evolution, it is possible to browse throughout
the current generation of stencils, which are arranged vertically by
descending order of ﬁtness. The system features a mode that renders
the elements of each stencil using different colours, either when pre-
viewing the entire stencil or the glyphs produced with it. The purpose
of this mode is to visualise the reuse of elements of the stencil between
the different glyphs. The user can select each stencil to (i) test it by
typing a couple of words with the glyphs produced with it; (ii) visu-
alise its features, or measurements, that are being considered by the
ﬁtness function; and (iii) export it as a vector graphics ﬁle, enabling
further reﬁnements and its utilisation outside the framework.
8.1.5.2 Fitness Function Design Interface
The ﬁtness function design interface enables the user to design ﬁtness
functions to automatically guide the evolution of type stencils. The
interface empowers the users by enabling them to express preferences
through the speciﬁcation of properties they intend to observe in the
evolved stencils. Although the main goal of the interface is the conﬁg-
uration of the ﬁtness function, it also enables the adjustment of several
parameters of the evolutionary process, including the population size,
elite size, tournament size, crossover rate, mutation rate, grid size,
phenotype size, and other parameters related to the novelty search
approach, such as the minimum ﬁtness for a stencil to be considered
feasible and the minimum dissimilarity to be added to the archive.
The ﬁtness function design interface consists of a web page with
multiple sliders and buttons that enables the user to adjust the evalua-
tion and evolution parameters in a high-level way. One could say this
module acts as an interactive facilitator for the parameterisation of the
ﬁrst module, the evolutionary system, abstracting the user from the
inner workings of the framework. This approach enables the user to
submit parameters to the evolutionary system at any time, as already
explained, and this way adjust user preferences throughout the gener-
ations. In addition to communicating parameters to the evolutionary

8.2 experimentation 121
system, the user can also export the parameters to a ﬁle and import
them later.
The ﬁtness parameters presented in the interface are arranged into
two groups that are visually distinguished using different colours. The
top group of parameters concerns the behaviour of the stencils, while
the bottom group is related to their morphology. The interface imple-
ments tooltips to help the user understand its different components,
e.g. the semantics associated with each feature and how it is calculated.
Therefore, when the user hovers the cursor over a component a tooltip
appears, displaying information about it.
For each parameter, the user can set: (i) the value that should be
matched by the evolved stencils and (ii) a weight that indicates the
importance of that parameter in the ﬁtness function. The only excep-
tion is the last parameter of the behaviour group, which presents an
array of vertical sliders to specify the relevance of each target charac-
ter for which the evolved stencils should be able to generate glyphs.
Changing the weight of one parameter results in having more or less
impact in comparison to the other parameters. In order to make the
adjustment of weights easier to understand, we adopt an approach
where the user indicates each weight by adding or subtracting units.
Nonetheless, the user can also set the weight to a speciﬁc ﬂoating value.
For instance, one parameter with a weight of 3 has an importance
three times greater than a parameter with a weight of 1. Following
this reasoning, setting the weight of one parameter to 0 means that it
will be ignored. One advantage of this approach is the precision it pro-
vides to the user when adjusting each weight, in comparison to other
approaches that employ, for example, sliders. The ﬁnal weight of each
parameter, considering the other weights, is displayed on the right
side. This information helps the user understanding the overall impact
of each parameter in the ﬁtness function. Also, by only displaying the
ﬁnal weights greater than zero, we are able to visually highlight the
parameters that have impact on the evaluation of the stencils.
8.2 experimentation
We conduct three experiments on the framework with different goals
in mind. In the ﬁrst experiment, we test hardwired ﬁtness functions
with different parameterisations for the behavioural features similarity
and recognisability. In the second experiment, we test ﬁtness functions
designed by the user, analysing the ability of the system to improve
ﬁtness and the ability of the evolved stencils to convey the preferences
expressed by the user. In the last experiment, we investigate the

8.2.1 Testing Hardwired Fitness Functions
In this experiment, we evolve stencils using three hardwired ﬁtness
functions with different parameterisations for the behavioural features,
i.e. recognisability and similarity. Based on experimental results ob-
tained in the previous Evotype iteration (see Subsection 7.2.2), we add
the morphological features size and continuity to the three ﬁtness
functions. The parameterisation of each ﬁtness function, i.e. the target
values and weights of the features, is presented in Table 8.4. We track
the values obtained for each feature that composes each ﬁtness func-
tion for analysis purposes, even if its weight is set to 0, i.e. it does not
count for the calculation of the ﬁtness.
The ﬁtRec function mostly uses the recognisability feature to assign
ﬁtness. Figure 8.7a summarises the progression of the ﬁtness and fea-
tures values when using this ﬁtness function to guide the evolutionary
process. We can see that the system is able to improve the ﬁtness of

the stencils. It starts with a relatively low ﬁtness value but rapidly
converges to high ﬁtness values in a few generations. If we analyse the
progression of the values of the other features, we can observe that the
similarity is not affected by the progression of the recognisability. The
size feature in the ﬁrst generations tends to rapidly increase, meaning
that elements are being removed from the evolved stencils. In the
end, the size feature achieves the highest value amongst the tested
functions, so the evolved stencils have fewer elements than the ones
evolved using the other ﬁtness functions. Regarding the continuity
feature, it consistently rises along the generations. Based on the con-
tinuity values at the end of the evolutionary process, ﬁtRec seems to
create stencils made of elements more disconnected when compared
with the ﬁtSim.
The ﬁtSim function mostly relies on the similarity feature to calculate
ﬁtness. As shown in Figure 8.7b, the evolutionary algorithm is able
to improve the stencils according to this ﬁtness function, although
it does not reach its maximum theoretical value. The value of the
recognisability feature tends to follow the increase of the similarity
feature. The size feature consistently increases, indicating that the
best stencil tends to lose elements over generations. The high values
of the continuity feature show that it creates stencils with connected
elements.
The ﬁtHybrid function combines both the similarity and the recog-
nisability features to assign ﬁtness. We assigned a higher weight to
the recognisability feature (see Table 8.4). The idea is to obtain stencils
that can produce expressive glyphs while preventing the system from
guide the evolutionary process towards recognisable but uncommon

can generate glyphs with visual coherence and legibility. The visual re-

at the visual level that support our analysis in terms of ﬁtness compo-
nents. The ﬁtSim function tends to generate stencils with elements that
are more connected. Although the SOM used to calculate the similarity
feature provides more ﬂexibility than the RMSE target approach used
in previous Evotype iterations, here, the glyphs of the evolved sten-

this ﬂexibility does not prevent glyphs of the same stencil to target

126 evotype iv : designing fitness functions

elements but these are more disconnected and dispersed. We see some
deviating lines in the generated glyphs that can be seen as exploits of
the classiﬁer that is used to calculate the recognisability feature. We
are aware of the tendency of evolutionary algorithms to ﬁnd shortcuts
and to exploit weaknesses on ﬁtness assignment approaches based
on ML. The ﬁtHybrid function tends to generate stencils with a small
number of connected elements, resulting in simpler glyphs with great
visual coherence. Figure 8.9 shows a sentence composed using glyphs
of a stencil evolved using the ﬁtHybrid ﬁtness function.
8.2.2 Testing Designed Fitness Functions
In this experiment, we analyse how the design of ﬁtness functions can
inﬂuence the outputs of the framework and if, and to what extent, they
are able to convey speciﬁc preferences. We focus on the morphological
features because these are easier to visually analyse both in the evolved
stencils and resulting glyphs. More speciﬁcally, we test features mostly
related to the curves of the evolved stencils, as the encoding of curves
was introduced in this Evotype iteration. Nevertheless, many different
combinations of features and parameterisations could be tested.
We designed and tested four ﬁtness functions to evolve stencils with
speciﬁc visual characteristics: ﬁtCurves for stencils entirely composed
of curves; ﬁtNoCurves for stencils with no curves; ﬁtSymCurves for
stencils with half of the elements being symmetrical curves; and
ﬁtSameLength for stencils with half of the elements being curves and
all lines with a length of two-thirds of the grid size. Each one of these
ﬁtness functions consists of ﬁtHybrid combined with more features.
These features, along with their target values and weights, are speciﬁed
in Table 8.5.

Overall, and based on the different runs of each ﬁtness function, the
results indicate that: (i) different ﬁtness functions lead to different
stencils; (ii) different runs with the same ﬁtness function converge to
different stencils, thus providing diverse stencils; and (iii) the four
designed ﬁtness functions are able to guide evolution towards stencils
with features that match the preferences that originated them.

The system can ﬁnd interesting ways to optimise the ﬁtness func-
tions, generating unusual glyphs with different levels of functionality.
Sometimes, the system generates stencils that, from the type design
point of view, can be appealing due to their novelty and aesthetics,
but which do not maximise all features of the ﬁtness function. In
other cases, the system evolves designs with high ﬁtness values that
are not so aesthetically appealing. Nevertheless, the results reveal
the effectiveness of the approach in exploring possibilities that are
consistent with the preferences or goals behind each designed ﬁtness
function. Looking at each stencil, and their glyphs, in Figure 8.10,
one can see that they exhibit visual characteristics that are aligned

8.2 experimentation 129
with the features added to each ﬁtness function. For instance, the
stencil evolved with ﬁtCurves function is mostly composed of curves
(only one line segment is used); on the other hand, the stencil evolved
with ﬁtNoCurves function is almost only composed of line segments
(only three curves are used); the stencil evolved with ﬁtSymCurves
function, in addition to using the same number of curves and line
segments, most of the curves used are symmetric (only one curve is
asymmetric); and the stencil evolved with ﬁtSameLength function has
the same balance of curves and line segments, all elements have more
or less the same length, with approximately two thirds the size of the
stencil grid.
Overall, the evolutionary process is able to optimise the morpho-
logical features without compromising the behavioural features. This
should be related to the substantial differences between the high
weights used for the behavioural features and the low weights used
for the morphological features.
8.2.3 Testing Novelty Search
In this experiment, we assess the ability of the novelty search mecha-
nism to generate diverse stencils in a single evolutionary run and the
adequacy of the archive, produced during the process, to summarise
the evolved solutions. The parameters of the novelty search mecha-
nism are presented in Table 8.6. To assign ﬁtness, we use the ﬁtHybrid
function from the ﬁrst experiment.
