8.3 application in typographic portraits
In this Section, stencils evolved with the system are applied in a design
project: an interactive installation that enables visitors of a museum
to generate their customised portraits made of letters. This work was
the basis for the interactive installation Photomaton that became part of
the permanent exhibition of “Torre Literária”, Cupertino de Miranda
Foundation1, dedicated to Portuguese literature. Figure 8.14 depicts a
photograph of the current installation.
Figure 8.14: Photograph of the interactive installation Photomaton (on the
right) at the permanent exhibition of “Torre Literária”, Cuper-
tino de Miranda Foundation, Vila Nova de Famalicão, Portugal.
Photo by Cupertino de Miranda Foundation, 2020.
1 https://www.cupertino.pt

132 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
The creation of images using text is a traditional design task. The
process of drawing with text can be traced back to manuscripts from
many centuries ago with illustrations made of handwritten words.
Fast forward to the late 1890s, Typewriter Art became an art form, with
the ﬁrst piece of known Typewriter Art being documented, an image
of a butterﬂy created by Flora Stacey in 1898 [281]. Later, in the 1960s,
computer artists started to explore automatic methods to represent
digitised images. Their process consisted in scanning an input image,
converting the resulting greyscale values into a grid of symbols, and
then drawing the symbols with a plotter, depicting different shades of
grey. Early examples include the work of Philip Peterson, who created
in 1964 a digital version of the Mona Lisa painting (see Figure 3.8a),
along with Kenneth Knowlton and Leon Harmon, who created in 1966
the image Studies in Perception I, one of the earliest known examples
of ASCII art and probably the ﬁrst computer-generated nude (see
Figure 3.8b). More information on these two works is presented in
Subsection 3.1.2.
8.3.1 Implementation
Inspired by these works, we developed a system that generates ty-
pographic portraits from input images. Each portrait consists of a
text composition using glyphs whose typographic weights, i.e. more
light or more bold, dynamically change to depict different shades of
the input image. This mapping of the darkness of the input image
into glyphs can be accomplished using a typeface with many weights
that enables the typographic representation of many shades of grey.
We consider that stencils evolved with the framework can play a role
here since they can generate glyphs with any thickness we need (see
Figure 8.15).
The generation of a typographic portrait involves the following steps:
(i) the input image is converted to grayscale; (ii) the brightness value of
each pixel is calculated; (iii) an input text is composed from left to right
and from top to bottom within a rectangular area proportional to the
input image; (iv) for each glyph, the average brightness of the pixels
located inside its bounds is calculated; and (v) the typographic weight
of each glyph is set inversely proportional to the average brightness
just calculated, i.e. a glyph positioned over a dark area of the input
image will be thicker than a glyph positioned over a lighter area.
The system that generates the portraits can be conﬁgured at differ-
ent levels, e.g. the number of text lines, leading, space between glyphs,
the width of the glyphs, the minimum and maximum thickness of

8.3 application in typographic portraits 133
Figure 8.15: Evolved stencil (left) and its glyphs drawn with different thick-
nesses (right).
the glyphs. The adjustment of these parameters enables the genera-
tion of typographic portraits with different visual characteristics. For
example, increasing the number of text lines provides more detail to
the portrait; decreasing the leading and/or space between the glyphs
makes the portrait visually denser; increasing the difference between
the minimum and maximum thickness of the glyphs provides more
contrast to the portrait. Furthermore, the system exports the generated
portraits to vector graphics. This way, it is possible to print the typo-
graphic portraits in different formats and scales, such as postcards
and posters.
8.3.2 Results
We tested the generator of portraits with different evolved stencils
and parameterisations to assess to what extent (i) the input image
remains recognisable in the typographic portrait and (ii) the input text
remains readable. Figures 8.16 and 8.17 show two typographic por-
traits generated from a photo of the graphic designer and researcher
Sérgio Rebelo. Both portraits are created using the stencil shown in
Figure 8.15 and, as input text, the poem “Eu Nunca Guardei Reban-
hos”, written in 1914 by Alberto Caeiro, an alter ego of the Portuguese
poet Fernando Pessoa. The difference between the two portraits relies
on the parameterisation. They have a different number of text lines
(50 and 100, respectively) and, consequently, different detail levels.
Analysing the generated portraits, we can say the input text remains
readable and the input image remains recognisable. This demonstrates
the ability of the stencil to draw legible glyphs while varying their
thickness to represent different shades of grey.
We have also experimented with the creation of animated portraits.
The video at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/evotype4_portrait.mov
shows an animated portrait created by rendering, in real-time, every
frame of an input video. It is noticeable the smooth change of the

134 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
Figure 8.16: Typographic portrait of the graphic designer and researcher
Sérgio Rebelo composed using 50 text lines.

8.3 application in typographic portraits 135
Figure 8.17: Typographic portrait of the graphic designer and researcher
Sérgio Rebelo composed using 100 text lines.

136 evotype iv : designing fitness functions
glyphs, in terms of shape, according to the moving image of the in-
put video. The resulting portraits, static and animated, demonstrate
the potential of the stencils evolved with the framework for the on-
demand generation of typefaces with variable form. More typographic
portraits and details can be found in the paper by Rebelo et al. [ 239].
8.4 summary
The framework can evolve type stencils using automated ﬁtness func-
tions designed by the users through a graphical interface. This way, it
enables the users to express preferences through the speciﬁcation of
behavioural and morphological features they intend to observe in the
evolved stencils. Overall, the experimental results show the ability of
the framework to evolve a set of alternative stencils with high ﬁtness
according to each designed ﬁtness function. That is, the framework can
evolve stencils to comply with different (typo)graphical preferences.
This framework further develops our stencil-based approach to
evolve glyph designs started in the previous Evotype iteration, with
focus, for example, on the representation and ﬁtness. The inclusion
of curves into the evolved stencils can facilitate the design of certain
parts of the glyphs and, consequently, reduce the number of elements
needed to draw the most curved parts. Similarly, the encoding of
the thickness of each line in the genes can give the evolved stencils
more ﬂexibility to draw small details of the glyphs, by reducing
thickness, or to draw wide parts, by increasing thickness and, this way,
eliminating the need to accumulate several lines. In terms of ﬁtness,
the combination of a CNN and a SOM to evaluate each glyph reveals
to be a more ﬂexible approach than the evaluation method used in
the previous Evotype iteration, which is based on a target font. As a
result, the evolutionary process can now converge to different suitable
solutions.
After testing the framework, we explored the application of evolved
stencils in a design project where we generate portraits made of letters.
This application is based on one of the many potential applications
for evolved stencils that we identiﬁed in the previous Evotype iteration
(see Section 7.3).
In conclusion, the framework, along with the previous Evotype itera-
tions, show some of the ways how EC and ML can be used to expand
tools for the creation of typefaces.

Part III
H U M A N FA C E G E N E R AT I O N

9
A U T O M AT I C G E N E R AT I O N O F FA C E S
This part of the thesis concerns our investigation into the automated
evolution of human face images. The developed system, titled X-faces
(after eXploit faces), unlocks many scientiﬁc and artistic applications.
In particular, X-faces can be applied in a more scientiﬁc, or technical,
way to generate face images that exploit the vulnerabilities of face de-
tectors and improve their performance by adding them to the training
dataset. The system X-faces can also have a more artistic, or creative,
application to develop a media art installation that allows the visitors
to interact with several artiﬁcial portraits whose realism makes them
wonder about the authenticity of what they are seeing and question
the idea of identity.
Each new face image is created by automatically recombining the
elementary parts of existing face images (see Figure 9.1). Then, we use
EC combined with automated ﬁtness assignment based on a ML face
detector to ﬁnd new faces that are not detected by this detector.
We present two iterations of X-faces. In the ﬁrst iteration, we cre-
ate frontal faces from a dataset of face images with the facial parts
annotated manually. Our main goal with this ﬁrst iteration is to ﬁnd
face images that fool a given face detector, i.e. images identiﬁed by
humans as faces but not recognised as faces by a face detector. In
the second iteration, we evolve faces with different poses, expressions
and illuminations, from a dataset of diverse face images with their
landmarks detected automatically. In this second iteration, we employ
the X-faces system to evolve face images that fool the face detector and
then we use these images to enrich the training dataset and improve
the performance of the detector. After testing the second iteration,
we develop the interactive installation Portraits of No One that uses
the generative process of X-faces to create and display new artiﬁcial
portraits by recombining the captured facial features of the people
who interact with it.
This work allowed us to investigate the computational generation
of visual content in the frontier between real and artiﬁcial. It also
allowed us to demonstrate the capabilities of AI to generate convincing
face images that can fool computers, deceive humans, and exploit the
differences between computer vision and human vision.
139

140 automatic generation of faces
Figure 9.1: Facial parts extracted and used to create new face images.

9.1 context 141
9.1 context
Systems for Face Detection ( FD), the task of ﬁnding the locations and
sizes of faces in a given image, are being thoroughly researched due
to their wide range of applications, including entertainment services,
social networks, search engines, and security systems [ 302, 305]. Typi-
cally, such detectors employ classiﬁers that are created using example-
based learning techniques. Therefore, the dataset plays a key role not
only for attaining competitive performances but also for assessing
the strengths and shortcomings of the detector. This means that the
creation of adequate datasets for training, testing, and validation of
the detector becomes a crucial process.
The creation of a face detector requires the construction of a dataset
composed of positive and negative examples. FD comprises two major
tasks: classify the occurrence of a face in an image and determine its
location and size. Therefore, a positive example consists of an image
containing at least one face and the respective bounds. Conversely, a
negative example consists of an image that does not contain faces. Col-
lecting both types of examples presents troublesome endeavours as the
annotation of several face images is an arduous and time-consuming
task and gathering negative examples is far from being a straightfor-
ward task due to the lack of well-deﬁned guidelines [ 176, 270]. For
these reasons, after gathering a number of examples, Data Augmenta-
tion (DA) techniques are usually used to expand the dataset [ 39].
The state-of-the-art models used for different classiﬁcation and
recognition tasks have become more complex and deep. Training a
deep model from scratch requires a large number of examples. A
model trained with a small number of examples can overﬁt to the
training dataset and generalise poorly. Some strategies exist to deal
with this problem, including ﬁne-tuning models trained on more
massive datasets [57], regularisation methods, or collection of more
data. Collecting more data becomes harder if the examples have to
be labelled, requiring the supervision of the label assignment process.
Even when using collaborative or crowd-sourcing platforms, the risk
of incorrect labelling can occur. As a result, DA has become an essential
component for the training of deep models.
9.2 related work
In the context of this work, we are concerned with the generation
of human face images using EC techniques. Therefore, we focus this
Section on contributions that employ EC to create face images.

142 automatic generation of faces
Some researchers investigate the creation of new faces from exist-
ing ones. Caldwell and Johnston [ 32, 131] present a computational
criminal sketch artist that implements a GA to recombine regions of
existing face images. Other approaches based on the recombination of
face regions include the work of Takagi and Kishi [ 275] and Hancock
and Frowd [ 108]. Lim [ 160] proposes an IEC approach to generate
facial expressions by applying two-dimensional deformations to face
images. In an attempt to improve an FD system, Chen, Chen, and Gao
[38] employ a self-adaptive GA to manipulate face images using image
segmentation techniques and photometric transformations. Frowd,
Hancock, and Carson [ 79] use a GA combined with Principal Compo-
nent Analysis (PCA) and eigenvectors to create an IEC system aimed to
evolve faces.
Other researchers try to create faces from scratch using, for example,
general-purpose evolutionary art tools. Examples of such approaches
include the work of DiPaola and Gabora [59] and Ventrella [292], where
the similarity to a target image is used to assign ﬁtness. Machado et
al. [ 48, 175, 184] also use a general-purpose evolutionary art tool
and an automated ﬁtness assignment approach to evolve images
from scratch, including ﬁgurative images such as human faces and
ambiguous images.
We identify the following limitations in the related work: (i) the
generation of invalid solutions by using transformations that can be
too destructive; (ii) the small variety of images that can be generated
in some approaches; (iii) the generation of images too abstract or too
similar to a target image; and (iv) the dependence on user guidance in
non-automatic approaches, which may lead to user fatigue.
The analysis of the related work helped us to deﬁne a set of guide-
lines for the design of our approach: (i) it should be able to explore the
search space automatically; (ii) it should guarantee the generation of
only valid faces; and (iii) it should create faces considerably different
from the ones contained in the dataset.

10
X FA C E S I : E V O LV I N G F R O N TA L FA C E I M A G E S
This Chapter presents the ﬁrst iteration of X-faces system that auto-
matically evolves frontal face images that are not detected by a face
detector. Figure 10.1 shows two face images of an existing dataset and
two new face images generated from them.
Figure 10.1: Two face images from an existing dataset and two new face
images generated from them.
10.1 framework
The approach creates frontal faces by recombining the facial parts,
i.e. eyes, nose, mouth and eyebrows, from different faces contained
in the dataset to be augmented. This way, an evolved face consists of
an image of an existing person with the facial parts of other persons
blended into it, creating a face image different from the original ones.
We designed an evolutionary engine to recombine the facial parts.
The evolutionary process is guided with an automated ﬁtness function
based on the response of a pre-trained face detector. Since we are
interested in evolving faces that the detector can not detect as such,
we designed the evolutionary engine so the activation response of the
detector is minimised. This way, we should be able to evolve faces that
are no longer detected.
The approach involves the following steps: (i) the annotation of face
images by indicating the bounds of the faces and their parts using a
custom annotation tool; (ii) the training of a face detector with the an-
notated examples; and (iii) the automated evolution of new examples
using the trained detector to assign ﬁtness. Therefore, the approach
relies on three modules: the annotation tool, the evolutionary engine,
and the face detector. We describe these modules in the following
Subsections.
143

144 xfaces i : evolving frontal face images
10.1.1 Annotation Tool
We develop a general-purpose image annotation tool to enable the
annotation of any object in an input image (see Figure 10.2). To do
so, the user positions a sequence of points along the contour of each
object and chooses the corresponding category. New categories can
be added at any moment. The data related to the annotations created
by the user is saved in output ﬁles that can be later used and edited.
These output ﬁles include one eXtensible Markup Language ( XML) ﬁle
for each image and one text ﬁle for each object category. The tool also
exports the image and the binary mask of each annotated object. When
opening a folder with images, the tool loads the corresponding anno-
tations saved in ﬁles if they exist. This annotation tool is implemented
in Java combined with the open-source library Processing.
Figure 10.2: Screenshot of the annotation tool.
In the context of this work, we use this tool to, for a given set of face
images, annotate the bounds of their elementary parts,i.e. left eye, right
eye, left eyebrow, right eyebrow, nose, mouth, as well as the bounds
of the entire face. A demo video of the annotation tool can be seen at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/xfaces1_annotation_tool.mov.

10.1 framework 145
10.1.2 Evolutionary Engine
The evolutionary engine implements a conventional GA where each
individual is a face image constructed from parts of multiple faces.
The genotype of each individual consists of a tuple of integers (face,
left eye, right eye, nose, mouth, left eyebrow, right eyebrow). Each
integer encodes the index of the annotated face image that will provide
that part. Each genotype is then mapped into a phenotype by creating
a composite face, i.e. the facial parts encoded in the genotype are
placed over a base face, also encoded in the genotype (see Figure 10.3).
This process is accomplished by using the seamless cloning algorithm
provided by OpenCV , which consists of an implementation of the
work of Pérez, Gangnet, and Blake [ 224]. The evolutionary engine is
implemented in Java.
Genotype Phenotype
193 145 94 140 163122 86
Figure 10.3: Genotype and phenotype of an individual.
10.1.3 Fitness Assignment
To evaluate each individual, we created an automated ﬁtness function
based on a face detector. We trained a cascade classiﬁer based on
the work of Viola and Jones [ 294]. It uses a set of small features in
combination with a variant of AdaBoost [ 78] to attain an efﬁcient
classiﬁer. This classiﬁer consists of a cascade of small stage classiﬁers.
Each stage is composed of a group of Multi-scale Block Local Binary
Patterns ( MB-LBP) [ 157] that are applied to the input pixels. MB-LBP
are calculated similarly to the Local Binary Patterns ( LBP) [218]. LBP
features label each pixel by looking at their surrounding pixels and
testing whether their values are higher or lower.MB-LBP features extend
the LBPs to different regions around the middle pixel. The behaviour
of the cascade classiﬁer can be summarised in the following steps:
1. Deﬁne w and h as the width and height, respectively, of the input
image;
2. Deﬁne a window of size w′× h′;

146 xfaces i : evolving frontal face images
3. Deﬁne a scale factor s greater than 1. For instance, a scale factor
of 1.2 means that the window will be enlarged by 20%;
4. Calculate all windows with size w′× h′ from the position (0, 0) to
(w− w′, h− h′) with 1 pixel increments of the upper left corner;
5. Apply the cascade classiﬁer to each window. If the window
passes all stages of the classiﬁer, it is classiﬁed as containing a
face. If the window fails one stage (i.e. obtains a value lower than
the stage threshold), it is classiﬁed as not containing a face;
6. Apply s to w′ and h′, and go to step 4 until w′ exceeds w or h′
exceeds h.
The ﬁtness function uses internal values of the detection process
that provide an indication of the degree of certainty in the detection.
In this work, we need a ﬁtness function that favours individuals that
are not detected as faces. As such, we designed the following ﬁtness
function f to calculate the ﬁtness of a face image i:
f (i) = ( tstg− pstg) + (tstg× ndet) + 1
1 + difstg ,
where tstg is the total number of stages of the face classiﬁer used in
the detection process, pstg is the number of stages that the image i
passes, ndet is a boolean variable that has the value of 0 when a face
is detected in image i or 1 when no face is detected, and difstg is the
difference between the value obtained in the last stage that the image i
passes and the threshold of that stage.
10.2 experimentation
We conduct a set of experiments to test the ability to evolve positive
examples (face images) that are misclassiﬁed as negative examples
(images with no faces) by a face detector.
10.2.1 Experimental Setup
We begin by deﬁning two datasets of positive examples, one with 200
examples and the other with 500 examples. Then, we use these two
datasets to train the detectors face200 and face500, respectively. The
face200 dataset includes the 200 annotated examples that are used
by the GA to create new faces. The face500 dataset contains all the
face200 examples plus 300 other examples. With these two datasets, we
intend to investigate the impact of the approach in a scenario where

10.2 experimentation 147
the GA can recombine all faces contained in the training dataset and
in a scenario where the GA can only recombine a part of the training
dataset.
The positive examples that compose both datasets are extracted from
the FACITY project1, which is a worldwide project that collects pictures
of human faces from different cities. The negative examples consist
of 1 905 images from the Urtho negative face dataset 2, which contains
different types of images including landscapes, objects, drawings, and
computer-generated images.

We conduct two experiments: exp200 and exp500. In exp200, face200
guides the evolution and face500 curates evolved individuals. In exp500,
face500 guides the evolution and face200 curates individuals. The role
of the curator is to select evolved individuals that it does not detect
as faces. With these two experiments, we intend to analyse the results
obtained when using face200 and face500 to assign ﬁtness, including
the behaviour and selection of the curators.

Regarding the evolutionary engine, the main settings are shown in
Table 10.3. In terms of the selection of facial parts that are recombined
to create new faces, we maintain the pairs of eyes and the pairs of
eyebrows. The rationale for this is related to the typical horizontal
symmetry of most faces that face detectors can learn from the positive
examples. This was veriﬁed in preliminary experiments, in which we
observed that detectors struggled on face images where the pairs of
eyes and eyebrows belonged to different faces, leading to an early
convergence of the evolutionary process. Also, the evolved face images
were unnatural and easily recognisable as blends.
10.2.2 Experimental Results
We now present and analyse the experimental results obtained in ex-
periments exp200 and exp500. We begin by analysing the progression
of ﬁtness and face detections over generations in the two experiments.
Then, we look at the evolved individuals, including the best individu-
als and the ones that are selected by the curators. Finally, we consider
the visuals of the evolved individuals.
Figure 10.4 shows the ﬁtness progression of the best individuals
over generations in exp200 and exp500. For each experiment, we plot
the ﬁtness values provided by face200 and face500 to visualise how one
affects the other. We can observe that evolution can optimise the ﬁtness
function. In both experiments, when the ﬁtness value of the guiding
detector increases, the ﬁtness value of the curating detector tends to
behave similarly. The values reveal that it is easier to satisfy face200
either when it is evaluating or curating. The observed ﬁtness behaviour
in exp500 suggests that even when the face500 is guiding, the ﬁtness
values of face200 are higher than the ones of face500. On the other
hand, when face200 is guiding, the ﬁtness of face500 also increases,

150 xfaces i : evolving frontal face images
suggesting that exp200 can also evolve solutions for the exp500. The
results suggest that individuals evolved in exp500 tend to be new from
the perspective of face200, i.e. different from the ones contained in the
face200 training dataset. The behaviour of face500 in exp200 suggests
that it can evolve individuals that are new in the face500 dataset but
at a lower rate. This can be a consequence of the face200 training
examples being included in the face500 training dataset.
In Figure 10.5, we visualise the average of individuals that are de-
tected as faces over generations. The number of detections decreases
in both experiments, showing the ability of the approach to evolve
face images that are no longer detected as such. In both experiments,
face500 detects more faces than face200. This difference is more pro-
nounced in exp200, in which the detections of face200 decrease at a
higher rate. In exp500, detections decrease at a similar rate. We can also
say that, in exp200, evolution promotes the generation of solutions that
are detected by face500. This is consistent with the ﬁtness behaviour
in Figure 10.4, suggesting that the approach exploits vulnerabilities
common to both detectors.
Figure 10.6 depicts in which generation the best individual, on
average, is no longer detected. Inexp200, it takes only a few generations
for the best individual to stop being detected by face200. However, the
best individual in some evolutionary runs is still detected by face500
in the ﬁnal generations. In exp500, the results indicate that there are
evolutionary runs where the best individual continues to be detected
by both detectors.
Regarding the individuals evolved in each experiment, Figure 10.7
presents the best individuals obtained in multiple evolutionary runs,
while Figure 10.8 shows examples of individuals selected by the cura-
tor. The results suggest that, although there are individuals in com-
mon, the two curators tend to select different individuals. Some of
the selected faces share characteristics that we consider as exploits
of the detectors, particularly at the level of the skin tone, contrast,
and size of some facial parts. In exp200, there are overlaps between
the best individuals (see Figure 10.7a) and the curated ones (see Fig-
ure 10.8a). When face500 is guiding (see Figure 10.7b) or curating (see
Figure 10.8a), the evolved individuals share more characteristics. This
is consistent with the idea that the exploits of face500 tend to be also
exploits of face200.
Figure 10.9 presents a selection of individuals evolved in multi-
ple evolutionary runs that we ﬁnd to be interesting and peculiar.
This approach creates atypical faces with unexpected features. For
instance, we can see convincing face images of strange babies, some

of them with piercings, cases of gender ambiguity, and mixtures
of interracial attributes. More evolved faces can be visualised at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/xfaces1.mov. Some of the generated
faces are photorealistic but disturbing at the same time. These faces
may fall into the uncanny valley [164], where we encounter entities ap-
pearing almost human that provoke a sense of unease and discomfort
in the viewers.
10.3 summary
The overall results show the ability of the approach to automatically
generate new photorealistic face images that put face detectors to the
test. Therefore, the approach can be a viable solution for DA in the
ﬁeld of FD by enabling the creation of new examples to train and test
face detectors. Besides creating convincing face images that are not
detected by face detectors, the resulting face images can also reveal
uncommon and unexpected features. Ultimately, this work contrasts
computer vision with human perception, highlighting the differences

152 xfaces i : evolving frontal face images
Figure 10.9: Examples of faces evolved in multiple evolutionary runs.
between them, and raising awareness towards the issues that such
strengths and limitations may arise.
We consider that the approach, along with its outputs, unlock some
possibilities for applications. For instance, we can see this approach
being used in the dynamic creation of faces for credible characters in
games, virtual reality environments, crowd simulations and cinema.
Additionally, the placement of the facial parts can be modiﬁed to create
more surrealistic images. For example, we can play with the placement
of the facial parts in different positions, e.g. replace all facial parts with
eyes or replace the eyes with mouths. Or, instead of using only parts
of human faces, we could recombine varied objects, e.g. parts from
other animals. Furthermore, this work, and its possible applications,

10.3 summary 153
highlight the need for ensuring fairness in AI applications, and for the
regulation of the use of AI.
This work allowed us to validate our idea of computationally cre-
ating new face images that can exploit the vulnerabilities of face
detectors. However, we consider there is space for further work, in-
cluding (i) the automation of the annotation process, (ii) the evolution
of face images with varied poses and expressions, and (iii) the use of
evolved face images to retrain the detector in an attempt to improve its
performance. These ideas paved the way to the next X-faces iteration,
which is presented in the following Chapter.

11
X FA C E S I I : E V O LV I N G U N C O N S T R A I N E D FA C E
I M A G E S
In this Chapter, we continue our investigation on the automatic cre-
ation of new face images. The result is a fully automatic approach
capable of recombining facial parts of existing face images with un-
constrained poses and expressions. Figure 11.1 shows a series of face
images generated by the second iteration of the X-faces system.
We start by presenting the application of this approach to augment
an input dataset, which is then used to retrain a face detector and
improve its performance. This application is relevant and promising,
however, in the context of this thesis, we are mainly interested in the
creative possibilities that it enables. Therefore, we use the developed
approach to create an interactive media art installation that generates
and displays artiﬁcial portraits using the facial features of the people
who interact with it.
Figure 11.1: Face images generated using parts of different faces blended
into the same face.
11.1 framework
The approach is built on the idea explored in the previous iteration of
X-faces, i.e. generate new face images by recombining the facial parts of
existing faces. We use facial landmarks prediction to automatically de-
tect facial parts and also to align them before their recombination. This
155

156 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
way, we can exchange facial parts between faces with unconstrained
poses or expressions.
There are two operating modes that control how facial parts are
selected to create new face images. In one mode, the selection of the
facial parts is random. In the other mode, the selection is evolved
using a GA designed to ﬁnd new face images that are not detected by
a pre-trained face detector. The approach is implemented in Python
and employs off-the-shelf ML and Computer Vision ( CV) tools. The
processes of generating and evolving face images are described below.
11.1.1 Generation of Face Images
The process of generating new face images, or examples, is twofold.
First, the examples of face images contained in the input dataset are
processed to extract data. Then, based on this data, new examples are
created from the input dataset.
11.1.1.1 Preprocessing
Given an input dataset containing face images, we extract data needed
to generate new examples, including the facial landmark points and
head pose of each face. For each image in the input dataset, we
proceed as follows. We begin by detecting possible face regions in the
image using a pre-trained face detector included in the Dlib library 1.
This detector can search an image for faces and return their regions,
i.e. bounding boxes. Then, inside each detected face region, we ﬁnd
facial landmarks using a facial landmark predictor that is also pre-
trained and provided by Dlib. This predictor places a sequence of
68 points along the contours of the following facial structures: jaw,
eyebrows, nose, eyes, and mouth. Once we have the landmark points,
we use them to estimate the head pose, i.e. the orientation angles of
the face in space.
The output of the preprocessing stage is a set of ﬁles, one per
face found in the input dataset. Each output ﬁle contains the facial
landmark points, pose information, and the path to the face image ﬁle.
The input dataset only needs to be preprocessed once, regardless of
the number of new examples to be generated.
11.1.1.2 Generation
Once we have the landmarks and pose calculated for all faces found in
the input dataset, we can proceed to the generation of new examples.
1 http://dlib.net

11.1 framework 157
The creation of a new face image consists in selecting a face of the
dataset and then replace its facial parts (eyebrows, eyes, nose and
mouth) with parts from other faces. However, the automatic recom-
bination of facial parts, especially under unconstrained conditions,
demands special care with their alignment in order to attain natural
and seamless facial blends.
Given the data extracted during the preprocessing of the input
dataset, we begin by selecting one face among all the faces saved.
This face will be our target face, i.e. the face whose facial parts will be
replaced with parts from other faces. Then, we select one face for each
facial part we want to replace. These faces are our source faces, i.e. the
faces that will provide the facial parts to be blended onto the target
face. The selection of the source faces takes into account the pose of the
target face. That is, the source faces should have head poses similar to
the one of the target face. By similar, we mean no greater than a preset
maximum difference.
The process of generating a new face image is overviewed in Fig-
ure 11.2 and is described as follows. Before blending parts of the source
faces onto the target face, we ﬁrst need to align each source face with
the target face. That is, warp each source so that the facial features are
aligned. In other words, the facial landmarks of each source should be
aligned with the landmarks of the target. Since we know the position
of the 68 landmark points in the faces, we can use this data to warp
each source to match the target. To do so, for each face, we use OpenCV
to calculate the Delaunay triangulation of each face using the speciﬁc
points of the landmarks plus a few more points (two points on both
sides of the nose and six points above the eyebrows) that are calcu-
lated based on the landmarks. The Delaunay triangulation of these
points allows us to divide each face image into triangles. The resulting
triangles cover the same facial regions in each face. Therefore, since we
know which triangle in each source face corresponds to which triangle
in the target face, the next step is to warp each source face triangle to
the corresponding target face triangle. To attain this, we use OpenCV
to calculate, for each source face triangle, the afﬁne transform that
maps its vertices to the vertices of the corresponding triangle in the
target face. Then, we apply this afﬁne transform to the pixels of the
source face contained inside the triangle before being transformed to
the target face. This results in the distortion of the source faces to fully
align with the target face.
Once each source face is aligned with the target face, the next step is
to blend them in a smooth way. We attain this by using the seamless
cloning algorithm provided by OpenCV to copy one region of each

158 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
EYES EYEBROWSFACE
1 2 3 4 5 6 7
MOUTH NOSE
Figure 11.2: Process of generating a new face image. From left to right: ( 1) in-
put face images; ( 2) facial key points deﬁned based on the de-
tected facial landmarks; ( 3) Delaunay triangulation of the key
points; (4) triangles with different colours showing their corre-
spondence between faces; (5) input faces warped to the base face;

11.1 framework 159
warped source face and place it upon the target image in a way that,
most of the times, produces a new photorealistic face. In order to
delimit the regions of the warped source faces that should be blended
into the target face, we create masks. We use speciﬁc vertices of the
Delaunay triangles to calculate the mask of each facial part. Each mask
includes a preset margin, which causes the mask to contain more area
than the one deﬁned by the vertices used to create it. The purpose of
this margin is to expand the area of each region in order to cover some
skin around each facial part. This is useful for the smooth assemblage
of facial parts.
11.1.2 Evolution of Face Images
Using the generative process described above, we can generate numer-
ous face images. As previously mentioned, the selection of the target
and source faces, which are used to create a new face image, can be
random or evolved using EC.
We use a GA to ﬁnd new face images that can enrich the input
training dataset and thus improve the performance of the resulting
detector. The evolutionary process is identical to the one implemented
in the ﬁrst iteration of this work. The genotype consists of a tuple of
integers that encode a target face and the source faces that provide the
left eyebrow, right eyebrow, left eye, right eye, nose, and mouth. The
phenotype consists of the composite of the facial parts encoded in
the genotype, according to the process illustrated in Figure 11.2. In
terms of variation operators, the crossover operator exchanges facial
parts between two faces, while the mutation operator replaces genes
of the faces with others selected at random. Both variation operators
ensure that each individual is composed of facial parts from faces with
similar head poses. This way, we try to avoid the evolution of defective
face images caused by, for example, overly extensive distortions.
The evolutionary process is guided with an automated ﬁtness func-
tion that employs a face detector. We designed the evolutionary engine
to ﬁnd new faces that are no longer detected. To do so, we designed
the following ﬁtness function f to calculate the ﬁtness of the face
image i:
f (i) = 1
1+| t− c| ,
where t is a preset target conﬁdence of the detector and c is the
conﬁdence of the detector in the predicted bounding box most similar
to the bounding box of the actual face. To calculate c, we use the

160 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
evaluation metric Intersection over Union (IoU) between the ground-
truth bounding box and each predicted bounding box. TheIoU between
two bounding boxes is calculated by dividing the area of their overlap
by the area of their union. Once we ﬁnd the predicted bounding box
with highest IoU in relation to the ground-truth bounding box, we use
the conﬁdence, or activation, of the detector in that region as our c.
The approach performs one evolutionary run per face to be evolved.
For each run, a face is selected from the input dataset and used as the
target face. Then, the evolutionary process ﬁnds the combination of
facial parts that, when blended into the selected target face, obtains
the best ﬁtness. This way, in each evolutionary run, all evolved faces
share the same target face, so the ﬁrst gene does not change. Our goal
with this approach is to increase diversity among the evolved faces,
for example, in terms of background and head pose.
11.2 experimentation
We conduct experiments on the approach to evaluate how it performs
as a method for DA in a FD scenario. To do so, we compare face
detectors trained with (i) a baseline dataset, (ii) the baseline dataset
augmented using our approach in the random mode, and (iii) the
baseline dataset augmented using our approach in evolutionary mode.
11.2.1 Experimental Setup
We designed and tested three experimental setups: setupBase, se-
tupRand, and setupEvo. In setupBase, we train the baseline detector
with a baseline dataset consisting of a subset of the CelebFaces At-
tributes (CelebA) dataset. In setupRand, we use our approach to ran-
domly generate new examples to double the number of examples
in the baseline dataset and then we retrain the baseline detector. In
setupEvo, we use our approach to evolve new examples while using
the baseline detector to assign ﬁtness. Again, we evolve new exam-
ples until we double the number of examples in the training dataset
and then we retrain the baseline detector. In each setup, we train 10
face detectors under the same conditions but with different random
weights initialisations.
The detector used is the Tiny You Only Look Once ( YOLO) v22, a
deep single shot detector that uses a cell-grid input and an anchor
system to provide fast and accurate detections in real-time [ 241]. We
choose this detector due to its state-of-the-art relevance, providing a
2 https://pjreddie.com/darknet/yolov2/

11.2 experimentation 161
good compromise between efﬁciency, resources needed, training and
testing speed. The training parameters are shown in Table 11.1. We
resorted to an off-the-shelf conﬁguration based on default parameters.
Some parameters are transversal to similar approaches, but there are
some that are speciﬁc for the training of this model. For instance, the
warmup epochs correspond to a certain number of epochs in which
the cells are forced to match the size of the anchors.

11.2.2 Experimental Results
In Figure 11.3, we visualise the average training loss of the detec-
tors. On average, setupEvo optimises at a faster rate than the other
setups, although all stabilise around the same value. By looking at
the progression of the ﬁtness of the best individual over generations
in setupEvo, shown in Figure 11.4, we can see that the evolutionary
process is able to improve the ﬁtness of the individuals according to
the ﬁtness function. In other words, on average, each evolutionary run
can ﬁnd a combination of facial parts that created a new face that is
detected with a low level of conﬁdence by the detector.

11.2 experimentation 163
After training the detectors in each setup, they are submitted to the
benchmark dataset for testing. For that purpose, we use the Face De-
tection Data set and Benchmark ( FDDB) dataset3, which contains 5 171
faces in 2 845 images taken from the Faces in the Wild dataset [ 127]. To
measure the performance of the face detectors trained in each setup,
we rely on the mean Average Precision ( mAP) based on the IoU of
the detections, which is a measure used on several benchmark and
competitions to assess object detection results [ 16]. The IoU threshold
used for calculating the mAP is set to 0.3.

Table 11.3: Results in terms of mean Average Precision obtained in the three
experimental setups.
The results in terms of mAP are overviewed in Table 11.3. The
setupEvo has the best average result, with an average mAP increase
of 0.3 over the setupBase, and 0.1 over the setupRand. The setupRand
creates examples for more stable training. Due to its stochastic nature,
it is expected that it explores more diverse faces. On the other hand,
the setupEvo explores the vulnerabilities of the detectors, generating
examples that they fail to detect and that can be useful for the training
dataset of the detectors. Figure 11.5 shows some typical face images
evolved in the setupEvo.
Figure 11.5: Typical face images evolved in the setupEvo. The images shown
are resized to the input size of the model.
3 http://vis-www.cs.umass.edu/fddb/

164 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
Overall, the results suggest that setupRand and setupEvo have an
advantageous impact on the baseline training dataset. More specif-
ically, the results indicate that: (i) adding new examples generated
randomly to the training dataset ( setupRand) improves the detector
performance; (ii) using EC to evolve new examples (setupEvo) results
in face images that exploit the vulnerabilities of the face detector; and
(iii) adding evolved examples to the training dataset improves the
detector performance, more than when using random examples.
11.3 application in interactive installation
After employing and testing the X-faces system in a more technical
area, we decided to take it to the public. We developed an interactive
media art installation that uses the generative process of X-faces (see
Subsection 11.1.1.2) to create and display new artiﬁcial facial portraits
by recombining the captured facial features of the people who interact
with it. The result is the installation Portraits of No One (see Figure 11.6).
With this installation, we explore the fabrication of visual content in the
frontier between the real and the artiﬁcial as it generates photorealistic
portraits of people who do not exist, i.e. portraits of no one. When
observing the generated portraits, which are projected on the walls of
the installation room, most visitors can ﬁnd it difﬁcult to tell which
portraits are real.
The installation was selected as a ﬁnalist work of the Sonae Media
Art Award 2019 and, consequently, it was exhibited at the National
Museum of Contemporary Art – Museu do Chiado, in Lisbon, Por-
tugal, from late November 2019 to early February 2020. A video of
the installation can be visualised at http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/
portraits_of_no_one.mp4. In the following paragraphs, we describe
the installation as it was exhibited in the Sonae Media Art Award
2019.
The room of the installation, with six meters long by four meters
wide, comprises two main areas: the input area and the output area.
The input area, which is located next to the entrance to the room, con-
tains the capturing box attached to one side of a pillar. The capturing
box allows visitors to capture their faces and, this way, “feed” the
installation. The output area is located at the back of the room, after
the input area, and is surrounded by an immersive video projection of
twelve meters wide on three walls. This projection contains an array
of portraits that are continuously generated by the installation. Each
portrait is displayed with a size similar to that of a real face. This
area also includes a speaker attached to the ceiling of the room that

11.3 application in interactive installation 165
Figure 11.6: Photographs of the installation Portraits of No One showing a
person interacting with the capturing box (top) and people ob-
serving the walls crowded with generated portraits (bottom).
National Museum of Contemporary Art – Museu do Chiado, Lis-
bon. Photos by José Paulo Ruas / Direção Geral do Património
Cultural, 2019.

166 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
reproduces an intertwined combination of sounds recorded during
the interactions of the visitors with the capturing box.
The hardware that builds the installation includes a custom cap-
turing box, three video projectors, a loudspeaker, and a computer.
The capturing box (see Figure 11.7) obtains the data needed for the
installation to operate, including face images of the users and sounds
produced by them. The capturing box contains: (i) a video camera to
capture the faces; (ii) a square light ring that ﬁts around the camera
to provide even illumination and, this way, reduce shadows in the
captured faces; (iii) a display to show users a live preview of their
faces before capturing; (iv) a microphone to record some seconds of
audio during each interaction; (v) a push-button, with a colour light,
that allows users to trigger the face capturing while providing colour
feedback; and (vi) a micro-controller to allow the computer to commu-
nicate with the push-button through which users trigger the capture
of the face. Figure 11.8 overviews the making process of the capturing
box.
LCD
video camera
fan
LED ring
microphone
push button
with RGB LED
MDF box
Figure 11.7: Schematic of the capturing box showing its main components.
The capturing box implements real-time face detection. We resort
to off-the-shelf solutions from OpenCV and Dlib libraries to analyse
each frame of the camera feed and detect the faces of users, and
their facial landmarks, when they stand in front of the capturing box.
We implemented a set of detection ﬁlters to determine if a detected
face should be considered as valid. When a valid detection is found,
the system draws a rectangle around the face on the live preview
shown on the screen. In addition to drawing a rectangle, the system
changes the colour of the push-button from red, which indicates that
no face is being detected, to white and presents a message on the
screen encouraging the user to take a picture. When the user presses
the push-button, the system begins a countdown of three seconds,
shown on the top of the screen close to the video camera. When the

11.3 application in interactive installation 167
Figure 11.8: Snapshots of the making process of the capturing box depicting
different stages from the laser cutting to the assemblage of
custom-made parts, including the body of the box, the adjustable
arm for the video camera, and the LED ring that ﬁts around the
camera.
countdown ends, the system captures the face and saves the related
data needed to generate new portraits.
To create the portraits, the installation uses the generator described
in Subsection 11.1.1.2. There is a selection mechanism that manages
the portraits that are displayed on the walls of the installation. This
mechanism selects a random set of faces captured from the users
and supplies them to the generator to create a new portrait from
the facial features of these faces. This selection process takes into
account the timestamps of the captured faces, selecting more recent
faces. Each portrait being projected on the walls has a lifespan. After
this lifespan is over, a new set of faces is selected and used by the
generator to create another artiﬁcial portrait, which replaces the old
portrait. Occasionally, the selection mechanism chooses a captured
face at random and displays it.
The installation also implements a producer of ambient sound that
combines sound samples, which are recorded by the capturing box

168 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
Figure 11.9: Typical portraits generated in the installation Portraits of No One
during the Sonae Media Art Award 2019 exhibition.

11.3 application in interactive installation 169
during the face capturing process, and reproduces the result using
the loudspeaker. The sound is generated by intertwining a total of
twelve samples randomly selected from the set of all recorded samples.
The generated portraits, along with the ambient sound, enables the
installation to maintain an ever-changing audiovisual environment
consisting of ephemeral portraits projected on the walls combined
with intertwined sounds produced by the real people behind the
portraits.
As the installation “feeds” from its users, their faces become part
of the artwork. This creates an engaging experience for visitors. We
observed varied interactions among users and also between users and
the generated portraits. Most users tend to regard the portraits on the
walls as images of real people and start to analyse and, sometimes,
talk with other people about their visual appearance. However, when
they recognise themselves or a familiar facial part in a portrait, they
change their behaviour. At this moment, users demonstrate empathy
with this artiﬁcial person and with other users who are also blended
into the same portrait. Furthermore, the photo-realism of the artiﬁcial
portraits may promote critical thinking about the capabilities of AI to
generate content that makes people question the veracity of what they
are observing.
During the Sonae Media Art Award 2019 exhibition, from late
November 2019 to early February 2020, the capturing box was used
about 5 000 times. This high involvement of the people, at the Na-
tional Museum of Contemporary Art – Museu do Chiado, allowed the
installation to create endless portraits (see Figure 11.9).
Later, we created a new version of the installation to be exhibited
at Círculo de Artes Plásticas de Coimbra, Portugal, from April 2021
to June 2021. This new version differs from the previous one in some
aspects, particularly concerning the installation space and the captur-
ing of the visitors’ faces. In this version, the installation takes place in
three rooms: (i) one where the faces of the visitors are captured; (ii) an-
other where the images of the captured faces are shown, through two
video projections; and (iii) another room where the artiﬁcial portraits
are shown, also through two video projections. To capture people’s
faces, we developed a new device speciﬁcally designed for this exhi-
bition. This device is operated by a person of the museum staff and
allows the capture of face images with better photographic quality.
Figure 11.10 shows the installation Portraits of No One at the Círculo
de Artes Plásticas de Coimbra.
Further information can be found on the publications regarding the
Portraits of No One installation [50, 194, 195].

170 xfaces ii : evolving unconstrained face images
Figure 11.10: Photographs of the installation Portraits of No One showing
the capturing of a visitor’s face (top) and people observing
generated portraits (bottom). Círculo de Artes Plásticas de

11.4 summary 171
11.4 summary
This work allowed us to explore the automatic creation of new face
images out of existing datasets. Overall, the experimental results show
that the approach, especially in the evolutionary mode, can generate
face examples that, when added to the training dataset, improve
the performance of face detectors. Despite the difﬁculty of creating
new face images from the unconstrained examples (different poses,
expressions, sizes, skin tones, and lighting), the approach is able to
recombine parts of existing faces to create new faces that are generally
photorealistic.
The ability to fabricate photorealistic face images opens up pos-
sibilities of many interesting applications. For instance, we applied
the approach in an artistic context through the development of the
media art installation Portraits of No One. This installation explores the
fabrication of artiﬁcial portraits using the facial parts of the people
who interact with it.
Looking forward, future research may focus on: (i) reﬁne the process
that generates each new face in an attempt to prevent, for example,
blurred facial parts, brightness and contrast differences between the
facial parts, and visible seams of some parts; (ii) implement an online
mode to enable the approach to generate new examples as they are
needed during training; (iii) use the evolutionary engine to ﬁnd small
subsets of generated examples that provide good performance to face
detectors; and (iv) integrate the approach with the Evolutionary FramE-
work for Classiﬁer assessmenT and ImproVemEnt ( EFECTIVE) [46] to
enable the automatic and iterative improvement of detectors by con-
tinuously generating new examples that they fail to detect and then
retraining them with such examples.
In conclusion, this work, X-faces, illustrates a way how EC with
automated ﬁtness assignment can be used to generate new face images
that may fool computational face detectors and deceive humans.

Part IV
N O N - P H O T O R E A L I S T I C R E N D E R I N G

12
PA I N T I N G I M A G E S W I T H A R T I F I C I A L A N T S
In this part of the thesis, we present our research on the automated
evolution of artiﬁcial ants that paint NPRs of input images. These
ants are the basis of the evolutionary art system Photogrowth [171,
179], which employs an approach inspired by ants and EC to generate
artworks with varied aesthetic characteristics.
We present an automated ﬁtness assignment approach to guide
the evolutionary process and, this way, ﬁnd ant species with features
that match preferences expressed by users. To attain this, we develop
a custom interface that enables users to interactively design ﬁtness
functions based on different visual and behavioural features. We also
present a rendering process that enables users to customise differ-
ent visual properties of each ant painting. We test the ability of the
approach to create NPRs that satisfy the users. Furthermore, we ex-
plore the application of evolved artiﬁcial ant species as image ﬁlters
on an online social network, allowing its users to experiment with
Photogrowth and, this way, create unique NPRs of their own images.
This work allowed us to explore the potential of Artiﬁcial Life (A-Life)
and EC to generate visually appealing and complex computer graphics
from simple ant behaviours.
12.1 context
The work presented in this part of the thesis is built on the work of
Machado et al. [ 171, 179]. Photogrowth started as a generative art sys-
tem based on an NPR algorithm inspired by ants’ foraging behaviour,
where the trails of artiﬁcial ants were used to produce a rendering
of an input image. The large number of parameters controlling the
behaviour of the ants, and the dependencies among parameters, made
it difﬁcult to set all the parameters by hand and ﬁnd valuable combi-
nations. Furthermore, the parameters were hard-coded and, therefore,
changes to these parameters involved changing the code. Thus, it
became clear that the creative potential of the algorithm was not being
fully explored.
Later, to overcome the aforementioned limitations, Photogrowth
evolved into an IEC system, allowing the users to become breeders
of artiﬁcial ant species. This avoided the need to perform low-level
175

176 painting images with artificial ants
changes in the parameters, which often require the understanding of
the technical details of the underlying algorithm. The experimental
results showed that it was possible to evolve a wide range of imagery.
However, the results also showed that(i) the approach put a signiﬁcant
burden on users; (ii) only the committed and patient users were able
to guide the evolutionary process towards non-trivial combinations of
parameters; (iii) most users tended to value novelty over quality; and
(iv) most users who started the process with a speciﬁc type of image
in mind failed to reach their goal.
To eliminate the need for individual assessment of the ant paintings
by the user, a further step in the automation of the evolutionary process
is needed. We want to enable users to express aesthetic preferences and
goals through the speciﬁcation of features they desire to be pursued or
avoided during evolution. Our aim is to automatically guide evolution
towards results that are consistent with the preferences of the user.
As for the rendering process, we need a method that is able to draw
rich organic lines from the ant trails. This method should also allow
the control of visual properties of the resulting lines, e.g. opacity and
outline, while they overlap.
12.2 related work
Artiﬁcial ants have been employed for image generation purposes by
several researchers. Tzafestas [282] presents a system where artiﬁcial
ants pick-up and deposit food, which is represented with paint, study-
ing the self-regulation properties of the system and complexity of the
resulting images. Ramos and Almeida [ 234] explore the use of ant
systems for pattern recognition purposes. The artiﬁcial ants success-
fully detect the edges of the images, producing stylised renderings
of the originals. The artistic potential of the approach is explored in
later work [233]. Urbano [285] describes a multi-agent system based
on artiﬁcial ants, presenting the ﬁrst artiﬁcial ant paintings produced
using a plausible biological model [ 286]. Greenﬁeld [ 102] develops
a faithful model of the seed foraging behaviour of the ant species
P . barbatus, exploring it for artistic purposes.
Aupetit et al. [ 7] present an interactive GA for the creation of ant
paintings. The algorithm evolves parameters of the rules that control
the behaviour of the ants. The artiﬁcial ants deposit paint on the canvas
as they move, thus producing a painting. In a later study, Monmarché,
Mahnich, and Slimane [ 208] reﬁne this approach, exploring different
rendering modes. Semet, O’Reilly, and Durand [263] apply ant colony
simulation to produce NPRs of input images. For the same purpose,

12.2 related work 177
Fernandes et al. [ 75] use an approach similar to Ramos and Almeida
[234]. Barrass [ 15] models ant behaviour using ANNs, exploring the
artistic potential of the approach. Greenﬁeld [98] presents an evolution-
ary approach to the production of ant paintings and explores the use
of behavioural statistics of the artiﬁcial ants to automatically assign
ﬁtness. Later, Greenﬁeld adopts a multiple pheromone model where
the movement and behaviour of the ants are inﬂuenced, i.e. they are
attracted or repelled, by both an environment-generated pheromone
and an ant-generated pheromone [ 99]. Greenﬁeld and Machado [ 103]
use a combination of behavioural and image statistics to assign ﬁtness
and value in the context of an agent-based simulation of artist and
critic dynamics.
The use of EC to create image ﬁlters and NPRs of input images has
been explored by several researchers. Ross et al. [ 215, 249] use GP,
multi-objective optimisation techniques and an empirical model of
aesthetics to automatically evolve image ﬁlters. Lewis [ 154] uses IEC
to evolve live-video processing ﬁlters. Machado, Dias, and Cardoso
[177] use GP to evolve image colouring ﬁlters from a set of examples.
Yip [303] employs GAs to evolve ﬁlters that produce images that match
certain features of a target image. Collomosse [ 42] uses image salience
metrics to determine the level of detail for portions of the image and
GAs to search for painterly renderings that match the desired salience
maps. Hewgill and Ross [ 121] use GP to evolve procedural textures
for three-dimensional objects. Schlechtweg, Germer, and Strothotte
[257] adopt a non-evolutionary multi-agent approach for stroke-based
rendering. Machado and Graça [178] employ GP to evolve assemblages
of three-dimensional objects as artistic representations of input images.
These works inﬂuenced the development of the work presented in
the next Chapter.

13
P H O T O G R O W T H
This Chapter presents our work in the context of the Photogrowth sys-
tem, in which we allow users to generate NPRs painted by artiﬁcial
ants that are automatically evolved to match different features speci-
ﬁed by the users through the design of ﬁtness functions. We begin by
describing the developed framework. Then, we perform experiments
with users to test the design of ﬁtness functions, perform multiple evo-
lutionary runs, and select evolved ant species. In the end, we overview
the application of evolved ant species to paint images located on a
social network. Figure 13.1 shows an example of an NPR painted by
artiﬁcial ants.
13.1 framework
The system implements a painting algorithm inspired by ants’ be-
haviour to produce NPRs of input images. Each rendering is produced
by simulating the behaviour of artiﬁcial ants while they travel on a
canvas and paint their trails as continuous organic lines. The inter-
twinement of the ant trails, together with their variation in width,
direction and colour, produce detailed and expressive artworks from
an underlying image.
The workﬂow of the framework can be described as follows. Users
begin by setting up the evolutionary runs, establishing parameters
such as the input image, population size, number of generations, num-
ber of runs, crossover and mutation rate. Then, users are responsible
for designing a ﬁtness function to guide evolution. To do this, users
use a custom graphical interface that enables them to interactively
specify the characteristics they desire to be pursued or avoided, as well
as the importance of each characteristic. Control is then passed to the
evolutionary engine. It uses a GA guided by a ﬁtness function designed
by a user to evolve species of artiﬁcial ants. Each ant species is deﬁned
by a set of parameters that determine the behaviour of the ants and
how they react to the input image. This way, different ant species
produce different types of imagery. When the evolutionary runs are
concluded, the results are presented to the users, in a summarised
way, allowing them to select the ant species that better match their
preferences. Finally, further control is given to the users by letting
179

180 photogrowth
Figure 13.1: Rendering of a photograph of a ﬂamenco dancer painted by
artiﬁcial ants.

13.1 framework 181
them apply evolved ant species to any input image and control the
visual properties of the ﬁnal rendering.
13.1.1 Simulation of Ants
The artiﬁcial ants live on a two-dimensional environment initialised
with the input image and paint on a painting canvas, which is initially
empty and is used exclusively for depositing ink (see Figure 13.2). In
this virtual environment, light is energy. This means that the luminance
of an area of the environment determines the available energy at
that location. During the simulation, ants gain energy by travelling
through bright areas and remove energy from the environment. If the
energy of an ant is below a death threshold, it dies. If the energy is
above a reproduction threshold, it generates offspring. The offspring
initiates with a percentage of the energy of the progenitor, which loses
energy, and inherits other properties, including velocity but with a
perturbation added to its angular velocity.
Figure 13.2: Illustration of an artiﬁcial ant creating a painting by removing
energy from the environment (left and centre image) and de-
positing ink on a canvas (right image). Photograph by Annie
Leibovitz / Vogue, 2002.
The movement of the ants is determined by how they react to light.
Each ant has a set of sensory vectors, each with a speciﬁc direction
and length. These sensory organs return the luminance value of the
area where each vector ends. The direction and length of these sensory
vectors, along with the way the ant responds to the sensory informa-
tion, is determined by the species’ parameters. To update the position
of each ant, we perform a weighted sum, calculating the sum of the
sensory vectors divided by their norms, multiplied by the luminance
at their endpoints and by the weight that the ant gives to each sen-
sor. The result of this operation is then multiplied by a scaling factor
that represents the base speed of the ant. To represent inaccuracy of

182 photogrowth
movement and sensory organs, the direction is perturbed by adding
Perlin noise [ 225] to its angle. Each ant has a position, colour, and
energy. The remaining parameters are initialised or set according to
the species’ parameters. Colour is determined at birth, so each ant
assumes the colour of the area of the environment where it is born
and does not change throughout its life. Thus, an ant may carry its
colour to areas of the canvas that have different colours in the original
image.
The system that simulates the artiﬁcial ants is implemented in
Java with the open-source library Processing. It is able to ﬂuidly run
and show thousands of ants simultaneously. This allows us to see
the ant painting process in real-time. Plus, there is an interactive
mode that allows the user to manually add ants to speciﬁc points
of the canvas by clicking with or dragging the mouse. The video
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/photogrowth_sim.mov demonstrates
the simulation of the artiﬁcial ants, travelling and feeding on the
environment and depositing ink on the painting canvas.
13.1.2 Evolution of Ant Species
We use a GA to evolve artiﬁcial ant species. Each individual in the
evolutionary process represents an ant species. Each genotype is a
tuple of ﬂoating point numbers that encode the parameters that deﬁne
the behaviour of each ant species and how it reacts to the environment,
i.e. the input image. Table 13.1 overviews the parameters encoded in
the genotype, including the number of genes used for each parameter.
Regarding variation operators, we use a two-point crossover operator
for recombination purposes and a Gaussian mutation operator. We
employ tournament selection and an elitist strategy, so the individual
with the highest ﬁtness proceeds unchanged to the next generation.
The phenotype of each individual consists of a painting that results
from the simulation of ants of the species encoded in the genotype.
13.1.3 Evaluation of Ant Paintings
The GA is guided by an automated ﬁtness function that speciﬁes
characteristics that the user desires to be pursued or avoided. The
ﬁtness function is designed by the user and is based on different
behavioural and image features. The behavioural features consist of
statistics describing the behaviour of the ants while they paint, and
image features are calculated when the painting is completed.

13.1 framework 183
During the simulation of each ant species, the following behavioural
statistics are collected:
• avg(ants) — Average number of living ants;
• depositedInk — Total amount of ink deposited by the ants;
• coverage — Percentage of the environment visited by the ants;
• avg(distance) — Average Euclidean distance between the position
where the ant was born and the position where it died;
• avg(trail), std(trail) — Average trail length and its standard de-
viation;
• avg(life), std(life) — Average lifespan of the ants and its standard
deviation;
• avg(avg(width)), std(avg(width)) — Average and standard devia-
tion of the average width of each trail;
• avg(std(width)), std(std(width)) — Average and standard devia-
tion of the standard deviation of the width of each trail;
• avg(avg(av)), std(avg(av)), avg(std(av)), std(std(av)) — Analo-
gous to the features regarding trail width, but pertaining to the
angular velocity of the ants.
When the simulation of each ant species ends, the following image
features are calculated from the resulting painting:
• complexity — Complexity of the image painted by the ants, p,
estimated using the following formula:
complexity(p) = rmse
(

similarity(p, o) = 1

The ﬁtness functions assume the form of a weighted sum. For each
feature, the user can indicate a weight that deﬁnes the importance
that will be assigned to it, and the intention to minimise, maximise or
make the feature match a target value. Specifying a target value for a
given feature, i, implies that the ﬁtness component associated with it
is calculated using:

This value is established analytically for some features, e.g. fractalDim

decreases as the fi becomes higher, thus promoting the discovery of
individuals that satisfy all features employed in the designed ﬁtness
function. This is accomplished using:
