13.1.4 Design of Fitness Functions
The ﬁtness functions are designed by users using a custom graphical
interface composed of a grid of responsive icons, one for each feature.
Figure 13.3 shows a screenshot of the interface for ﬁtness function
design. The vertical slider on the right side of each icon speciﬁes
1 http://rsb.info.nih.gov/ij/plugins/fraclac/FLHelp/Introduction.htm

186 photogrowth
the weight associated with the corresponding feature, which can be
adjusted in the [−1, 1] interval. Pressing the leftmost button beneath
each icon indicates minimisation of the corresponding feature, while
pressing the rightmost button indicates maximisation. Specifying a
target value for a feature is accomplished using the plus and minus
buttons. For most users, indicating a speciﬁc value for a feature such
as the complexity of an image could be meaningless. To tackle this
problem, all values are presented and adjusted in the [0, 1] interval
and then mapped to the [ofﬂineMini, ofﬂineMaxi] interval. Thus, for
example, specifying a target value of 0.8 for a given feature and
a positive weight indicates the wish to reach a value close to its
maximum. Indicating a target value of 0.2 indicates the wish to reach
a value close to its minimum. Specifying a negative weight indicates
the wish to deviate from the target value.
Each icon of the interface is designed and implemented to be re-
sponsive, in an attempt to give the user a better understanding of the
meaning of each feature. This way, the design of each icon changes
in accordance with the changes in the value for that feature. The
video http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/photogrowth_ui.mov demon-
strates the responsiveness of all icons. For instance, increasing the
avg(ants) increases the number of points displayed in the correspond-
ing icon; increasing the avg(distance) increases the distance between
the start and endpoints of each trail. Furthermore, since the features
are not independent, changes on a feature value may affect the ap-
pearance of other icons. For instance, increasing the avg(ants) while
leaving the coverage and depositedInk unchanged implies that each ant
visits a smaller area of the canvas and deposits a smaller amount
of ink. The interface reﬂects these dependencies by decreasing the
size and opacity of the circles of the icons corresponding to coverage
and depositedInk.
Looking at Figure 13.3, the ﬁtness function that is being speciﬁed can
be described as follows: maximise coverage and similarity to promote
paintings where the ants visit the entire canvas to closely match the
original image; in what concerns line width, maximise avg(std(width))
and std(avg(width)) to promote high variations of width and heteroge-
neous widths among lines, respectively, and minimise avg(avg(width))
to promote thin lines; line direction is controlled by maximising
avg(avg(av)) while minimising avg(std(av)), which promotes the ap-
pearance of circular motifs that result from high angular velocity and
low variation of angular velocity.

13.1 framework 187
Figure 13.3: Screenshot of the ﬁtness function design interface.
13.1.5 Rendering of Ant Trails
The process that renders the ant trails generates scalable vector graph-
ics, thus contrasting with the pixel-based approaches used in most ant
painting algorithms. This allows us to create resolution-independent
artworks. During the simulation, the ant trails are created by draw-
ing circles of a given colour and transparency as the ants move. The
resulting image is then saved in bitmap format and scalable vector
graphics, in this case as PNG and PDF ﬁles, respectively. However,
drawing the ant trails using circles has some drawbacks: (i) the indi-
vidual circles, along with their gaps and overlaps, become visible at
high zoom levels; (ii) the transparency of the trails becomes difﬁcult

188 photogrowth
Figure 13.4: On the left, the default rendering conﬁguration that is used
during the simulation. On the right, a more elaborated rendering
conﬁguration based on the developed drawing algorithm.
to control as it depends on the density of circles; and (iii) the output
PDF ﬁles become very large, reaching several hundreds of megabytes.
Furthermore, neither using circles nor other existing technique to draw
curved lines allow the construction of vector graphics with the visual
properties we envisioned for the ant trails. Therefore, we came across
the need to implement our own algorithm to draw the ant trails that
compose the ﬁnal rendering.
We developed a drawing algorithm to enable the rendering of the
ant trails as continuous curved lines with varying width and organic
motion. With this algorithm, we are able to produce self-overlapping
lines, with transparency, and specify additional rendering options.
Since the rendering of all ant trails can be computationally intensive,
we implemented the ﬁnal rendering process as an independent step.
Once the evolutionary runs are concluded, users select their favourite
individuals for ﬁnal rendering that is conﬁgured using a custom
interface (see Figure 13.4). This interface allows users to customise
different rendering options: (i) choose to render the ant trails using
circles or using lines created with our algorithm; (ii) control the trail
width, which can be set to the simulation output multiplied by an
input factor, or set to a speciﬁc value; (iii) control the trail opacity,
which has a customisation analogous to the trail width; (iv) control
the trail colour, which can be set to the simulation output or set to
a speciﬁc colour; (v) specify an outline for the trails by indicating its
width, opacity and colour, in a similar way as the options of the trail;

13.1 framework 189
Figure 13.5: Screenshot of a visual experiment created during the develop-
ment of the proposed drawing algorithm.
and (vi) invert the painting by making ants search darkness instead of
luminance. The video http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/photogrowth.
mov shows different rendering conﬁgurations being applied to the
same simulation result.
We now explain the proposed drawing algorithm used to render
the ant trails. This algorithm converts a list of circles into a curved
line with varying width (see Figure 13.5). The input circles deﬁne
the trajectory and width that the output line should take along the
path. We begin by calculating the line segments that connect the centre
points of all consecutive pairs of circles (see Figure 13.6a). Then, for
each pair of consecutive line segments, we calculate the mid angle
and place a line segment with this angle, intersecting the centre point
of the circle, and with length equal to the diameter of the circle. We
also place these line segments in the ﬁrst and last circles but they are
perpendicular to the ﬁrst and last line segment, respectively, plus one
perpendicular line segment with half the length (see Figure 13.6b).
Next, we compute the line segments that connect the endpoints of the
successive pairs of segments calculated in the previous step in order
to create the contours of a basic linear shape that goes through all
input circles and adapt its thickness accordingly (see Figure 13.6c). To
transform these linear contours into a curvy and organic shape, we use
Bézier curves. A Bézier curve is deﬁned by two anchor points and two
control points. The anchor points deﬁne the beginning and the end of
the curve. The control points, in combination with the anchor points,
create two vectors that deﬁne the curve by tangency. In our algorithm,
we use as anchor points the vertices of the previously calculated linear

190 photogrowth
(a)
 (b)
 (c)
(d)
 (e)
 (f)
Figure 13.6: Process to create a curved line from a list of circles.
(a)
 (b)
 (c)
Figure 13.7: Semi-transparent curved line with (a) no cuts, (b) cuts at all
input points, and (c) one cut.
contours. As for the control points, these are calculated based on the
lengths of the edges that join at each anchor point and the angle
between these edges (see Figure 13.6d). This way, the resulting Bézier
curves can adapt to any conﬁguration of input circles (see Figure 13.6e).
The shape of the ﬁnal curve is created by linking all Bézier curves (see
Figure 13.6f).
When drawing the calculated curve with some transparency, self-
overlaps, which are supposed to be darker, are not visible (see Fig-
ure 13.7a). To enable the representation of self-overlaps in the curved
line, we break down the curve into parts and draw each one at a time,
making overlaps visible. One way to break down the curve is cutting
it by all anchor points (see Figure 13.7b). This approach is simple but
creates redundant and unnecessary points. We overcome this issue by
cutting the curve only at the necessary points that enable overlaps to
be visible (see Figure 13.7c).
In Figure 13.8, we illustrate the process to calculate the cutting
points of the curve to enable the representation of self-overlaps with

13.2 experimentation 191
(a)
 (b)
 (c)
(d)
 (e)
 (f)
Figure 13.8: Process to cut a curved line to enable the representation of
self-overlaps with transparency.
transparency. We begin by stepping through the curve parts that
connect the input circles (see Figures 13.8a, 13.8b and 13.8c). When
the current part intersects another one (see Figure 13.8d), we cut the
curve at the beginning of the current part, i.e. immediately before the
overlap (see Figure 13.8e). To detect the intersection between curve
parts, we verify if the Bézier curves of their contours intersect. Then,
we continue to step through the curve parts from the last cut point.
This process is repeated until the end of the curve is reached while
ignoring overlaps with parts already separated (see Figure 13.8f).
13.2 experimentation
The design of our interfaces was guided by user feedback. The appear-
ance of the icons of the ﬁtness function design interface was validated
individually. For each icon, we performed evolutionary runs while
maximising the value of the associated feature, minimising it, and
setting as target an intermediate value. We confronted the users with
the visual outcomes of these runs and adjusted or re-designed the
icons accordingly.
We gave access to the tool to eleven users. After a short explanation
of the tool and its workﬂow, the users designed several ﬁtness func-
tions, conducted multiple evolutionary runs and curated the outcomes.
By analysing the ﬁtness functions created by the users, it becomes
obvious that negative weights and speciﬁc target values were rarely
used. When questioning the users, we realised that when they use

192 photogrowth
Figure 13.9: Examples of phenotypes, or ant paintings, resulting from each
of the 15 ﬁtness functions.

13.2 experimentation 193
these options their motivation was to “see what it does” rather than
achieve a speciﬁc outcome. The same applies to the use of fractalDim
and lacunarity features.
Overall, most users were able to express goals using the ﬁtness
function design interface and the evolutionary system was able to
ﬁnd a wide variety of outcomes that match the goals speciﬁed in
the designed ﬁtness functions. Different evolutionary runs tend to
converge to different images, opening the range of possibilities that
are consistent with the preferences expressed by the users. Moreover,
while browsing the outcomes of evolutionary runs, users often found
images that they considered appealing due to their novelty or aesthetic
properties, even if they do not maximise the ﬁtness functions they
speciﬁed.
To convey the type of results that can be obtained by a committed
user, we focus on the results obtained by one of the users: a graphic
designer who was not familiar either with the inner workings of the
system or with the interface. After a short explanation of the workﬂow,
we asked him to create ﬁve different ﬁtness functions and conducted
ten evolutionary runs for each one of these functions. Population
size was set to 25 and the number of generations to 50. The other
Figure 13.10: The same genotype, or ant species, applied to different images.

194 photogrowth
Figure 13.11: The same ant painting rendered with different ﬁnal rendering
options.
GA parameters are similar to the ones used by Machado and Amaro
[171]. The input image was selected by the user, a photo of Angelina
Jolie taken by Annie Leibovitz. Once these runs were ﬁnalised, the
user reviewed the results, selected his favourite individuals, and was
asked to design an additional set of ﬁve ﬁtness functions. This process
was repeated iteratively resulting in a total of 15 ﬁtness functions
and 150 evolutionary runs. Figure 13.9 summarises the results of
these experiments by showing one individual per ﬁtness function. The
results highlight the diversity of the results and how ﬁtness function
design impacts the outcomes of the evolutionary runs. It is important
to notice that although we are only presenting one image per ﬁtness
function, the system creates hundreds of images that correspond to
local or global optima for each one of the ﬁtness functions.

13.3 application through instagram artist 195
One of the key aspects of our approach is the possibility of applying
a given ant species to different images. Each ant species tends to
be robust and thus able to survive in these different environments,
producing ant paintings that are characteristic of the species. Thus,
over time, the users compile ant species that match their preferences
and intentions, which then can be applied to create NPRs of several
images. In Figure 13.10, we show the results of applying the ant species
corresponding to the rightmost image of the bottom row of Figure 13.9
to different input images. These results corroborate the idea that
although the ant species are sensitive to the environment, i.e. to the
input image, the characteristics of the painting, e.g. the curviness of
the produced lines, are inherent to the ant species. Therefore, applying
the same ant species to different input images tends to result in ant
paintings with similar aesthetic qualities.
The ﬁnal rendering interface gives an additional degree of control
to the users, allowing them to ﬁne-tune rendering options and explore
alternative rendering modes. Figure 13.11 illustrates how different
combinations of parameters affect the visual outcome.
13.3 application through instagram artist
The possibility of applying ant species as ﬁlters to different images,
combined with our desire to allow people to create “antworks” [ 105],
led us to deploy them online, enabling anyone to get their own NPRs
of their images. The result is the system Insta.ants, which uses evolved
artiﬁcial ants to paint Instagram posts tagged with #instaants. The
idea is to initiate a computational creative system that creates antworks
from images provided by people.
Insta.ants make it possible for everyone with an Instagram account
to have artiﬁcial ants painting for them. The process is simple. First,
the user posts an image on Instagram using the hashtag #instaants
in the caption. Then, the system will ﬁnd this post and paint a unique
antwork from it. When the antwork is ﬁnished, the ants will post
it back on their Instagram proﬁle with a tag to the author of the
original image. The proﬁle of the artiﬁcial painting ants can be found
at https://www.instagram.com/insta.ants/. Instagram users with
private proﬁles must follow the Insta.ants proﬁle before using their
hashtag, so they can follow them back and paint their posts.
The functioning of Insta.ants relies on a series of routines, includ-
ing ﬁnding images on Instagram tagged with #instaants, painting
antworks from these images, and posting the resulting antworks
to their proﬁle. These routines also include doing some network-

196 photogrowth
Figure 13.12: Image painted by the 18 ant species employed by Insta.ants.

13.3 application through instagram artist 197
ing. Insta.ants follow users who, in some way, interacted with them,
e.g. used their hashtag, liked or commented on one of their posts, or
followed them.
We evolved and selected 18 ant species to paint the antworks. When
a new image is found, an ant species is selected at random to paint
it. This way, Insta.ants can produce antworks with different aesthetic
qualities. Figure 13.12 shows the same image painted by the ant species
employed by Insta.ants.
There is also an online repository containing all antworks created
by the ants. This repository can be found at https://cdv.dei.uc.pt/
antworks and contains, for each antwork, a high-resolution image and
a video of the painting process. Although the main goal of the ants is
to paint images, we consider that the videos showing the simulation of
the ants are also enticing. Therefore, one of these videos is occasionally
posted to let people see the ants working. Each antwork painted and
posted on Instagram is identiﬁed by a unique code composed of three
letters. This code appears on the caption of the Instagram post and
intends to help people ﬁnding the image or video of a speciﬁc antwork
in the repository. In addition, higher resolution and vector versions of
any antwork are provided upon request.
Overall, we consider that Insta.ants have accomplished its main goal
of creating and sharing antworks from varied input images provided
by several people. Several people used the hashtag to get their images
painted by the artiﬁcial ants. This way, varied antworks created from
different types of input images have been posted to Instagram. In
Figure 13.13, we present examples of NPRs created by Insta.ants.
Data collected over time show the level of user engagement achieved
(see Table 13.2). Several users, after seeing their images rendered by
the ants, shared the resulting antworks in their Instagram proﬁle or
sent a message asking the image of their antworks to, for example,
print or use as wallpaper. In addition, users showed appreciation

through likes, comments or messages. All this helped Insta.ants to

13.4 summary
We presented an evolutionary approach that empowers the users by
allowing them to express aesthetic goals and intentions through the
design of ﬁtness functions that automatically guide the evolution of
NPRs painted by artiﬁcial ants. We developed a responsive graphical
interface to enable the design of ﬁtness functions through the speciﬁ-
cation of the desired image features and behavioural characteristics of
the painting ants. With this approach, we can overcome some limita-
tions of traditional user-guided evolutionary systems, by freeing users
from the need to perform individual assessments and, consequently,
reducing user-fatigue. Additionally, unlike fully automated systems,
this approach engages the users by making them a decisive part of the
system.

13.4 summary 199
The experimental results indicate that (i) most users are able to
express aesthetic preferences or goals through the design of ﬁtness
functions; and (ii) for each designed ﬁtness function, the evolution-
ary system can ﬁnd varied outcomes that are consistent with the
preferences expressed by the user.
The ability to specify different features of the ant species, output
image, and ﬁnal rendering details empowers the users by allowing
them to generate a wide variety of images. Furthermore, the proposed
system creates a new range of artistic possibilities for the users, as it
can produce images that would be difﬁcult to execute or even conceive
by other means. We consider that it is unfeasible to create the obtained
results by direct manipulation of the parameters or by interactive
evolution. Finally, the algorithm developed to draw the curved lines
enables the creation of vector graphics with great detail and potential
for the production of large-format artworks.
Looking forward, future work may focus on the exploration of
novelty search and clustering techniques to further increase the di-
versity of results consistent with the user preferences and to better
summarise those results, simplifying the curation process. We also
intend to further reﬁne the ﬁtness function design interface and in-
clude additional features, e.g. features related to colour analysis. As
for the Insta.ants, it might be interesting to allow people to choose
the ant species that will paint their images, e.g. through a system of
hashtags that enables the identiﬁcation and selection of the desired
ant species. Finally, in the future, we intend to experiment with the
creation of NPRs of videos (see an example of such application at
http://cdv.dei.uc.pt/tiagofm/videogrowth.mp4).
In conclusion, this work shows how A-Life combined with EC and
automated ﬁtness assignment can be used to generate unique and
detailed NPRs with applications in areas such as image processing,
image enhancement, computer-generated aesthetics and art.

Part V
F I N A L E

14
C O N C L U S I O N S
The research hypothesis of this thesis is that EC and automated ﬁtness
assignment can be used to create valuable autonomous generators
of visual design artefacts. To test this research hypothesis, we identi-
ﬁed and focused on three challenges in three different domains: type
design, face image generation, and NPRs. For these three challenges,
we proposed evolutionary generators to evolve visual design arte-
facts, while using ANNs and hardwired ﬁtness functions to assign
ﬁtness based on the speciﬁc requirements of each challenge and the
preferences of the users.
The resulting systems demonstrate the feasibility of using EC and
automated ﬁtness assignment for the computational creation of de-
signs. The proposed evolutionary generators allowed us to answer
positively to research question A (Can we automate the evolution of
visual design artefacts in different domains?). With these generators,
we successfully automated the evolution of visual design artefacts, in
particular type designs, face images, and non-photorealistic render-
ings. The presented generators enable the quick creation of a wide
variety of artefacts with different compromises between functionality
and aesthetics. This can be advantageous for design exploration, where
evolved artefacts can be used as ﬁnished solutions but also as assets
or components that can be modiﬁed, combined, and used in other
contexts, or simply as starting points for something else, e.g., trigger
new ideas.
We demonstrated how users can be allowed to express goals and
preferences using intuitive and interactive interfaces that enable the
design and customisation of ﬁtness functions to automatically guide
evolution towards designs with the desired characteristics. The pro-
posed interfaces to design ﬁtness functions answer research question B
(How can we allow users to express goals and preferences?). With
these interfaces, we are able to avoid the burdensome process of
user-guided evolution and yet allow users to inﬂuence and indirectly
determine the outcome of the design process.
The experimentation carried out in the context of this thesis allowed
us to answer research question C (Which limitations may arise from
automated ﬁtness assignment?). First, the full automation of ﬁtness
assignment, where the user is completely removed from the process,
may result in the standardisation of the outputs, i.e. lack of diversity
203

204 conclusions
among the evolved designs. We addressed this issue by enabling
the user to design ﬁtness functions. The evolutionary process can
converge to a particular type of designs, resulting in a lack of diversity,
which is another potential problem. We tackled this issue by exploring
novelty search mechanisms. Additionally, the ﬁtness function may fail
to properly assess the quality of some of the evolved designs. As a
result, designs with high ﬁtness values are not always aesthetically
appealing from the user point of view, and visually appealing designs
can have low ﬁtness, although the latter is not so problematic as it
does not affect the evolutionary process. This issue is related to the
shortcomings of current ANNs approaches and to the ability of the
evolutionary process to ﬁnd shortcuts and exploit weaknesses on
automated ﬁtness functions based on ANNs. This outcome highlights
the difference between computer vision and human vision.
An overview of the main parts of this thesis is presented in the
following paragraphs.
In Part i, we reviewed the literature. First, in Chapter 2, we intro-
duced the areas of GD (Section 2.1), EC with focus on GAs (Section 2.2),
and ML (Section 2.3). Then, in Chapter 3, we reviewed the state of the
art on the core topics of this thesis, more precisely on GD (Section 3.1),
evolutionary visual art and design (Section 3.2), and automated ﬁt-
ness assignment in evolutionary visual art and design (Section 3.3).
In addition, throughout the thesis, we overviewed the related work
speciﬁcally on evolutionary type design (Section 4.2), automatic gener-
ation of photorealistic face images (Section 9.2), and computer visual
art inspired by ant colony approaches (Section 12.2). This study al-
lowed us to identify opportunities to be explored and thus inspire the
experimental research presented in the succeeding Parts of this thesis.
In Part ii, we investigated the automated evolution of glyph de-
signs, exploring different approaches to design and evaluate glyphs.
To create glyphs, we began by evolving them by combining line seg-
ments (Chapter 5) and then shapes (Chapter 6). Later, we created
glyphs by evolving stencils made of line segments that allow the
design of several glyphs (Chapters 7 and 8). To evaluate the glyphs,
we developed automated ﬁtness functions based on ANNs and other
techniques that allowed us to automatically evaluate features such as
legibility, recognisability, and other visual characteristics. In Chapter 8,
we developed an approach that allows the users to control, through a
custom graphical interface, the features that build the ﬁtness function.
This approach can generate a wide variety of designs that match the
aesthetic preferences speciﬁed by the users. This Chapter partially
answers research question B. Overall, the proposed evolutionary ap-

conclusions 205
proaches can quickly generate a wide variety of glyph designs with
different compromises between legibility, visual coherence and ex-
pressiveness. Therefore, they can enhance the computational support
in the type design process by, for example, automatically searching
large spaces of potential designs and creating new possibilities for the
design of typographic forms. The experimental results, including the
application of evolved glyph designs in the creation of typographic
portraits, partially answer research question A. The experimentation
of the developed approaches also allowed us to identify possible lim-
itations, including the convergence of the evolutionary process to a
particular type of designs, the ability of the evolutionary process to
ﬁnd shortcomings of automated ﬁtness assignment approaches based
on ANNs, and the difference between the computed ﬁtness of evolved
designs and their aesthetic appeal from the human point of view.
These observations shed some light to research question C.
In Part ii, we evolved human face images. We explored two ap-
proaches to automatically generate new photorealistic face images
through the recombination of facial parts of existing faces. In both
approaches, we employed EC combined with automated ﬁtness assign-
ment based on a face detector to ﬁnd new faces that are not detected
by the same detector. In the ﬁrst approach, presented in Chapter 10,
we evolved images of frontal faces from a dataset of face images with
the facial parts annotated manually. Then, in the second approach,
presented in Chapter 11, we evolved faces under unconstrained condi-
tions, i.e. different poses, expressions and illuminations, from a dataset
of diverse face images with their landmarks detected automatically.
The overall experimental results showed that the proposed approaches
can generate new face images that fool the face detector, i.e. images
containing faces that are not detected, thus exploiting the limitations
of the detector. Furthermore, in Chapter 11, we demonstrated that
faces generated with the approach can be used to enrich the training
dataset and, potentially, improve the performance of the detector. More
importantly to this thesis, the ability to fabricate photorealistic face
images opens up possibilities of many interesting design applications,
as exempliﬁed with the creation of the interactive media art installa-
tion Portraits of No One, in which we applied the developed approach
to fabricate artiﬁcial portraits using the facial parts captured from the
people who interact with it. These outcomes also contribute to an-
swer positively to research question A. When recombining faces with
unconstrained poses and expressions, in Chapter 11, we encounter
visual imperfections on some of the generated faces, e.g. blurred facial
parts, brightness and contrast differences between the facial parts, and

206 conclusions
visible seams of some parts. These blends can get high ﬁtness values,
since they are not detected by the guiding detector, and consequently
guide the evolutionary process towards face images not so photoreal-
istic. This issue highlights some of the limitations of automated ﬁtness
assignment, thus contributing to answer research question C.
In Part iv, we investigated the automated evolution of artiﬁcial ants
that paint NPRs of input images consistent with the aesthetic goals
and preferences expressed by the users. We used as a starting point
the work of Machado et al. [ 171, 179] and developed, in Chapter 13,
an approach to the autonomous evolution of artiﬁcial painting ant
species with characteristics consistent with the users’ preferences.
When simulated, these ants produce emerging paintings that result
from them travelling across an input image, feeding on it, and leav-
ing their trails on a canvas. The trails are continuous organic lines
drawn using a custom rendering algorithm that was developed for
this purpose. We developed an evolutionary approach that allows the
users to express aesthetic preferences through the design of ﬁtness
functions, thus enabling the automatic evolution of ant species. To
allow the users to express their preferences, we developed a respon-
sive graphical interface that enables the interactive design of ﬁtness
functions by specifying different features of the ant species and of the
output image. The experimental results indicate that this approach
can generate NPRs with varied aesthetic characteristics that satisfy the
users’ preferences. Therefore, this approach further demonstrates how
giving to the users the possibility of deﬁning ﬁtness functions and
intuitive interactive tools for ﬁtness function design avoids the need
for user-guided evolution while allowing the users to express goals
and preferences, thus addressing research question B. Overall, this
approach enables the users to explore a new range of artistic possibil-
ities, as it can evolve visually appealing and complex artworks that
would be difﬁcult to attain by other means. Finally, we explored the
application of evolved artiﬁcial ant species to allow anyone online to
create unique NPRs of their own images. The results obtained from
both the aforementioned experimentation and application allow us to
answer positively to research question A.
In what concerns the dissemination of our research, most of the
contributions have been presented at international conferences and
published in international journals. The resulting publications are
listed next, sorted according to the experimental Part of the thesis. The
work presented in Part ii of this thesis, about the evolution of glyph
designs, resulted in the following publications:

conclusions 209
The publication [ 183] received the Best Paper Award at EvoMusArt
2014 conference. The publication [ 185] allowed Photogrowth to be fea-
tured as cover artwork of that Leonardo journal issue 3, published
by MIT Press. In addition, it was chosen as the cover image for the
2017 MIT Press Journal catalog 4. Photogrowth has also been featured
as cover artwork of an issue of Joelho – Journal of Architectural Cul-
ture [207], published by e|d|arq, and mentioned in an article titled
“Art, Creativity, and Computer Science” in the magazine of computing
and digital making Hello World [ 53]5. Photogrowth has been exhib-
ited in different places, including the 5th Conference on Computation,
Communication, Aesthetics & X (xCoAx 2017), in Lisbon, Portugal, in
July 2017; the event Romaria Cultural 2017, in Gouveia, Portugal, from
July to August 2017; the exhibition Growing Forms: Natural Born Mathe-
matics, in Coimbra, Portugal, in June 2018; and the commemoration
of the 50th anniversary of the Computing Center of the Complutense
University of Madrid, in March 2019. Finally, the development of
Photogrowth, like Evotype, led the European Innovation Radar platform
to identify the University of Coimbra as a «key innovator» in the
category Exploration6, in the context of the FP7 project ConCreTe.
As for future work, we aim to extend this research to other visual
domains and, this way, investigate howAI can address other challenges,
expand the tools for visual creation, and evolve novel artefacts. We
intend to study new approaches to enable the user to be involved
in the evolutionary design generators and customise their outputs.
Plus, we aim to explore methods to make these generators open to
external factors such as trends or cultural criteria. We also intend to
investigate the seamless integration of evolutionary processes into the
design process of professional users, e.g. through the development of
plugins for their usual software tools or the creation of code libraries
that can be used to develop custom tools. Ultimately, we will continue
exploring the intersection of computer science, design, and art.
